Hochschild (Co)-homology of D-cap modules on rigid spaces I
Fernando Peña Vázquez

Abstract
. We introduce a formalism of Hochschild (co)-homology for D-cap modules on a smooth rigid analytic space X, based on the homological tools of Ind-Banach D-cap modules. We introduce several categories of D-cap bimodules for which this theory is well-behaved. Among these, the most important example is the category of diagonal C-complexes. We give an explicit calculation of the Hochschild complex for diagonal C-complexes, and show that the Hochschild complex of D-cap is canonically isomorphic to the de Rham complex of X. In particular, we obtain a Hodge-de Rham spectral sequence converging to the Hochschild cohomology groups of D-cap. We obtain explicit formulas relating the Hochschild cohomology and homology of a given diagonal C-complex.



1.
Introduction

Let
K
be a complete non-archimedean extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic space over
K
. We let
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
be its sheaf of completed
p
-adic differential operators, as defined by Ardakov-Wadsley in
[
3
]
. This is the first in a series of papers
[
32
]
,
[
33
]
,
[
34
]
,
[
35
]
, in which we convey a systematic study of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
and its relevant categories of modules (
i.e.
co-admissible,
\mathcal{C}
-complexes
etc
) employing techniques from non-commutative geometry and deformation theory.

In particular, the main goal of this paper is establishing the foundations of an
analytic
theory of Hochschild (co)-homology for sheaves of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules.

In recent years, there has been a great push towards developing a theory of
quasi-coherent
modules adapted to analytic geometry. In particular, the aim is obtaining categories of modules which incorporate the analytical side of the picture, while allowing us to work in a completely algebraic setting. Namely,
there is the theory of condensed mathematics and analytic spaces of Clausen-Scholze
[
15
]
, and the theory of Ben-Bassat, Kremnizer
et al.
, based upon sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces
[
7
]
. This is the one that this paper is closer to. The new influx of techniques and ideas that these theories have brought in provides us with sufficient homological algebra to adapt some of the classical results of Hochschild (co)-homology to the rigid analytic setting. In particular, our goal is setting up a cohomology theory for sheaves of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules which retains the deformation-theoretic aspects of the classical Hochschild (co)-homology, but also incorporates the
p
-adic analytic aspects of the objects we are working with. Thus, we obtain a technical framework in which the algebraic theory of deformations and the analytic theory of perturbations can be studied via the same formalism.

Before moving on to the specific contents of the paper, let us give an introduction to some of these notions:
The use of homological techniques in the study of associative algebras can be dated back to G. Hochschild in the 1940’s. In
[
21
]
, Hochschild gives the first definition of cohomology of an associative algebra. He does so in terms of the
Hochschild cohomology complex
. Let
K
be a field of characteristic zero,
A
be a
K
-algebra, and
A^{e}=A\otimes_{K}A^{\operatorname{op}}
be the enveloping algebra of
A
. The bar complex is a
canonical resolution of
A
as an
A^{e}
-module. Namely, it is the following complex of
A^{e}
-modules:

B(A)^{\bullet}:=\left(\cdots\rightarrow A\otimes_{K}A\otimes_{K}A\xrightarrow{d_{2}}A\otimes_{K}A\xrightarrow{d_{1}}A\rightarrow 0\right),

where the boundary maps are given by the formula:

d_{n}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n+1})=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i}a_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n+1}.

The Hochschild cohomology complex is obtained by applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}(-,A)
to the bar resolution
B(A)^{\bullet}
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(A):=\left(0\rightarrow A\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(A,A)\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(A^{\otimes n},A)\rightarrow\cdots\right).

The cohomology groups of this complex are called the Hochschild cohomology groups of
A
. The explicit description of
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(A)
allowed Hochschild to give tangible interpretations of the Hochschild cohomology groups in low degrees. In particular,
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(A)=Z(A)
, and
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(A)
is isomorphic to the
K
-vector space of outer derivations of
A
. Even better, M. Gerstenhaber showed in
[
18
]
that Hochschild cohomology is closely related to the deformation theory of associative algebras:

Definition
.

We define the following concepts:

(i)

An infinitesimal deformation of
A
is a
K[t]/t^{2}
-algebra structure on:

A^{\prime}:=A\otimes_{K}K[t]/t^{2},

which is isomorphic to
A
after reducing modulo
t
.

(ii)

A formal deformation of
A
is a
K[[t]]
-algebra structure on:

\widehat{A}:=\varprojlim_{n}A\otimes_{K}K[t]/t^{n},

which is isomorphic to
A
after reducing modulo
t
.

As shown by Gerstenhaber,
\operatorname{HH}^{2}(A)
parametrizes the isomorphism classes of infinitesimal deformations of
A
. Furthermore, the obstructions to lifting an infinitesimal deformation of
A
to a formal deformation are classified by
\operatorname{HH}^{3}(A)
.

Similarly, the
Hochschild homology complex
of
A
, which we will write
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(A)
, can be obtained from the bar resolution by regarding
A
as a right
A^{e}
-module, and applying
A\otimes_{A^{e}}-
to
B(A)^{\bullet}
. Again,
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(A)
can be described explicitly, and there are concrete interpretations for the Hochschild homology groups in low degrees. More generally, we can consider Hochschild (co)-homology complexes with coefficients in any
A^{e}
-module
M
, which we write
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(A,M)
, and
\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(A,M)
. These are obtained from the bar resolution of
A
by applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}(-,M)
, and
M\otimes_{A^{e}}-
respectively. For example, given an
A
-module
M
, we obtain a canonical structure of
A^{e}
-module on
\operatorname{End}_{K}(M)
. The Hochschild cohomology groups
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\operatorname{End}_{K}(M))
are specially interesting, as they govern the deformation theory of
M
. Let us introduce the notion of a deformation of a module:

Definition
.

Let
A
be an associative
K
-algebra, and

R
be a commutative augmented
K
-algebra, with augmentation ideal
R^{+}
.
A deformation of an
A
-module
M
over
R
is an
A
-module structure on
M_{R}:=M\otimes_{K}R
, commuting with the
R
-action, and such that
M_{R}/R^{+}\cong M

as
A
-modules.

For example, if
R=K[t]/t^{2}
, we call the
R
-deformations of
M
the infinitesimal deformations of
M
, and if
R=K[t]/t^{3}
, we call the
R
-deformations of
M
the second order deformations of
M
. Again, using the structure of the Hochschild cohomology complex, it can be shown that the isomorphism classes of infinitesimal deformations are classified by
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\operatorname{End}_{K}(M))
, and the obstructions to extending a deformation of
M
to a second order deformation lie in
\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\operatorname{End}_{K}(M))
.

Another interesting feature of Hochschild cohomology is that it generalizes many cohomology theories associated to relevant objects in representation theory. For instance, let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite-dimensional
K
-Lie algebra, and
M
be a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule. Then there is a canonical isomorphism:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(U(\mathfrak{g}),M)=\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},M_{\operatorname{ad}}),

where
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},-)
is the Lie algebra cohomology, and the
\mathfrak{g}
-representation
M_{\operatorname{ad}}
is defined by the formula:

\operatorname{ad}(x)m=xm-mx,\textnormal{ for }x\in\mathfrak{g},\textnormal{ and }m\in M.

Let
V
be a
K
-linear
\mathfrak{g}
-representation. As above, we obtain a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule structure on
\operatorname{End}_{K}(V)
, and the previous formula allows us to use Hochschild cohomology to relate Lie algebra cohomology and deformation theory of
\mathfrak{g}
-representations. Furthermore, if
\mathfrak{g}
is a finite-dimensional reductive
K
-Lie algebra, then we have the following formula:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(U(\mathfrak{g}))=Z(U(\mathfrak{g}))\otimes_{K}\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},K),

which showcases that Hochschild cohomology and Lie algebra cohomology are intimately related. Finally, let us now give a more geometrically flavored example: Let
G
be a linear algebraic group over
K
,
\mathcal{O}(G)
be the commutative Hopf algebra of regular functions on
G
, and
V
be a finite-dimensional
K
-linear representation of
G
. In this situation,
V
has a canonical structure as a co-module over
\mathcal{O}(G)
. Thus, its dual
V^{*}
is a module over
\mathcal{O}(G)^{*}
. As before, this endows
\operatorname{End}_{K}(V)
with an
\mathcal{O}(G)^{*}
-bimodule structure, and we have a canonical isomorphism:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}(G)^{*},\operatorname{End}_{K}(V))=\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}(G,\operatorname{End}_{K}(V)_{\operatorname{ad}}),

where
\operatorname{End}_{K}(V)_{\operatorname{ad}}
is the adjoint representation of
G
on
\operatorname{End}_{K}(V)
, and
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}(G,-)
stands for group cohomology (
i.e.
the derived functor of the invariants). Thus, the Hochschild cohomology of
\mathcal{O}(G)^{*}
determines the deformation theory of the
K
-linear
G
-representations, and this is analogous to the deformation theory of vector bundles over the classifying stack
BG
.

Ever since their inception, Hochschild cohomology and homology have become central tools in modern mathematics, with applications ranging from deformation theory, algebraic topology, algebraic geometry, functional analysis, and representation theory. The naive definitions in terms of explicit complexes have been replaced by categorical methods, which allow Hochschild (co)-homology to be defined in greater generality. For instance, even if the definition using the bar resolution does not generalize to a lot of settings, a quick glance at the definition leads us to deduce the following identities:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(A,M)=R\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}(A,M),\quad\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(A,M)=A\otimes_{A^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}M.

Thus, we may extend the definition of Hochschild (co)-homology to any closed symmetric monoidal abelian category that behaves well with respect to homological algebra. In particular, this approach allows us to consider Hochschild (co)-homology of sheaves of modules on ringed spaces, and of quasi-coherent modules on an algebraic variety. This theory has been thoroughly studied in recent years, and has proven to be a powerful tool in the study of certain deformation problems in algebraic geometry. See, for instance, the work of M. Kontsevich on deformation-quantization of Poisson structures on
C^{\infty}
-manifolds
[
29
]
, as well as A. Yukutieli generalization for algebraic varieties over a field of characteristic zero
[
51
]
, and his work on the continuous Hochschild cochain complex of a scheme
[
50
]
.

Up until now, we have only painted the algebraic part of the picture. That is, the part concerning algebraic deformation theory. Let us now describe its analytic counterpart, in the form of perturbation theory. Assume that
K
is a complete non-archimedean field of characteristic zero, and let
\mathscr{A}
be a unital associative Banach
K
-algebra. We let
-\widehat{\otimes}_{K}-
denote the complete tensor product of Banach
K
-vector spaces, and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{K}(-,-)
denote the
space of continuous
K
-linear maps, which we regard as a Banach space with respect to the operator norm. Before introducing perturbation theory, let us first describe the cohomology theory that parametrizes it. This will turn out to be just an analytic version of the Hochschild (co)-homology described above. As we are now working in a purely analytic framework, we need all of our algebras to be of analytic nature. Hence, the first step is modifying the definition of the enveloping algebra of
\mathscr{A}
:

\mathscr{A}^{e}:=\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}.

As in the algebraic situation, the category of Banach
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-modules is equivalent to the category of Banach
\mathscr{A}
-bimodules. In particular, it follows that
\mathscr{A}
is an
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module. Furthermore, there is an analytic version of the bar resolution:

B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}:=\left(\cdots\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{e}\right),

which is an exact complex of Banach
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-modules satisfying that the augmented complex:

B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathscr{A},

is split exact as a complex of Banach
K
-vector spaces. Applying
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(-,\mathscr{A})
to
B(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
, we obtain the following chain complex:

\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow\mathscr{A}\xrightarrow{\delta_{0}^{\mathscr{A}}}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{K}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{A})\xrightarrow{\delta_{1}^{\mathscr{A}}}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{K}(\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{2}\mathscr{A},\mathscr{A})\xrightarrow{\delta_{2}^{\mathscr{A}}}\cdots\right).

This can be regarded as an analytic version of the Hochschild cohomology complex discussed above. However, the key difference between the algebraic and analytic setups is that
\operatorname{Ban}_{K}
, the category of Banach
K
-vector spaces, is not an abelian category. Hence, it is not possible to define the
cohomology groups of
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
in
\operatorname{Ban}_{K}
directly. For instance, given an injective map of Banach spaces
V\rightarrow W
, the quotient space
W/V
is Banach if and only if the image of
V
in
W
is closed. More generally, the cohomology of a complex of Banach spaces is only well-defined if all the morphisms in the complex are strict, that is, if each morphism is closed.

In order to overcome this difficulty, one could use the fact that
\operatorname{Ban}_{K}
is a quasi-abelian category to compute the cohomology groups of
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
in the left heart
LH(\operatorname{Ban}_{K})
. The category
LH(\operatorname{Ban}_{K})
is an abelian category equipped with a functor
I:\operatorname{Ban}_{K}\rightarrow LH(\operatorname{Ban}_{K})

which is fully faithful, admits a right adjoint, and satisfies that a complex of Banach spaces is strict and exact if and only if its image under
I
is an exact complex. This perspective is better behaved homologically, since it allows us to translate analytic problems into algebraic problems in an abelian category. In fact, our approach to Hochschild (co)-homology is based upon this construction. However, the cohomology groups that we obtain via this procedure are highly inexplicit. Specifically, they are objects in
LH(\operatorname{Ban}_{K})
which need not be Banach spaces. In fact, the do not have an underlying vector space.

Instead of this abstract approach, one can instead define Hochschild cohomology for a Banach algebra in a more naive way. In particular, following the work of J. Taylor in
[
44
]
, the cohomology groups of a
\mathscr{A}
are defined as the following cokernel in the category of semi-normed spaces:

\operatorname{HH}^{n}_{\operatorname{T}}(\mathscr{A}):=\operatorname{Coker}(\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{n-1}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{A}})).

In other words,
\operatorname{HH}^{n}_{\operatorname{T}}(\mathscr{A})
is the
n
-th cohomology group of
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{\bullet}
regarded as a complex of
K
-vector spaces, together with the semi-norm given by the surjection
\operatorname{Ker}(\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{A}})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{n}_{\operatorname{T}}(\mathscr{A})
. Although the homological properties of this definition are lackluster (especially if one wishes to consider more general topological algebras), its main advantage lies in the fact that the differentials
\delta_{n}^{\mathscr{A}}
have an explicit description for all
n\geq 0
. In particular, the cohomology groups
\operatorname{HH}^{n}_{\operatorname{T}}(\mathscr{A})
can be given explicit meaning in low degrees. Namely, one can define outer derivations and infinitesimal deformations for Banach algebras, and it can be shown that they are classified by
\operatorname{HH}^{1}_{\operatorname{T}}(\mathscr{A})
and
\operatorname{HH}^{2}_{\operatorname{T}}(\mathscr{A})
respectively.

Let us mention at this point that the cohomology groups
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{T}}(-)
can be defined for more general locally convex algebras. One just needs to mind some technical subtleties regarding the behavior of the completed tensor product
\widehat{\otimes}_{K}
and the inner homomorphism functor
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{K}(-,-)
. This is the level of generality considered by J. Taylor in
[
44
]
. However, for the purpose of this introduction, it is enough to mention that everything we have discussed thus far carries over verbatim
if
\mathscr{A}
is a Fréchet algebra.

Now that the board has been set, we can start discussing perturbation theory: Let
m:\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
denote the multiplication in
\mathscr{A}
. In order to clarify notation, we let
(\mathscr{A},m)
be
\mathscr{A}
with its Banach algebra structure given by
m
. Notice that
m
can be regarded as an element in the space:

\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{2}=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{K}(\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A},\mathscr{A}),

which is a Banach space under the operator norm. Let
m^{\prime}:\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
be another multiplication map making
\mathscr{A}
a Banach algebra. This is also an element in
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A})^{2}
, so we can consider the operator norm
|m-m^{\prime}|
. The main idea behind perturbation theory is studying which properties of
(\mathscr{A},m)
are stable under small perturbations. That is, for which properties of
(\mathscr{A},m)
there is some
\epsilon>0
such that if
(\mathscr{A},m^{\prime})
is a Banach algebra with
|m-m^{\prime}|<\epsilon
, then
(\mathscr{A},m^{\prime})
also satisfies the aforementioned property. For instance, we have the following:

Definition
.

We say a Banach algebra
(\mathscr{A},m)
is stable if there is some

\epsilon>0
such that every Banach algebra
(\mathscr{A},m^{\prime})
satisfying that the following inequality holds:

|m-m^{\prime}|<\epsilon,

admits an isomorphism of Banach algebras
(\mathscr{A},m)\rightarrow(\mathscr{A},m^{\prime})
.

As it turns out, the perturbation theory of
\mathscr{A}
is also classified by its Hochschild cohomology. For instance, it can be shown that if
\operatorname{HH}^{2}_{\operatorname{T}}(\mathscr{A})=0
and
\operatorname{HH}^{3}_{\operatorname{T}}(\mathscr{A})
is a Hausdorff space, then
\mathscr{A}
is a stable Banach algebra (
cf.

[
20
, I.2.7]
).

Furthermore, this flavor of Hochschild cohomology can also be taken with coefficients in any
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module and, in analogy with the previous discussion, Hochschild cohomology with coefficients can be used to study the perturbation theory of left
\mathscr{A}
-modules. Indeed, let
\mathscr{M}
be a Banach left
\mathscr{A}
-module. Then the action
a:\mathscr{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathscr{M}
determines a unique representation homomorphism:

\varphi_{a}:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{K}(\mathscr{M},\mathscr{M}),

which we may regard as an element in
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{1}:=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{K}(\mathscr{A},\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{K}(\mathscr{M},\mathscr{M}))
. As every left
\mathscr{A}
-module structure on
\mathscr{M}
is determined by a representation homomorphism
\varphi_{b}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})^{1}
, we can again make the following definition:

Definition
.

Let
(\mathscr{M},\varphi_{a})
be an
\mathscr{A}
-module. We say
(\mathscr{M},\varphi_{a})
is stable if there is some
\epsilon>0
such that any other
\mathscr{A}
-module
(\mathscr{M},\varphi_{b})
satisfying the inequality:

|\varphi_{a}-\varphi_{b}|<\epsilon,

admits an
\mathscr{A}
-linear isomorphism
(\mathscr{M},\varphi_{a})\rightarrow(\mathscr{M},\varphi_{b})
.

Again, this phenomenon is captured by the Hochschild cohomology groups. For instance, any Banach
\mathscr{A}
-module
\mathscr{M}
satisfying:

\operatorname{HH}_{T}^{1}(\mathscr{A},\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{K}(\mathscr{M},\mathscr{M}))=\operatorname{HH}_{T}^{2}(\mathscr{A},\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{K}(\mathscr{M},\mathscr{M}))=0,

is a stable Banach
\mathscr{A}
-module (
cf.

[
20
, III.4.8.]
).

Going back to our arithmetic setup, one can now see how this type of phenomenon could have a profound impact on the theory of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules on smooth rigid analytic spaces, and in particular on the theory of
p
-adic differential equations. The reasoning is that, if one can show that a certain differential module defined by a system of
p
-adic differential equations is stable, then it is possible to replace it by a small perturbation which is easier to approach. This idea is at the core of perturbation theory, and it is the guiding principle of what we want to achieve.

Let us now discuss the contents of the paper, and give an overview of the constructions: Let
K
be a complete non-archimedean extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and let
X
be a smooth, separated rigid analytic space over
K
. We let
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
be its sheaf of infinite order differential operators, as defined by Ardakov-Wadsley in
[
3
]
. The goal of this paper is developing a formalism of Hochschild (co)-homology for
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. In order to do this, we will rely on the homological machinery of quasi-abelian categories and sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces developed by A. Bode in
[
9
]
. In order to keep this introduction as short as possible, we will abstain from discussing these concepts here. A straightforward introduction to these topics can be found in Sections
2
and
3
. We make special mention of the tensor product of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces, which we denote by
-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}-
, and of the inner homomorphism functor, which we denote by
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}(-,-)
. These functors endow
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, the category of sheaves on
X
with values on Ind-Banach spaces over
K
, with a closed symmetric monoidal structure, which shares many properties with the tensor product and sheaves of homomorphisms of sheaves of
K
-vector spaces. In particular, it is possible to define sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras, and categories of modules attached to them. Their construction and basic properties can be found in Proposition
3.1.2
and the discussion above. The derived functors of these operations are thoroughly studied in Section
3.3
.

Before we start, let us mention that some of the contents of the paper hold in greater generality than the one stated in this introduction. In particular, many of the constructions below can be adapted for arbitrary Lie algebroids other than the tangent sheaf, and it is the belief of the author that there should be an analog of these results for twisted differential operators. Given the relevance of twisted differential operators in the theory of locally analytic representations of
p
-adic Lie groups (
cf.
the Beilinson-Bernstein correspondence in
[
2
]
), we have decided to work in this more general setting. Again, for the sake of brevity, we will only cover the case of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules in this introduction, but one can check the body of the paper for the concrete statements.

Let us start by clarifying what we mean by Hochschild (co)-homology: We define the sheaf of enveloping algebras of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
as the following sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}:=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}}.

Notice that the category of left Ind-Banach
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
-modules is canonically isomorphic to the category of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-bimodules. In particular,
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is canonically a left and right
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
-module. Choose a chain complex of left
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
-modules
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})
. In analogy with the previous discussion, one could be tempted to define the Hochschild homology and cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}
as the following complexes:

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}^{naive}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}}\mathcal{M},\quad\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}_{naive}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M})=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}).

However, one can easily see that this is not the correct way to go. The first problem we encounter when using this definition is that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
is rather poorly behaved. In particular, the sheafification process involved in taking tensor products of Ind-Banach spaces makes it hard to calculate sections of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
, and its algebraic properties are complicated to elucidate. Our solution is introducing the sheaf of bi-enveloping algebras
\wideparen{E}_{X}
. This is a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras which is much better behaved, and such that there is a canonical triangulated functor from
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})
to
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
.

Let
X^{2}=X\times_{\operatorname{Sp}(K)}X
, and denote the projections by
p_{i}:X^{2}\rightarrow X
, for
i=1,2
. We define the sheaf of bi-enveloping algebras of
X
as the following sheaf of Ind-Banach spaces on the product
X^{2}
:

\wideparen{E}_{X}:=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\boxtimes}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}}=\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}}\left(p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}}\right).

At first, it may seem like the situation is not much better than before. However,
\wideparen{E}_{X}
satisfies surprisingly good properties:

Theorem A
.

The sheaf of bi-enveloping algebras satisfies the following properties:

(i)

There is a unique Ind-Banach algebra structure on
\wideparen{E}_{X}
extending the canonical ones on
\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}
and
p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}}
.

(ii)

The pushforward along the diagonal
\Delta:X\rightarrow X^{2}
induces a functor:

\Delta_{*}^{E}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{E}_{X}),

which is a strongly right exact functor of quasi-abelian categories.

(iii)

If
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
is free, there is an isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras:

\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}\rightarrow\wideparen{E}_{X}.

(iv)

There are mutually inverse equivalences of quasi-abelian categories:

\operatorname{S}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{E}_{X}):\operatorname{S}^{-1},

which we call the side-switching equivalence for bimodules.

If the conditions of
(iii)
hold, then
\operatorname{S}
agrees with the extension of scalars along
\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}
.

Proof.

Item
(i)
is Theorem
5.4.7
,
(ii)
is Proposition
5.4.9
,
(iii)
is Proposition
6.1.6
. The statement at the end of the theorem is shown in Proposition
6.3.1
.
∎

Let us point out that
(ii)
in the preceding theorem requires that
X
is a smooth and separated rigid analytic space. More concretely, the functor can be defined whenever
X
is smooth, but strong right exactness requires that
X
is separated. This functor is called the
extension functor
, and can be roughly described as a pushforward composed with an extension of scalars. It admits a version for left hearts, which we denote by
\Delta_{*}^{I(E)}
, and this version admits a left derived functor:

\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X}),

which we call the
derived extension functor
. However, the identity:

I(\Delta_{*}^{E}(-))=\Delta_{*}^{I(E)}(I(-)),

is generally false, so we do not have
\operatorname{H}^{0}(\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}(-))=I(\Delta_{*}^{E}(-))
in general. This is due to the fact that the definition of both
\Delta_{*}^{E}
and
\Delta_{*}^{I(E)}
involves a tensor product, and tensor products do not behave well under
I
(
cf
. Section
3.3
). However, we will later define a subcategory of
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})
for which this does not happen.

We can now define Hochschild (co)-homology for sheaves of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules:

Definition
.

For any
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})
we define the following objects:

(i)

The inner Hochschild cohomology complex of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
is the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}):=\Delta^{-1}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),

(ii)

The Hochschild cohomology complex of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
is the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})).

(iii)

The inner Hochschild homology complex of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
is the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}):=\Delta^{-1}\left(\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\right),

(iv)

The Hochschild homology complex of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
is the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
:

\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})).

Most of the material in the paper will be centered around giving an explicit description of these complexes and their cohomology groups. As a first step, we compose the derived extension functor
\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}
with the side-switching operator
\operatorname{S}^{-1}
from Theorem
A
, arriving at the
derived immersion functor
:

\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}:=\operatorname{S}^{-1}\circ\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}),

as well as the immersion functor
\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}:=\operatorname{S}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{*}^{E}
. As shown in the body of the paper, we may use the immersion functor to obtain a new expression for the
inner Hochschild cohomology complex
. In particular, for
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})
we have:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M})=\Delta^{-1}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}(\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}\mathcal{M}),

and the analogous statement holds for the
inner Hochschild homology complex
.

Again, it may seem like we are not gaining much with this change. However, as

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
is the sheaf of infinite order differential operators on
X^{2}
, this simplification allows us to apply the machinery of
[
9
]
to our study of Hochschild (co)-homology. In particular, as
\operatorname{S}^{-1}
does not change the supports of the sheaves, it follows that
\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
, and
\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}\mathcal{M}
have cohomology supported on the diagonal. Hence, the strategy is using Kashiwara’s equivalence along the closed immersion
\Delta:X\rightarrow X^{2}
to obtain an explicit calculation of the inner Hochschild (co)-homology complexes. Namely, as showcased in
[
9
]
, a very general category in which Kashiwara’s equivalence works well is the category of
\mathcal{C}
-complexes on
X
(cf. Definition
4.2.1
). Roughly speaking, the category of
\mathcal{C}
complexes of
X
is a
p
-adic analog of the category of bounded complexes with coherent cohomology from the classical theory of
\mathcal{D}
-modules.

Let
\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})
denote the category of
\mathcal{C}
-complexes on
X^{2}
with cohomology supported on
\Delta(X)
. The next step is defining an adequate category of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
-modules which is general enough to contain important examples, and is mapped to
\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})
under the derived immersion functor. This is the category in which we will be able to give complete calculations of the inner Hochschild (co)-homology.

In order to achieve this, we need to be able to understand what conditions on a
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
-module
\mathcal{M}
ensure that
\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}(\mathcal{M})
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
-module. Instead of pursuing this goal directly, we will first take a small detour, and use Theorem

A
to dig a bit deeper into the properties of
\wideparen{E}_{X}
. Namely, the fact that
\wideparen{E}_{X}
and
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
are locally isomorphic suggests that the relevant features of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
should also be present in
\wideparen{E}_{X}
. In particular, there should be well-behaved categories of co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules and
\mathcal{C}
-complexes. The following theorem confirms this intuition:

Theorem B
.

There is a well-defined category of co-admissible
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules, which we denote by
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
. It is an abelian full subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
, and the side-switching equivalence restricts to an equivalence of abelian categories:

\operatorname{S}:\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X}):\operatorname{S}^{-1}.

The analogous statements hold for
\mathcal{C}
-complexes of
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules, and we call the corresponding category
\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{E}_{X})
. Furthermore assume
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
is an affinoid space which admits an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{K}
. Then the following holds:

(i)

\wideparen{E}_{X}(X^{2})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}
.

(ii)

Let
\mathscr{X}:=\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{A})
be an affine formal model of
X
with a finite-free
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
. Then the canonical inverse limit:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}=\varprojlim_{n}\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{T})_{K}^{e},

is a Fréchet-Stein presentation of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}
.

(iii)

There is an equivalence of abelian categories:

\operatorname{Loc}(-):\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}_{X}):\Gamma(X,-).

(iv)

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e})
satisfy that
\mathcal{M}
is co-admissible as a left or right
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-module. Then
\mathcal{M}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}
-module.

Proof.

The first part of the theorem is a condensed version of the contents of Section
6.3
. See Theorem
6.3.6
and Proposition
6.3.9
for details. For the second part, statements
(i)
and
(ii)
are shown in Corollary
6.2.3
. Item
(iii)
is Proposition
6.2.9
, and the last part is a special case of Theorem
7.1.11
.
∎

This theorem allows us to deal with co-admissibility problems directly at the level of the bi-enveloping algebra
\wideparen{E}_{X}
, which is a much more natural
way of dealing with
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-bimodules.

Now that this final piece of machinery has been put into place, we can finally define the categories of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-bimodules we were looking for: These are the categories of co-admissible diagonal
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-bimodules
\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
(cf. Definition
7.3.1
), and more generally diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complexes
\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{Bi}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
(cf. Definition
7.3.7
). Roughly speaking,
\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
is the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})
given by the modules which are co-admissible as both
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules and
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}}
-modules plus an additional compatibility condition. Similarly,
\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{Bi}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
is the full subcategory of
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})
given by the complexes with cohomology in
\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
plus some finiteness condition. Let us give an overview of the most important features of these categories:

Theorem C
.

The category
\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
is abelian and
\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{Bi}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
is triangulated.

(ii)

Every
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
satisfies that there are canonical quasi-isomorphisms:

\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}(\mathcal{M})\cong\Delta_{*}^{I(E)}(\mathcal{M})\cong I(\Delta_{*}^{E}(\mathcal{M})).

In particular, objects in
\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
are
\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}
-acyclic and
\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}
-acyclic.

(iii)

The immersion functor induces an embedding of triangulated categories:

\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}:\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{Bi}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}\rightarrow\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}),

with partial inverse
\Delta^{-1}\circ\operatorname{S}
. This embedding restricts to an exact embedding:
\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}:\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})_{\Delta}
, whose image is a Serre subcategory.

(iv)

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is an object of
\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
, and we have
\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Proof.

Statement
(i)
is shown in Propositions
7.3.4
and
7.4.3
, and claim
(ii)
is Lemma
7.4.4
. Property
(iii)
is a consequence of Proposition
7.4.5
and the previous claim. Finally,
(iv)
is a combination of Propositions
7.5.1
and
7.5.2
.
∎

Hence, the category of diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complexes satisfies the properties we want with respect to the immersion functor, and is general enough to contain
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. This theorem, together with the version of Kashiwara’s equivalence shown in Proposition
8.1.1
allows us to show the following theorem:

Theorem D
.

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid space. For any diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complex

\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{Bi}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\Omega_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\left(\Omega_{X}^{-1}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}[\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}]\right)[-\operatorname{dim}(X)].

In particular, we have the following identification in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}.

Proof.

This is Theorem
8.1.6
.
∎

Thus, the de Rham complex of
X
agrees with the
inner Hochschild complex
of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. As a consequence, we may calculate the Hochschild cohomology complex of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
via the following formula:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}).

A priory, it could seem like this is precisely the complex computing the de Rham cohomology of
X
. However, the situation is not as simple. Indeed, in both cases, the complex we are interested in arises as the hypercohomology of
\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}
. However, in the case of the de Rham cohomology, we regard
\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}
as an object in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Vect}_{K}))
, and in the case of Hochschild cohomology we regard it as an object in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
. Thus, it is clear that both complexes are not the same. However, the similarity between both constructions hints at the fact that both cohomology theories are somehow related.

In order to find this relation, there are some technical difficulties that need to be overcome. First, as
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is a quasi-abelian category, the cohomology groups of
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
are objects of the left heart
LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, which are not necessarily contained in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. Furthermore, even if the cohomology groups of
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
are Ind-Banach spaces, there is still not a direct way of relating them to
K
-vector spaces. In particular, unlike locally convex and bornological spaces, Ind-Banach spaces do not have an underlying
K
-vector space. However, by making use of some homological algebra and
p
-adic functional analysis, it is possible to obtain suitable functors at the level of the derived categories. Namely, we have an exact and fully faithful functor:

J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}c_{K},

defined in Proposition
8.1.7
, and an exact and faithful forgetful functor:

\operatorname{Forget}(-):\mathcal{B}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

Furthermore, these functors can be extended to the associated categories of sheaves, and the extensions are still exact (
cf.
Lemma
8.1.10
). These functors can be used to prove Corollary
8.1.12
, where we show the following formula:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)=\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})).

Along the way, we will develop the formalism of Hochschild homology, which is formally very similar to that of Hochschild cohomology. Our final result in this direction is Theorem
8.2.2
, where we give an explicit formula for the inner Hochschild homology complex of any diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complex on
X
. Specializing to the case of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
, we arrive at the following formula:

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})[2\operatorname{dim}(X)],

which yields a
p
-adic version of the classical duality between Hochschild cohomology and homology of the algebra of differential operators on a smooth complex algebraic variety. Furthermore, our calculation of the inner Hochschild cohomology complex of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
yields the following formula:

\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}^{2\operatorname{dim}(X)-n}\left(R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X}^{\bullet})\right).

We point out that similar formulas have been obtained in different contexts. In particular, this result was first obtained for affine algebraic varieties over a field of characteristic zero, Stein complex manifolds, and compact
C^{\infty}
-manifolds by Wodzicki in
[
49
, Theorem 2]
. The result for affine algebraic varieties was shown independently by Kassel and Mitschi (see
[
11
, pp. 19]
), and for smooth real manifolds by Brylinski-Getzler in
[
11
, pp. 18]
. As a consequence, we obtain an alternative proof of the well-known fact that
\operatorname{H}^{n}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)=0
for
n>2\operatorname{dim}(X)
.

Future and related work

This is the first part of a series of papers
[
32
]
,
[
33
]
,
[
34
]
,
[
35
]
in which we convey a comprehensive study of the deformation theory of the algebras
G\ltimes\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, where
G
is a finite group acting on a smooth Stein space
X
. In particular, the results of this paper are further extended in
[
34
]
, where we focus on the case where
X
is a smooth Stein space. Our interest in Stein spaces stems from the fact that they behave like affinoid spaces with respect to sheaf cohomology, but are better behaved analytically. In particular, if
X
is a smooth Stein space, then the de Rham complex:

\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}(X):=\left(0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\rightarrow\Omega_{X/K}^{1}(X)\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\Omega_{X/K}^{\operatorname{dim}(X)}\rightarrow 0\right),

is a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces. It can be shown that this implies that
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X),\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
is also a strict complex of nuclear Fréchet spaces, and this property descends to the Hochschild cohomology spaces. In this setting, we can give a more tangible meaning to Hochschild cohomology. In particular, it is shown in

[
34
]
that for every
n\geq 0
the vector space underlying

\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
classifies the
n
-th degree Yoneda extensions of
I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
by
I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
as
I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))^{e}
-modules.

Following this line of thought, we then show that we can use the bar resolution to calculate the Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
in low degrees, and this allows us to compare our notion of Hochschild cohomology with J. Taylor’s Hochschild cohomology for locally convex algebras (
cf
.
[
20
]
,
[
44
]
). The advantage of this approach is that we obtain explicit interpretations of many Hochschild cohomology spaces in terms of algebraic invariants of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
. Furthermore, our results reinforce the heuristic that the spaces
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
should be regarded as
p
-adic analytic versions of the classical Hochschild cohomology groups of associative algebras. For instance, the first Hochschild cohomology space
\operatorname{HH}^{1}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X))
parametrizes the bounded outer derivations of

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, whereas the classical first Hochschild cohomology group parametrizes arbitrary outer derivations. Finally, we use these results to classify the infinitesimal deformations of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
in certain situations.

In
[
32
]
we develop a theory of
p
-adic Cherednik algebras on smooth rigid analytic spaces with a finite group action. These algebras can be regarded as a
p
-adic analytic version of the sheaves of Cherednik algebras constructed by P. Etingof in
[
17
]
. In particular, let
X
be a smooth rigid analytic space with an action of a finite group
G
satisfying some mild technical conditions. The family of
p
-adic Cherednik algebras associated to the action of
G
on
X
is a family of sheaves of complete bornological algebras on the quotient
X/G
which generalizes the skew group algebra
G\ltimes\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. In particular, given a
p
-adic Cherednik algebra
\mathcal{H}_{t,c,\omega}
, its restriction to the smooth locus of
X/G
is canonically isomorphic to
G\ltimes\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. Furthermore, all
p
-adic Cherednik algebras are locally Fréchet-Stein, and their isomorphism classes are parameterized by geometric invariants associated to the action of
G
on
X
.

In the algebraic setting, this parameter space has a deep deformation-theoretic meaning. Namely, if
X
is a smooth affine
K
-variety with an action of a finite group
G
, then it can be shown that the family of Cherednik algebras associated to the action of
G
on
X
parametrizes all the infinitesimal deformations of the skew-group algebra
G\ltimes\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
. We hope that our methods can be extended to show that this phenomena is also present in the rigid analytic setting. The paper
[
32
]
is devoted to laying the foundations of the theory of
p
-adic Cherednik algebras. In particular, we provide a construction of these algebras, study their algebraic properties, and obtain a classification theorem.

The study of
p
-adic Cherednik algebras is continued in
[
33
]
,
where we study the representation theory of
p
-adic Cherednik algebras, focusing on the case where
X
is the analytification of an affine space, and
G
acts linearly on
X
. The algebras arising from such an action are called
p
-adic rational Cherednik algebras, and their representation theory shares many features with that of the
p
-adic Arens-Michael envelope
\wideparen{U}(\mathfrak{g})
of a finite dimensional reductive Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
studied in
[
40
]
. In particular, to any
p
-adic rational Cherednik algebra one can attach a category
\wideparen{\mathcal{O}}
, which is a highest weight category whose simple objects are in bijection with the irreducible representations of
G
.

Finally, in our under construction paper
[
35
]
we aim at employing several techniques from
p
-adic Hodge theory to further our study of the perturbation theory of integrable connections on smooth and separated rigid analytic spaces. Roughly speaking, our strategy here is the following: The homological nature of our theory fits in well with the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence for co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules constructed by F. Wiersig in
[
46
]
,
[
47
]
, and we expect that this correspondence can be used to obtain a well-behaved deformation theory for co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. In particular, working in the pro-étale topology,
[
47
]
defines a pair of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras
\mathbb{B}_{\operatorname{pdR}}^{\dagger}
and
\mathcal{O}\mathbb{B}_{\operatorname{pdR}}^{\dagger}
, which can be regarded as decompletions of the almost de Rham period and structure sheaves respectively. These sheaves can be used to define pro-étale versions of the de Rham and solution functors for
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules, which in particular turn co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules (and more generally
\mathcal{C}
-complexes) into complexes of
\mathbb{B}_{\operatorname{pdR}}^{\dagger}
-modules. In this setting, the deformations we are interested in appear via
studying the deformations of sheaves of Ind-Banach modules over
\mathbb{B}_{\operatorname{pdR}}^{\dagger}
, and then applying the so called reconstruction functor (a composition of the derived pushforward to the analytic topology with certain operator). Heuristically, we want to study the deformations of the systems of differential equations by studying their sheaves of solutions.

Additionally, our line of work is specially synergetic with the works of Cortiñas-Meyer-Mukherjee, in which the authors develop a version of analytic cyclic homology for non-archimedean bornological algebras (
cf.

[
13
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
30
]
). We expect that our understanding of the Hochschild homology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
can be used to calculate the cyclic, periodic cyclic, and analytic cyclic homology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
in many interesting situations. Namely, we aim to obtain complete calculations at least whenever
X
is a
p
-adic Stein space, such as the Drinfeld upper half plane.

To motivate this, we recall that the main reason behind A. Connes’ definition of cyclic (co)-homology was its relation with
K
-theory in the context of differential geometry (
cf.

[
12
]
). In the non-archimedean setting, there has been a push in recent years to obtain versions of (bivariant)
K
-theory for non-archimedean analytic algebras. For instance, there are the works of Kerz-Saito-Tamme
[
26
]
,
[
27
]
, and D. Mukherjee
[
31
]
. Moreover, the non-archimedean bivariant K-theory from
[
31
]
comes equipped with a canonical map to non-archimedean analytic cyclic homology. Thus, we expect that our results pave the way to studying the
K
-theory of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, and allow for the introduction of
K
-theoretic techniques in the study of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules on rigid analytic spaces. Finally, let us mention that, in the archimedean setup, algebras of differential operators cannot be endowed with submultiplicative seminorms. Thus, this type of phenomenon is unique to the non-archimedean theory.

Structure of the paper

We will now give an overview of the contents:

In Chapter
2
we will provide the reader with the basic notions on quasi-abelian categories, focusing on the cases of complete bornological spaces
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, and Ind-Banach spaces
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. We will describe the closed symmetric monoidal structures on
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, and
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, compare them via the dissection functor, and discuss their extension to the left heart. Finally, we define algebras in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, and
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
and study their categories of modules.

In Chapter
3
we will continue with the background material by discussing the category of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces on a rigid analytic space
X
. We deal with the extension of the closed symmetric monoidal structure on
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
to the category
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces on
X
, define pushforward and pullback along morphisms of rigid analytic spaces, and establish a theory of derived functors in this setting. As in the previous section, we finish the chapter by defining sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras, and studying their categories of modules.

We finish the preliminaries in Chapter
4
, where we give an overview of the theory of sheaves of Ind-Banach modules over the sheaf of hypercomplete enveloping algebras of a Lie algebroid. In particular,
we will define the category of sheaves of co-admissible modules, the category of
\mathcal{C}
-complexes, and study certain operations between them.

We will devote Chapter
5
to the construction of the product of two Lie algebroids and the definition of the bi-enveloping algebra. In particular, if
X
is a smooth rigid analytic space equipped with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
, we construct a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}^{2}
, and a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
on
X^{2}=X\times X
which are determined by the identities:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\boxtimes}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\quad\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\boxtimes}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}},

and we finish the chapter by studying their properties.

We continue our study of the sheaf of complete bi-enveloping algebras in Chapter
6
, where we study the category of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces over the sheaf of complete bi-enveloping algebras. In particular, if
\mathscr{L}
is a Lie algebroid on a smooth rigid analytic space
X
, we build a canonical equivalence of categories:

\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2}))\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})),

thus obtaining a side-switching formalism for bimodules. We use this equivalence to obtain a category of sheaves of co-admissible
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
-modules, and
\mathcal{C}
-complexes, and show that the previous equivalence induces equivalences between the corresponding categories.

We conclude our study of the category of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
-modules in Chapter
7
, where we specialize to the case
\mathscr{L}=\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
, and study the properties of the pushforward along the diagonal
\Delta:X\rightarrow X^{2}
. Namely, we identify certain subcategories of
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})
, which satisfy that for every
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e})
the sheaf of
\wideparen{E}_{X}
-modules
\Delta_{*}\mathcal{M}
is co-admissible. In particular, we define the category of co-admissible diagonal bimodules, show that it contains
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
, and then give a derived generalization, which we call the category of diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complexes on
X
.

We finish the paper with Chapter
8
, where we calculate the inner Hochschild (co)-homology complex of every diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complex on
X
. Additionally, we obtain a duality result between Hochschild cohomology and homology of diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complexes, and a comparison result between the Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
and the de Rham cohomology of
X
.

Notation and conventions

For the rest of the text, we let
K
be be a complete non-archimedean extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, denote its valuation ring by
\mathcal{R}
, and choose a pseudo-uniformizer
\pi
. Following the conventions in
[
3
]
, for a
\mathcal{R}
-module
M
, we will often times write
M_{K}
instead of
M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}K
. In order for our quasi-abelian categories of interest to be elementary, we will work within a fixed Grothendieck universe.

2.
Background: Bornological and Ind-Banach spaces

We begin the paper by discussing some of the foundational results needed for the rest of the text. Namely, in this chapter we will deal with the basics of closed symmetric monoidal structures on quasi-abelian categories, focusing on the cases of (complete) bornological and Ind-Banach spaces. We will give the definitions of these two types of spaces, discuss some of their basic properties, and analyze tensor products and inner homomorphism functors in those two categories. In particular, we will construct closed symmetric monoidal structures in the categories of complete bornological spaces, and Ind-Banach spaces, compare these two structures via the dissection functor, study their extensions to the left hearts, and establish several important adjunctions. Lastly, we finish the chapter by obtaining certain derived adjunctions arising from the classical tensor-hom adjunction. The material in this chapter will be systematically used throughout the text. We would like to point out that we claim no originality in this chapter. In particular, in order to keep the preliminaries contained, we have not included proofs of any of our statements. We have, however, included references in which detailed accounts of the facts discussed below can be found.

2.1.
Bornological spaces

In this section we will recall some notions on bornological spaces and quasi-abelian categories. This will be based on the material in
[
9
]
,
[
37
]
, and

[
43
]
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

A bornology on a
K
-vector space
V
is a family
\mathcal{B}
of subsets
of
V
such that:

(i)

If
B_{1}\in\mathcal{B}
and
B_{2}\subset B_{1}
, then
B_{2}\in\mathcal{B}
.

(ii)

\{v\}\in\mathcal{B}
for all
v\in V
.

(iii)

\mathcal{B}
is closed under taking finite unions.

(iv)

If
B\in\mathcal{B}
, then
\lambda\cdot B\in\mathcal{B}
for all
\lambda\in K
.

(v)

Let
B\in\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{R}\cdot B
be the
\mathcal{R}
-module spawned by
B
. Then
\mathcal{R}\cdot B\in\mathcal{B}
.

We call the pair
(V,\mathcal{B})
a bornological space, and call the elements of
\mathcal{B}
bounded subsets. We will refer to the pair
(V,\mathcal{B})
as
V
. A
K
-linear map between bornological spaces
f:V\rightarrow W
is bounded if it maps bounded subsets to bounded subsets. We let
\mathcal{B}c_{K}

be the category of bornological spaces and bounded maps.

Let
V
be a bornological space and
W
be a
K
-vector space. Assume we have a
K
-linear surjection
f:V\rightarrow W
. This map induces a bornology on
W
, with bounded subsets being the images under
f
of the bounded subsets of
V
. We call this the quotient bornology. Dually, for an injective map
W\rightarrow V
, we get a unique bornology on
W
, which is called the subspace bornology. In particular, given a map of bornological spaces
f:V\rightarrow W
,
the spaces
\operatorname{CoIm}(f)
and
\operatorname{Im}(f)
have canonical bornologies, and we will always regard them as bornological spaces with respect to these bornologies.

The category of bornological spaces is closely related to that of locally convex spaces. Indeed, let
LCS_{K}
be the category of locally convex topological
K
-vector spaces with continuous maps (see
[
41
]
for a complete treatment on
LCS_{K}
). Then we have an adjunction:

(-)^{t}:\mathcal{B}c_{K}\leftrightarrows LCS_{K}:(-)^{b}.

A precise description of this adjunction may be found in
[
23
, pp. 93, Proposition 3]
. We remark that the functor
(-)^{b}
is fully faithful when restricted to the category of metrizable spaces, and exact on semi-normed spaces (cf.
[
9
, Lemma 4.3]
,
[
23
, pp. 102, pp. 109]
). A bornological space which is in the image of
(-)^{b}
will be called the bornologification of a locally convex space.

Remark 2.1.2
.

We will say that a bornological space is a normed space if it is the image under
(-)^{b}
of a normed space, and we will follow the same convention for Banach spaces, Fréchet spaces and metrizable spaces.

There are notions of completeness and separability for bornological spaces, akin to those in
LCS_{K}
. We will focus on completeness.
Let
V
be a bornological space. By property
(v)
in Definition
2.1.1
, every bounded subset
B\in\mathcal{B}
is contained in a bounded
\mathcal{R}
-module. Assume
B
is a bounded
\mathcal{R}
-module in
V
, and let
V_{B}=B_{K}
be the associated
K
-vector space. Then
V_{B}
may be regarded as a normed space with unit ball
V_{B}^{\circ}=B
, and topology induced by the
\pi
-adic topology on
B
. In this situation, the canonical map
V_{B}\rightarrow V
is bounded and we obtain the following identity in
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
:

V=\varinjlim V_{B},

where the colimit is taken over all the bounded
\mathcal{R}
-submodules of
V
.

Definition 2.1.3
.

We say a bornological space
V
is complete if every bounded subspace
B
is contained in a bounded
\mathcal{R}
-module
B^{\prime}
such that
V_{B^{\prime}}
is a Banach space. We define
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
as the full subcategory of
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
given by the complete bornological spaces.

In particular, every complete bornological space is a colimit of Banach spaces. Furthermore, in
[
37
]
F. Prosmans and J-P. Schneiders show that
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
can be regarded as a certain full subcategory of the category of Ind-Banach spaces,
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. Namely, that of essentially monomorphic objects in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. We will give a more detailed account of the implications of this fact in the next section.

By
[
37
, Proposition 5.11]
the inclusion
J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}c_{K}
has a left adjoint:

J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{B}c_{K}:\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}}.

The completion functor is discussed at length in
[
37
, Section 5]
. The most relevant fact for us that a short exact sequence in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is strict if and only if its image under
J
is strict. Recall the notion of quasi-abelian categories from
[
43
, Chapter 1]
:

Definition 2.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an additive category with kernels and cokernels. Any morphism
f:C\rightarrow D
induces a canonical morphism:

\operatorname{CoIm}(f)\rightarrow\operatorname{Im}(f).

We say
f:C\rightarrow D
is strict if the above map is an isomorphism. We say
\mathcal{E}
is a quasi-abelian category if the following conditions hold:

(i)

For every cartesian square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

such that
f
is a strict epimorphism,
f^{\prime}
is also a strict epimorphism.

(ii)

For every cocartesian square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
f
is a strict monomorphism,
f^{\prime}
is also a strict monomorphism.

Notice that any abelian category is quasi-abelian. We say a sequence:

C\xrightarrow{f}D\xrightarrow{g}E

is strictly exact if
f
is strict and
\operatorname{Im}(f)=\operatorname{Ker}(g)
. The class of short strictly exact sequences gives
\mathcal{E}
the structure of an exact category in the sense of Quillen
[
38
]
. Hence, we can define the following:

Definition 2.1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a quasi-abelian category. We define the following objects:

(i)

The homotopy category of
\mathcal{E}
, denoted
\operatorname{K}(\mathcal{E})
, is the localization of the category of chain complexes
\operatorname{Ch}(\mathcal{E})
at the full triangulated subcategory of complexes homotopic to
0
.

(ii)

The derived category of
\mathcal{E}
, denoted
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{E})
, is the localization of
\operatorname{K}(\mathcal{E})
at the full triangulated subcategory of strictly exact complexes.

Notice that if
\mathcal{E}
is abelian every morphism is strict, so
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{E})
is the usual derived category. For any quasi-abelian category
\mathcal{E}
, we can form a certain abelian category
LH(\mathcal{E})
called its left heart. It comes equipped with a left exact functor:

I:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{E}),

satisfying certain universal property (cf.
[
43
, Section 1.2.4.]
). The inclusion into the left heart
I:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{E})
has a left adjoint, is fully faithful, and induces an equivalence of triangulated categories
I:\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(LH(\mathcal{E}))
.
The main feature of quasi-abelian categories is the fact that, as
LH(\mathcal{C})
is an abelian category, we can use the previous equivalence to deduce homological properties of
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{E})
via
\operatorname{D}(LH(\mathcal{E}))
. With this equivalence in mind, we will sometimes write
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{C})
instead of
\operatorname{D}(LH(\mathcal{C}))
whenever we dim convenient.

There are versions of the usual notions of exactness for functors between quasi-abelian categories:

Definition 2.1.6
.

Let
F:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
be a functor between quasi-abelian categories.

(i)

We say
F
is left exact if it maps a strictly exact sequence in
\mathcal{E}
:

0\rightarrow C\rightarrow D\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0

to a strictly exact sequence in
\mathcal{F}
:

0\rightarrow F(C)\rightarrow F(D)\rightarrow F(E).

(ii)

We say
F
is strongly left exact if it maps a strictly exact sequence in
\mathcal{E}
:

0\rightarrow C\rightarrow D\rightarrow E

to a strictly exact sequence in
\mathcal{F}
:

0\rightarrow F(C)\rightarrow F(D)\rightarrow F(E).

The corresponding notions of right exactness are defined dually. A functor is said to be (strongly) exact if it is both (strongly) left exact and
(strongly) right exact.

In some situations, functors between quasi-abelian categories induce functors between their left hearts, and there is a theory of derived functors in this setting. This is studied in
[
43
, Section 1.3]
.

Many of the constructions of homological algebra depend on the category
\mathcal{E}
having enough injective or projective objects (
i.e.
existence of derived functors). In particular, when considering sheaves with values on a given category, it is of crucial importance that the category has enough injective objects. Unfortunately, a quasi-abelian category
\mathcal{E}
will almost never have enough injective objects (cf.
[
43
, Proposition 1.3.26]
). However, in many situations
LH(\mathcal{E})
will have enough injective objects. Thus, in the spirit mentioned above, we will be able to study sheaves with values on
\mathcal{E}
by considering sheaves with values on
LH(\mathcal{E})
. Unfortunately, not every quasi-abelian category is suited for this kind of study. In particular, certain operations on sheaves such as sheafification, require strong exactness of filtered colimits. Hence, we need to restrict the scope, and impose certain conditions on
\mathcal{E}
which ensure that filtered colimits are strongly exact. This line of thought leads to the definition of elementary and quasi-elementary quasi-abelian categories, which we now define:

Definition 2.1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a quasi-abelian category. We define the following objects:

(i)

An object
C
of
\mathcal{E}
is called small if the functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(C,-)
commutes with direct sums.

(ii)

An object
C
of
\mathcal{E}
is called tiny if the functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(C,-)
commutes with all filtered colimits.

(iii)

A set
G
of objects in
\mathcal{E}
is called a strictly generating set if for any
D\in\mathcal{E}
, there is a family of objects
\{P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subset G
together with a strict epimorphism:

\bigoplus_{i\in I}P_{i}\rightarrow D.

Definition 2.1.8
.

We say that a quasi-abelian category
\mathcal{E}
is quasi-elementary (
resp.
elementary)
if it is cocomplete and admits a strictly generating set of small (
resp.
tiny) projective objects.

The relevance of this notion is made apparent via the following Propositions:

Proposition 2.1.9

(
[
43
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a quasi-elementary quasi-abelian category. Then
LH(\mathcal{E})
is a Grothendieck abelian category with enough projective objects. In particular, it has enough injective objects, exact filtered colimits, and a generator.

We can relate these notions to bornological spaces via the following proposition:

Proposition 2.1.10
.

The following hold:

(i)

\mathcal{B}c_{K}
is a complete and co-complete quasi-elementary quasi-abelian category.

(ii)

\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is a complete and co-complete quasi-elementary quasi-abelian category .

(iii)

Let
X
be a set and
V
be a Banach space. We define
c_{0}(X,V)
as the
K
-vector space of all functions
f:X\rightarrow V
such that for each
\epsilon>0
the following set is finite:

\{x\in X,\textnormal{ with }|f(x)|>\epsilon\},

We regard
c_{0}(X,V)
as a Banach space with respect to the supremum norm. Every Banach space of the form
c_{0}(X,V)
is a small projective object in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. Furthermore, every object in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is the cokernel of a strict morphism of direct sums of Banach spaces of the above form.

Proof.

The first statement is
[
37
, Proposition 1.8]
, the second is
[
37
, Proposition 5.6]
, and the third is
[
9
, Corollary 4.18]
.
∎

Thus,
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is a Grothendieck abelian category with enough projective objects. Notice that the choice of a Grothendieck universe we made at the beginning of the paper is essential here. Otherwise, there is not a set of small projective generators of
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, only a proper class.

In light of the previous results, one could wonder why we made the effort of defining elementary quasi-abelian categories. After all, we are mainly interested in homological algebra, and being quasi-elementary is enough for the left heart to be a Grothendieck abelian category. Unfortunately, being quasi-elementary is not enough for our purposes. In particular, let
\mathcal{E}
be a quasi-elementary quasi-abelian category. Then direct sums are strongly exact in

\mathcal{E}
. However, filtered inductive limits are not strongly exact in
\mathcal{E}
. Furthermore, the canonical morphism:

I:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{E}),

does not commute with filtered colimits. This makes it impossible to define a sheafification functor for presheaves with values in
\mathcal{E}
, so the theory of
\mathcal{E}
-valued sheaves is not well-behaved. For this reason, we need to work with elementary abelian categories.

2.2.
Ind-Banach spaces

Unfortunately for us,
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is not an elementary quasi-abelian category. Thus, we need to introduce an auxiliary category that is somehow related to
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. This role will be played by the Ind-Banach spaces. Let us start by giving the appropriate definitions:

Definition 2.2.1
.

We define the category
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
of Ind-Banach spaces as the category with objects given by functors
V:I\rightarrow\operatorname{Ban}_{K}
, where
I
is a small filtered category. We call these objects the Ind-Banach spaces. For simplicity, we will write Ind-Banach spaces as formal direct limits:

``\varinjlim"V_{i}.

Morphisms in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
are defined as follows: Given two objects
``\varinjlim"V_{i}
,
``\varinjlim"W_{j}
, we set:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(``\varinjlim"V_{i},``\varinjlim"W_{j})=\varprojlim_{i}\varinjlim_{j}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ban}_{K}}(V_{i},W_{j}).

The category of Ind-Banach spaces is admittedly more inexplicit than
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, and it is harder to apply the traditional methods of
p
-adic functional analysis in this setting. On the other hand, the homological properties of
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
are much better than those of
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
:

Proposition 2.2.2
.

The category
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is an elementary quasi-abelian category. Furthermore, every Banach space of the form
c_{0}(X,V)
is projective in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of
[
43
, Proposition 2.1.17]
.
∎

As mentioned above, every complete bornological space
V
may be expressed as a filtered colimit:

V=\varinjlim V_{B},

where the colimit is taken over all bounded
\mathcal{R}
-submodules of
V
. This allows us to define the dissection functor:

\operatorname{diss}:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}),

defined on objects by taken a complete bornological space
V
to the formal colimit
``\varinjlim"V_{B}
, and similarly for morphisms. This functor was thoroughly studied in
[
37
, Section 5]
. Let us now give an overview of its main features:

Theorem 2.2.3
.

The dissection functor
\operatorname{diss}:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
has a left adjoint functor:

\widehat{L}:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K},\,``\varinjlim"V_{i}\mapsto\varinjlim V_{i}.

These functors satisfy the following identity:
\widehat{L}\circ\operatorname{diss}=\operatorname{Id}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}.
Furthermore, the dissection functor is exact, and induces mutually inverse equivalences of triangulated categories:

\operatorname{diss}:\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})):\mathbb{L}\widehat{L},

These functors restrict to mutually inverse equivalences of abelian categories:

\widetilde{\operatorname{diss}}:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\leftrightarrows LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})):\widetilde{L}.

Proof.

This is shown in
[
37
, Proposition 5.15]
, and
[
37
, Proposition 5.16.]
.
∎

In light of this theorem, it follows that we can regard any complete bornological space as an Ind-Banach space. As shown above,
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is an elementary quasi-abelian category. Hence, it presents a much better behavior than
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
with respect to sheaf theory and homological algebra. Furthermore, as
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
and
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
have isomorphic left hearts, the techniques of
p
-adic functional analysis are still available to us.

2.3.
The Tensor-Hom adjunction

We will now construct closed symmetric monoidal structures on
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
and
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, and discuss their extensions to the left heart. We would like to stress that the constructions also work for
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
. However, in order to keep the preliminaries to a minimum, we will not discuss this case. A full discussion on closed symmetric monoidal structures on quasi-abelian categories may be found in
[
9
, Section 3.3]
, and
[
43
, Section 1.5]
.

We may endow
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
with a closed symmetric monoidal structure via the usual tensor-hom adjunction. Namely, consider a pair of bornological spaces
V,W\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. We may regard the
K
-vector space
V\otimes_{K}W
as a bornological space with respect to the bornology induced by the family of subsets:

\{B_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}B_{2}\textnormal{ }|\textnormal{ }B_{1}\subset V,B_{2}\subset W\textnormal{ are bounded }\mathcal{R}\textnormal{-modules}\}.

This is called the (projective) bornological tensor product of
V
and
W
. We define
V\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W
as the completion of
V\otimes_{K}W
with respect to this bornology.

The
K
-vector space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)
has a natural bornology with bounded subsets:

\{U\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)\textnormal{ }|\textnormal{ for all bounded }B\subset V,\textnormal{ }\cup_{f\in U}f(B)\textnormal{ is bounded in }W\}.

We let
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)
denote
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)
equipped with this bornology. We remark that the space
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)
is complete with respect to this bornology whenever
W
is complete.

Proposition 2.3.1

(
[
9
, Theorem 4.10]
)
.

The functors
\widehat{\otimes}_{K}
, and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}
make
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
into a closed symmetric monoidal category. In particular, for
U,V,W\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, we have an adjunction:

(1)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(U\widehat{\otimes}_{K}V,W)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(U,\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)).

The unit of the closed symmetric monoidal structure of
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is
K
.

Notice that, by construction,
\widehat{\otimes}_{K}
maps Banach spaces to Banach spaces. Furthermore, for every set
X
and every Banach space
V
, the functor
