c_{0}(X,V)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}-
is exact. As we saw in Proposition
2.1.10
, every object in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
admits a strict resolution by direct sums of Banach spaces of this type. In other words,
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
has enough flat projective objects stable under
\widehat{\otimes}_{K}
. This situation was extensively studied in
[
9
, Section 3.3]
. The main consequence is that the functors
\widehat{\otimes}_{K}
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}
extend to a pair of functors
\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}
making
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
a closed symmetric monoidal abelian category with unit
I(K)
. Furthermore, for each
V,W\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
we have the following identities:

I(V)\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(W)=\operatorname{H}^{0}(V\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}W),\quad\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(V),I(W))=\operatorname{H}^{0}(R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(V,W)).

We point out, however, that the fact that filtered colimits are not strongly exact in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, implies that it is not clear if every object in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is flat. In fact, this is probably false. Hence, it is not known if the functor
I:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is strong symmetric monoidal. In particular, the identity:

I(V)\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(W)=I(V\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W),

may not hold in general. In any case, the functor is lax symmetric monoidal (
cf.

[
9
, Lemma 4.19]
), so that we have a natural transformation:

I(-)\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(-)\rightarrow I(-\widehat{\otimes}_{K}-).

We point out that the left adjoint to the inclusion into the left heart:

C:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K},

is strong symmetric monoidal. As before, we can solve the issues of the closed symmetric monoidal structure on
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
by passing onto
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. However, this is done at the cost of working with spaces that are less explicit, and worse behaved from the analytical perspective.

We will now discuss the closed symmetric monoidal structure in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. This is a natural extension of the closed symmetric monoidal structure in the category of
K
-Banach spaces. Namely, let
``\varinjlim"V_{i}
, and
``\varinjlim"W_{j}
be two Ind-Banach spaces. We define the tensor product of Ind-Banach spaces as follows:

``\varinjlim"V_{i}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}``\varinjlim"W_{j}=``\varinjlim"V_{i}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W_{j},

where
V_{i}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W_{j}
denotes the complete projective tensor product
of locally convex spaces (
cf
.
[
41
, Section 17.B]
). Similarly, we define an inner hom functor via the following formula:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(``\varinjlim"V_{i},``\varinjlim"W_{j})=\varprojlim_{i}``\varinjlim_{j}"\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ban}_{K}}(V_{i},W_{j}),

where
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ban}_{K}}(V_{i},W_{j})
represents the Banach space of continuous linear operators with the operator norm (
cf
.
[
41
, Section 6]
). Similar to the case of complete bornological spaces, we have the following:

Proposition 2.3.2

(
[
9
, Theorem 4.10]
)
.

The functors
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
, and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}
make
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
into a closed symmetric monoidal category. For
U,V,W\in\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, we have an adjunction:

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(U\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V,W)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(U,\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V,W)).

We point out that the unit of the closed symmetric monoidal structure of
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is
K
.

The fact that Banach spaces are flat with respect to the complete projective tensor product of locally convex spaces implies that Banach spaces are flat with respect to
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
. Furthermore, every object in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is, by construction, a filtered colimit of Banach spaces. Furthermore, unlike in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
, filtered colimits are strongly exact in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. Thus, every object in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is flat with respect to
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
, and
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
has enough flat projectives stable under
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
. As before, this implies that the closed symmetric monoidal structure on
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
extends uniquely to a closed symmetric monoidal structure on
LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
.

As we saw in Theorem
2.2.3
, the dissection functor induces mutually inverse equivalences of abelian categories:

\widetilde{\operatorname{diss}}:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\leftrightarrows LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})):\widetilde{L}.

The next step is using this equivalence to compare the two closed symmetric monoidal structures:

Proposition 2.3.3
.

The following hold:

(i)

The functor
\operatorname{diss}:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is lax symmetric monoidal.

(ii)

The functor
\widehat{L}:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is strong symmetric monoidal.

(iii)

The pair of adjoint functors:

I:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\leftrightarrows LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})):C,

are strong symmetric monoidal.

(iv)

The pair mutually inverse equivalences of abelian categories:

\widetilde{\operatorname{diss}}:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\leftrightarrows LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})):\widetilde{L},

are strong symmetric monoidal.

Proof.

This is shown in
[
9
, pp. 36]
, and
[
9
, Proposition 4.22]
.
∎

This proposition reinforces the heuristic that we should forgo of explicit objects in seek of better algebraic properties. In other words, the strategy here is showing that everything we do in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
can be translated to
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, and that the corresponding extensions to the left hearts agree. After seeing all this, one could wonder why do we care to define the category of complete bornological spaces at all. After all, many of the developments in this section are aimed at showing that
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
has much better algebraic properties that
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
. Our interest in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
stems from the fact that, as we will see in Chapter
4
, most of the objects we will be interested in are naturally Fréchet spaces satisfying some nuclearity condition. These spaces are subject to powerful
p
-adic analytic theorems (
i.e.
Banach’s open mapping theorem), so ignoring this features is not the correct approach. Furthermore, all Fréchet spaces are metrizable, and for this class of spaces the dissection functor is well-behaved:

Proposition 2.3.4

(
[
9
, Proposition 4.25]
)
.

Let
V,W
be metrizable locally convex spaces. Then there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
:

\operatorname{diss}(V)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{diss}(W)\rightarrow\operatorname{diss}(V\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W).

This proposition will be a powerful tool later, when we discuss categories of modules over complete bornological algebras and Ind-Banach algebras. To summarize, the closed symmetric monoidal structure on
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
given by the functors
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}
extends to a closed symmetric monoidal structure on
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
, given by a pair of functors
\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}
. Furthermore, we have natural isomorphisms of functors:

(3)

I(-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}-)=I(-)\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(-),

(4)

I(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(-,-))=\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(-),I(-)).

2.4.
Categories of modules

With all these tools at hand, we can start working towards suitable categories of modules in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
and
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
:

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category with tensor
\otimes
and unit
U
.

(i)

An object
\mathscr{A}\in\mathcal{E}
is called a monoid if there is a morphism:

\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A},

called the product, and a unit map
U\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
satisfying the usual axioms of an algebra.

(ii)

Let
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathscr{A})
be the category of
\mathscr{A}
-modules. These are objects
M
in
\mathcal{E}
equipped with an action
\mathscr{A}\otimes M\rightarrow M
satisfying the usual axioms of a module.

The monoids in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
are called complete bornological algebras, and the monoids in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
will be called Ind-Banach algebras. Most of the properties of
\mathcal{E}
can be extended to categories of modules in a natural way. In particular, we have the following proposition:

Proposition 2.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a closed symmetric monoidal quasi-abelian category, and
\mathscr{A}
be a monoid in
\mathcal{E}
. The following hold:

(i)

\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathscr{A})
is a quasi-abelian category.

(ii)

The forgetful functor:
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
commutes with limits and colimits. A morphism in
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathscr{A})
is strict if and only if it is strict in
\mathcal{E}
.

(iii)

If
\mathcal{E}
is (co)-complete, then so is
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathscr{A})
.

(iv)

Is
\mathcal{E}
is quasi-elementary (
resp.
elementary) then so is
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathscr{A})
.

Proof.

This is
[
9
, Lemma 3.7]
.
∎

In particular, if
\mathscr{A}
is a complete bornological algebra then
\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A})
is a quasi-elementary quasi-abelian category which is complete and co-complete. Thus,
LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category. Similarly, if
\mathscr{A}
is an Ind-Banach algebra, then
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
is a complete and cocomplete elementary quasi-abelian category. Furthermore, in the Ind-Banach case,
I
commutes with the tensor product. In particular
I(\mathscr{A})
is a monoid in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. In this situation, it follows by
[
9
, Proposition 2.6]
that
I
induces an equivalence of abelian categories:

LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})).

In the complete bornological case,
I
is not strong symmetric monoidal. Thus, the above isomorphism does not hold in general. However, we can once again bridge the gap by passing onto
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. Indeed, let
\mathscr{A}
be a complete bornological algebra. The fact that
\operatorname{diss}:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is lax symmetric monoidal implies that
\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})
has a canonical structure as an Ind-Banach algebra (
cf.

[
9
, Proposition 3.10]
). The two categories of modules are connected via the following proposition:

Proposition 2.4.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a complete bornological algebra. The dissection functor

\operatorname{diss}:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
induces an exact and fully faithful functor:

\operatorname{diss}_{A}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})).

If
\mathscr{A}
is the bornologification of a Fréchet K-algebra, this yields an equivalence of
categories:

LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathscr{A}))\cong LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})))\cong\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})).

Proof.

This is
[
9
,
Proposition 4.3]
.
∎

In all the cases of interest our complete bornological algebra will be a Fréchet (in fact Fréchet-Stein) algebra. Thus, we may use this to work directly in the category of Ind-Banach spaces.

Remark 2.4.4
.

In order to simplify the notation, given a bornologification of a Fréchet
K
-algebra
\mathscr{A}
, we will identify
\mathscr{A}
with its image under the dissection functor, and write
\mathscr{A}
instead of
\operatorname{diss}(\mathscr{A})
. Similarly, a complete bornological
\mathscr{A}
-module is an object in
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
in the image of
\operatorname{diss}_{A}
.

Most of the classical operations of modules over a ring generalize to the setting of quasi-abelian categories with a closed symmetric monoidal structure. In order to be more concise, we will only develop them in the Ind-Banach setting, but these constructions can be applied to a much more broader context. Let
\mathscr{A}
be an Ind-Banach algebra. There is an extension of scalars functor:

-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}-:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\times\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}).

Namely, let
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
, and
V\in\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. We may endow
\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V
with an
\mathscr{A}
-module structure given by:

\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\left(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V\right)=\left(\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{M}\right)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V,

where the last map is induced by the action of
\mathscr{A}
on
\mathcal{M}
. Analogously, we get a functor:

-\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}-:\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))\times LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})),

and these functors commute with
I
.

There is also a co-extension of scalars functor:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(-,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}).

Namely, for
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})
,
V\in\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, we may promote the space
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},V)
to an Ind-Banach
\mathscr{A}
-module via the formula:

(5)

\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},V)\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A},V)\\
\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},V),

where the second map is induced by the canonical inclusion
\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}
, and the first one is the image under the adjunction
(\ref{equation tensor-hom adjunction complete bornological modules})
of the map in:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A},\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},V),\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A},V))),

defined on each Banach space by the rule
a\mapsto\left(f\mapsto f\circ\cdot a\right)
, where
\cdot a
denotes the action of
a
on
\mathcal{M}
.

This construction makes sense in any closed symmetric monoidal category. In particular, we may repeat this construction on
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
, and we get a functor:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(-,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}))^{\operatorname{op}}\times LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})).

As before, the identities in equations (
3
), and (
4
) show that these two functors commute with the embedding into the left heart
I
.

There are versions of the complete tensor product and internal hom for
\mathscr{A}
-modules. Namely, the complete tensor product of
\mathscr{A}
-modules:

-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}-:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})\times\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}),

is defined for
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})
, and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
by the formula:

\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{N}:=\operatorname{Coeq}\left(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N}\right),

where the maps in the coequalizer are given by the maps:

T_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{M},\quad T_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{N},

induced by the action of
\mathscr{A}
on
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
respectively. Similarly, we have a tensor product for
I(\mathscr{A})
-modules, which we denote by
\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}
, and is defined analogously.

Notice that the functor
I:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
does not preserve arbitrary cokernels. In particular, given a complex
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
of objects in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, the complex
I(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
is exact if and only if
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
is strict and exact. Thus, the identity:

I(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{N})=I(\mathcal{M})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}I(\mathcal{N}),

holds if and only if the morphism
T_{\mathcal{M}}-T_{\mathcal{N}}
is strict.

There is also a inner homomorphisms functor:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}).

This is
defined for
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
by the expression:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=\operatorname{Eq}\left(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})\rightrightarrows\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})\right).

The first map in the equalizer is obtained by applying
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(-,\mathcal{N})
to the action:

\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M},

and the second is obtained by applying
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},-)
to the canonical morphism:

\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{N}).

We remark that this construction can be performed in any symmetric monoidal category. In particular, the analogous construction yields the inner homomorphism functor
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{I(\mathscr{A})}
for
I(\mathscr{A})
-modules. As the functor
I:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
has a left adjoint, it commutes with equalizers. Hence, by the identities in equations
(\ref{equation extension of closed symmetric monoidal category structure})
, and
(\ref{equation extension of closed symmetric monoidal category structure 2})
we have:

(6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}I(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))&\\
=\operatorname{Eq}\left(I(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))\rightrightarrows I(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))\right)&\\
=\operatorname{Eq}\left(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathcal{M}),I(\mathcal{N}))\rightrightarrows\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathcal{M}),I(\mathcal{N}))\right)&\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}),I(\mathcal{N}))&.\end{split}

Notice that this is in stark contrast with the situation of the functor
-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}-
:

Proposition 2.4.5
.

The functors
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}
fit into the following adjunction:

(7)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{N},V)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{N},\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M},V)),

where
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}),\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
and
V\in\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
.

Proposition 2.4.6
.

The functors
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}
fit into the following adjunction:

(8)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V,\mathcal{N})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V,\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})),

where
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
and
V\in\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
.

These adjunctions, follow from general properties of closed symmetric monoidal categories. Hence, we get analogous adjunctions between the left heart versions
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
and
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
.

The symmetric monoidal structure on
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
extends in a natural way to the chain complex category
\operatorname{Ch}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, and to the homotopy category
\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. Namely, choose chain complexes
V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{Ch}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. We can construct a double complex
V^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\bullet,\bullet}_{K}W^{\bullet}
by setting:

V^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{n,m}W^{\bullet}=V^{n}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W^{m},

and differentials induced by the differentials of
V^{\bullet}
and
W^{\bullet}
. We then define:

V^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W^{\bullet}=\operatorname{Tot}_{\oplus}\left(V^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\bullet,\bullet}_{K}W^{\bullet}\right).

Similarly, we define a double complex
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{\bullet,\bullet}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V^{-\bullet},W^{\bullet})
by setting:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{n,m}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V^{-\bullet},W^{\bullet})=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V^{-n},W^{m}),

and differentials induced by those in
V^{\bullet}
, and
W^{\bullet}
. We then define:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Tot}_{\pi}\left(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{\bullet,\bullet}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V^{-\bullet},W^{\bullet})\right).

These constructions yield an adjunction:

(9)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))}(U^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet})\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))}(U^{\bullet},\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet})),

and the analogous adjunction holds in
\operatorname{Ch}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. In order to show this, we can use that the functor
I:\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{K}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
is fully faithful, and commutes with taking tensor products and inner homomorphisms. This reduces
(\ref{equation thensor-hom adjunction homotopy category})
to showing the analogous adjunction in
\operatorname{K}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
. As this is the homotopy category of a Grothendieck abelian category, we can use
[
52
, Tag 0A8H]
, and in particular equation
[
52
, Tag 0A5X]
, and Lemma
[
52
, Tag 0A5Y]
.

2.5.
Derived Tensor-Hom adjunction I

We will now deal with the theory of derived functors in this setting. The main references are
[
9
, Section 3.6]
and
[
43
, Section 1.3]
. Recall in particular the notion of explicitly right (left) derivable functor
[
43
, Definition 1.3.6]
.

As stated above, the tensor product
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
is exact. Hence, it has
a left derived functor:

-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}-:\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\times\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}).

By construction of the left heart of a quasi-abelian category, for every
W\in LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
there are
U,V\in\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
such that there is a short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow I(U)\rightarrow I(V)\rightarrow W\rightarrow 0.

Furthermore, every element in the essential image of
I:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is flat with respect to
\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}
. Thus, we also have left derived functor:

-\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}-:\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\times\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})).

The equivalence of categories
I:\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
allows us to get an identification:

I(-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}-)=I(-)\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}I(-).

Thus, we will identify
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
and
\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
, and simply write:

-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}-=-\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}-.

Recall that
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is a Grothendieck abelian category. Thus, the version in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
of the adjunction
(\ref{equation tensor-hom adjunction complete bornological modules})
, together with
[
25
, Theorem 14.4.8]
imply that we have a right derived functor:

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(-,-):\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})).

such that for
U^{\bullet},V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
we have a derived adjunction:

(10)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))}(U^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{K}V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet})\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))}(U^{\bullet},R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet})).

Remark 2.5.1
.

As
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
does not have enough injectives, the hom functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(-,-)
may not be explicitly right derivable. However,if it is, then by
[
43
, Proposition 1.3.16]
we have:

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(-,-)=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(-),I(-)).

Thus, we will follow the conventions in
[
9
]
and write:

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(-,-)=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(-,-),

in an abuse of notation. We will follow similar conventions for the different versions of this functor.

This functor can be computed explicitly. Namely, let
V^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. There is a complex
\widetilde{V}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
with a quasi-isomorphism
I(\widetilde{V})^{\bullet}\rightarrow V^{\bullet}
. Let
W^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. As
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is a Grothendieck abelian category, there is a homotopically injective complex
\widetilde{W}^{\bullet}
such that there is a quasi-isomorphism
I(W)^{\bullet}\rightarrow\widetilde{W}^{\bullet}
. Then by
[
25
, Theorem 14.4.8]
, we have a chain of identities:

(11)

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet})=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\widetilde{V}^{\bullet}),\widetilde{W}^{\bullet})\\
=\operatorname{Tot}_{\pi}\left(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{\bullet,\bullet}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\widetilde{V}^{-\bullet}),\widetilde{W}^{\bullet})\right),

which we will encounter in many computations.

3.
Background: Sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces

In this chapter we will continue introducing the background material needed for the rest of the paper. In particular, this chapter is devoted to giving a quick introduction to the theory of sheaves with values on quasi-abelian categories. Namely, we will start by endowing the category of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces with a quasi-abelian structure, and determining its left heart. We continue by constructing the sheafification functor, and use it to extend the closed symmetric monoidal structure on
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
to the sheaf setting. Later, we will discuss the theory of derived functors of certain important functors (
i.e.
pushforward, inner homomorphisms, tensor products). We finish this chapter by analyzing some situations which will be of use in our study of Hochschild (co)-homology, and in particular in the version of Kashiwara’s equivalence given in Section
8.1
. We point out that most of the contents in this chapter are not original, and that we have only included proofs of the facts that we were not able to find in the literature. In particular, we only claim originality of the contents of Sections
3.4
and
3.5
. As before, we have included references of all the statements not proved in the text.

3.1.
Sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces

Let
X
be a rigid space. We will now show how the previous constructions extend to the category of sheaves on
X
with values on Ind-Banach spaces. This may be performed on general
G
-topological spaces, but we do not need such generality here.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
X
be a rigid space, and denote its underlying
G
-topological space by
X_{\operatorname{An}}
. For any quasi-abelian category
\mathcal{C}
we define the following categories:

(i)

The category of presheaves of
X
with values in
\mathcal{C}
is:

\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C}):=\operatorname{Functors}(X_{\operatorname{An}}^{\operatorname{op}},\mathcal{C}).

(ii)

The category of sheaves on
X
with values in
\mathcal{C}
is the full subcategory of
\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C})
given by presheaves
\mathcal{F}
such that for every admissible open
U\subset X
and every admissible cover
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
of
U
the diagram:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(U)\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i}\cap U_{j}),

is strictly exact in
\mathcal{C}
. We denote this category by
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C})
.

The categories
\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C})
, and
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C})
are quasi-abelian categories in a natural way (see
[
43
, Section 2.2]
). Furthermore, if
\mathcal{C}
is elementary, there is a sheafification functor:

(-)^{a}:\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C}),

which is strongly exact, and left adjoint to the inclusion
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C})
. In particular,
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C})
is a reflexive subcategory of
\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C})
. Thus, every limit in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C})
agrees with its limit in
\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C})
, and every colimit in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C})
is the sheafification of the corresponding colimit in
\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C})
. Furthermore, we have canonical equivalences of abelian categories:

\displaystyle LH(\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C}))=\operatorname{PreShv}(X,LH(\mathcal{C})),

\displaystyle LH(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C}))=\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\mathcal{C})).

The functor
I:\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{PreShv}(X,LH(\mathcal{C}))
, is defined by the rule:

I(\mathcal{F})(U)=I(\mathcal{F}(U)),

for every
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C})
, and every admissible open subspace
U\subset X
. Sheafification and inclusion into the left heart fit into the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{PreShv}(X,LH(\mathcal{C}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\mathcal{C}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

If
\mathcal{C}
is not elementary, then filtered colimits are not strongly exact, and do not commute with
I
. Hence, there are problems when defining a sheafification functor, and the diagram above will not be commutative. For this reasons, we will restrict to the case where
\mathcal{C}
is elementary. We remark that, in general,
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C})

does not have enough projective nor injective objects. Hence, it is not a good category from the homological point of view. However if
\mathcal{C}

has good enough properties (
i.e.
it is elementary, complete and co-complete), then
LH(\mathcal{C})
is a Grothendieck abelian category. Thus,
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
is also a Grothendieck abelian category. Thus, it has enough injective objects.
This is the case, for example, if
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
.

The closed symmetric monoidal category structure of
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
extends naturally to
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. Namely, for
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, we define
\mathcal{F}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{G}
as the sheafification of the tensor product presheaf:

U\mapsto\mathcal{F}(U)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{G}(U).

As shown above,
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
is exact in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. Hence, the tensor product of presheaves is exact. As sheafification is strongly exact, it follows that:

-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}-:\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\times\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})),

is an exact functor. We may repeat this construction in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
to get another version of tensor product:

-\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}-:\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\times\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})).

As
I
commutes with sheafification, the identity in
(\ref{equation extension of closed symmetric monoidal category structure})
shows that:

I(-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}-)=I(-)\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(-).

We will now construct the inner homomorphism functor for sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces. For
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, we define
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
by setting for every admissible open
U\subset X
:

(12)

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})(U)\\
=\operatorname{Eq}\left(\prod_{V\subset U}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F}(V),\mathcal{G}(V))\rightrightarrows\prod_{W\subset V}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F}(V),\mathcal{G}(W))\right).

The maps in the equalizer are the following: For admissible open
subspaces:

W\subset V\subset U,

a homomorphism
f\in\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F}(V),\mathcal{G}(V))
is mapped to the composition:

\mathcal{F}(V)\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{G}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}(W).

Similarly, every
g\in\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{F}(W),\mathcal{G}(W))
is mapped to:

\mathcal{F}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(W)\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{G}(W).

We may define
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(-,-)
analogously, and the fact that
I
commutes with equalizers, together with
(\ref{equation extension of closed symmetric monoidal category structure 2})
show that the following equation holds:

I(\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(-,-))=\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(-),I(-)).

Furthermore, we have the following adjunction:

Proposition 3.1.2
.

These functors endow
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
with the structure of a closed symmetric monoidal categories. In particular, we have an adjunction:

(13)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))}(U\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V,W)\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))}(U,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V,W)),

where
U,V,W\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. Furthermore, the analogous statement holds for
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
. In particular, for
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
, we have :

(14)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))}(\mathcal{U}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))}(\mathcal{U},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})).

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a sheaf of complete Ind-Banach algebras on
X
( i.e. a monoid in

\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
). We denote the category of Ind-Banach
\mathscr{A}
-modules by
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
. As before, this is a quasi-abelian category, and we have:

LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}))=\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})).

The constructions in Section
2.3
make sense in any closed symmetric monoidal category. In particular, we have a tensor product
-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}-
, an inner homomorphisms functor
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)
, and their counterparts in
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
are constructed analogously. Furthermore, the same arguments as above show that we have an identification:

I(\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-))=\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(-),I(-)).

Similarly, the adjunctions of equations
(\ref{equation relative tensor hom adjunction})
,
(\ref{equation tensor relative hom adjunction})
hold in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, and
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
respectively.

As before, for
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})
and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
we have:

I(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{N})=I(\mathcal{M})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}I(\mathcal{N}),

if and only if the canonical map:

\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N},

is a strict epimorphism. In particular, we have an extension-restriction of scalars adjunction:

I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}-:\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})):\operatorname{Forget}(-).

This adjunction satisfies that
\operatorname{Forget}
commutes with (co)-limits, and reflects monomorphisms and epimorphisms. Furthermore, for every
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
, the canonical map:

I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{Forget}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{M},

is an epimorphism. As
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category, this implies that
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
is also Grothendieck. In particular, it has enough injective objects. Following the conventions in
[
9
]
, we introduce the following:

Definition 3.1.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a sheave of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
. We say
\mathscr{A}
is a sheaf of complete bornological algebras if for every admissible open
U\subset X
the Ind-Banach space
\mathscr{A}(U)
is a complete bornological space. We define a sheaf of complete bornological
\mathscr{A}
-modules analogously.

The necessity for this definition stems from the fact that
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is not an elementary quasi-abelian category. Hence, there is no sheafification functor, and therefore no tensor product in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. Thus, it is not clear how to define sheaves of complete bornological algebras directly, and the same problem arises when defining sheaves of complete bornological modules. In any case, by Proposition
2.4.3
, our current definition is enough for our purposes.

3.2.
Derived pushforward and pullback

Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a morphism of rigid spaces. As usual, there is a pushforward-pullback adjunction, which fits into the following diagram:

(15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{-1}_{LH}:\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})):f_{*,LH}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{-1}:\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Shv}(Y,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})):f_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

As usual, the pushforward:
f_{*}:\operatorname{Shv}(Y,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, is defined by sending
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Shv}(Y,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
to the sheaf defined on admissible open subspaces
U\subset X
by the rule:

f_{*}\mathcal{F}(U)=\mathcal{F}(f^{-1}(U)).

The pushforward between the left hearts
f_{*,LH}
is defined analogously.

The pullback functor
f^{-1}:\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(Y,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
is defined by sending a sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
to the sheafification of the presheaf:

f^{-1}_{\operatorname{Pre}}\mathcal{F}(U)=\varinjlim_{f(U)\subset V}\mathcal{F}(V),

where
U\subset Y
, and the
V\subset f(U)
are admissible open subspaces. The left heart version
f^{-1}_{LH}
is constructed via the analogous procedure.

As discussed above, the fact that
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is an elementary quasi-abelian category implies that
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
is also elementary. In particular, filtered colimits are strongly exact in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. As sheafification is also strongly exact, it follows that
f^{-1}

is strongly exact. Therefore, it extends a triangulated functor between the derived categories:

f^{-1}:\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))).

By the same arguments,
f^{-1}_{LH}
is also exact, so we obtain a derived functor:

f^{-1}_{LH}:\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))).

Commutativity of diagram
(\ref{equation diagram pushforward and pullback})
implies that both extensions get identified by
I
.

As
\operatorname{Shv}(Y,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
does not have enough injective objects, it is not clear whether if
f_{*}
is explicitly right derivable or not. However, as
\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category, we have a right derived functor:

Rf_{*,LH}:\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))).

Again, by commutativity of
(\ref{equation diagram pushforward and pullback})
, we may abuse notation and write
Rf_{*}=Rf_{*,LH}
.

3.3.
Derived Tensor-Hom adjunction II

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
. In this section, we will now discuss the existence of derived functors for
-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}-
,
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)
, and their counterparts in the left heart. Setting
X=\operatorname{Sp}(K)
, we obtain the corresponding results for Ind-Banach
K
-algebras. First, notice that for any
V\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, the functor:

V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}-:\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})),

is exact. Indeed, let
0\rightarrow W_{1}\rightarrow W_{2}\rightarrow W_{3}\rightarrow 0
be a strict exact sequence in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. We need to show that the sequence:

0\rightarrow V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W_{1}\rightarrow V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W_{2}\rightarrow V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W_{3}\rightarrow 0,

is still a strict short exact sequence in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
.
By the adjunction
(\ref{equation tensor-hom adjunctions for sheaves})
, it is enough to show that
V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W_{1}\rightarrow V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W_{2}
is a strict injection. Notice that
W_{1}\rightarrow W_{2}
is a strict injection in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
if and only if it is a strict injection in
\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. In particular, for every admissible open
U\subset X
, the map
W_{1}(U)\rightarrow W_{2}(U)
is a strict injection. Hence, by exactness of the tensor product in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, it follows that

V(U)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W_{1}(U)\rightarrow V(U)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W_{2}(U)
is a strict injection. As sheafification is strongly exact, it follows that:

V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W_{1}\rightarrow V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}W_{2},

is a strict injection, as we wanted to show. By exactness of
-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}-
, we obtain a left derived functor:

(16)

-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}-:\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))\times\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))\\
\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))).

As above, we may use the fact that every
W\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
fits in a short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow I(U)\rightarrow I(V)\rightarrow W\rightarrow 0,

with
U,V\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
, together with
(\ref{equation extension of closed symmetric monoidal category structure})
to conclude that the left heart
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
has enough flat objects. This, together with the fact that
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category, and the adjunction
(\ref{equation tensor-hom adjunction left heart})
, allows us to apply
[
25
, Theorem 14.4.8]
to obtain a pair of derived functors:

-\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}-:\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))\times\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))),\\
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(-,-):\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))\\
\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))).

As before, the equivalence of triangulated categories
I:\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
, induces an identification:
I(-\widehat{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}-)=I(-)\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}I(-)
, and we may calculate the derived inner homomorphisms functor
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(-,-)
explicitly using the procedure showcased in
(\ref{equation explicit computation of RHom})
. Repeating the abuse of notation in
2.5.1
, we will write
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(-,-)=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(-,-)
. As a consequence of
[
25
, Theorem 14.4.8]
, these functors fit into the following derived adjunction:

Proposition 3.3.1
.

For
U^{\bullet},V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
we have:

(17)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))}(U^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{K}V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet})\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))}(U^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet})).

The next goal is developing analogous adjunctions for the relative tensor product and inner homomorphism functors.

Let
V\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
,
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})
,
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
. There are canonical isomorphisms of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces:

\left(V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\right)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{N}=V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N},\quad\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\left(\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V\right)=\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V.

In particular,
\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}V
is a flat
\mathscr{A}
-module, and
V\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}
is a flat
\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}
-module. Regard
\mathcal{N}
as an object in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
via the forgetful functor. Then we have a strict epimorphism:

\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{N},

given by the action of
\mathscr{A}
on
\mathcal{N}
. Hence, every object in
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
admits a strict epimorphism from a flat
\mathscr{A}
-module, and the analogous statement holds in
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})
.

As
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
is not an abelian category, this is not enough to show that
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}
has a derived functor. However, we may use these constructions to obtain a derived functor for
\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}
. Namely, we have the following identity:

\mathcal{M}\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}\left(I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(V)\right)=\mathcal{M}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(V).

As
I(V)
is flat for
\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}
, it follows that
I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(V)
is a flat
I(\mathscr{A})
-module. The equivalence of abelian categories:

\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))=LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})),

implies that for every
I(\mathscr{A})
-module
\mathcal{M}
there is an
\mathscr{A}
-module
\mathcal{N}
which admits an epimorphism
I(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
. As
I:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
preserves strict epimorphisms, we get an epimorphism
I(\mathscr{A})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
. In particular,
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
has enough flat objects. A similar argument shows that
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))^{\operatorname{op}})
also has enough flat objects. As both are Grothendieck abelian categories, we obtain the following proposition:

Proposition 3.3.2

(
[
9
, Proposition 3.34]
)
.

The bifunctor
\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}
admits a derived bifunctor:

(18)

-\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}^{\mathbb{L}}-:\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})\times\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))).

Furthermore, the adjunction in
(\ref{equation relative tensor hom adjunction})
lifts to a derived adjunction:

(19)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},V^{\bullet})\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},V^{\bullet})),

where
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
,
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})
, and
V^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
.

Even though the functor
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}
cannot be derived explicitly in the unbounded derived category, it is still possible to construct a derived functor:

(20)

-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathbb{L}}-:\operatorname{D}^{-}(\mathscr{A})\times\operatorname{D}^{-}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}^{-}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))),

satisfying that
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathbb{L}}=\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}^{\mathbb{L}}
. In particular, for
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
,
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})
, we have the following identification of
I(\mathscr{A})
-modules:

\operatorname{H}^{0}(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{N})=I(\mathcal{M})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}I(\mathcal{N}).

Notice that this is in general not isomorphic to
I(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{N})
. Details of this construction can be found in
[
9
, Proposition 3.36]
.

There is a version of the previous adjunction for
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}
. In particular, a second application of
[
25
, Theorem 14.4.8]
to this setting yields the following proposition:

Proposition 3.3.3

(
[
9
, Proposition 3.34]
)
.

The bifunctor
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(-,-)
admits a derived bifunctor:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-):=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(-,-):\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))).

Furthermore, the adjunction in
(\ref{equation tensor relative hom adjunction})
lifts to a derived adjunction:

(21)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{K}\mathcal{V}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))}(\mathcal{V}^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})),

where
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
, and
\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
.

As before,
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)
may be calculated following the procedure in
(\ref{equation explicit computation of RHom})
.

Definition 3.3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. We define the
\underline{\mathcal{E}xt}^{n}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
sheaves as:

\underline{\mathcal{E}xt}^{n}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{H}^{n}(R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})).

This is an object of
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
, and for
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
we have:

\underline{\mathcal{E}xt}^{0}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}),I(\mathcal{N}))=I(\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})),

where the first identity follows by the definition of derived functor, and the second identity is
(\ref{equation extension of inner hom to left heart})
.

Our next goal is giving an explicit description of the inner ext sheaves. For this reason, we recall the following well-known construction:

Definition 3.3.5
.

Let
U\subset X
be an admissible open subset. The extension by zero functor:

i_{U!}:\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})_{|U})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})),

is defined for any
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})_{|U})
by the formula:

i_{U!}\mathcal{M}=\left(i^{P}_{U!}\mathcal{M}\right)^{a},

where
i^{P}_{U!}\mathcal{M}
is the presheaf defined on each admissible open subspace
V\subset X
by:

i^{p}_{U!}\mathcal{M}(V)=\mathcal{M}(V)\textnormal{ if }V\subset U,\textnormal{ and }i^{P}_{U!}\mathcal{M}(V)=0\textnormal{ if }V\not\subset U.

We now show that extension by zero satisfies the usual properties:

Proposition 3.3.6
.

Extension by zero satisfies the following properties:

(i)

i_{U!}:\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})_{|U})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
is exact.

(ii)

i_{U!}
is left adjoint to the restriction functor:

i^{-1}:\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})_{|U}).

(iii)

i^{-1}:\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})_{|U}
preserves homotopically injective objects.

In particular, we have a derived adjunction
i_{U!}:\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}_{|U})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}):i_{U}^{-1}
.

Proof.

The first statement is
[
52
, Tag 03DI]
, the second one is
[
52
, Tag 03DJ]
. Statement
(iii)
follows by the fact that for a homotopically injective complex
\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
and an acyclic complex
C^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}_{|U})
we have:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}(I(\mathscr{A})_{|U})}(C^{\bullet},\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}_{|U})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}(I(\mathscr{A}))}(i_{U!}C^{\bullet},\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})=0,

where the middle map follows by the fact that
i_{U!}
is exact, so it preserves acyclicity.

In order to show the derived adjunction, choose complexes
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}_{|U})
and
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
. As
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category, we have a homotopically injective object
\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}
with a quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}
. But then we have:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})}(i_{U!}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}(I(\mathscr{A}))}(i_{U!}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}(I(\mathscr{A}_{|U}))}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{I}_{|U}^{\bullet})\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}_{|U})}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{I}_{|U}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}_{|U})}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}_{|U}^{\bullet}),

where the last two identities follow because
i^{-1}_{U}
is exact and preserves homotopically injective objects.
∎

The next goal is relating extension by zero with inner homomorphisms and tensor products:

Lemma 3.3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}_{|U})
. Then
i_{U!}I(\mathcal{M})
is in the image of the functor:

I:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})).

Proof.

Follows at once by the fact that the functor:

I:\operatorname{PreShv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{PreShv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})),

commutes with sheafification.
∎

Lemma 3.3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
, and
V^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(U,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
. There is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
:

i_{U!}\left(\mathcal{M}_{|U}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}V^{\bullet}\right)=\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}i_{U!}V^{\bullet}.

Proof.

Choose
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{K}(\mathscr{A})
, and
\widetilde{V}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Shv}(U,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
such that we have quasi-isomorphisms:

I(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\quad I(\widetilde{V}^{\bullet})\rightarrow V^{\bullet}.

In this case, by Lemma
3.3.7
we have the following identities:

\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}i_{U!}V^{\bullet}=I(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\bullet})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}i_{U!}I(\widetilde{V}^{\bullet}),\quad\mathcal{M}_{|U}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}V^{\bullet}=I(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\bullet}_{|U})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\widetilde{V}^{\bullet}).

By construction of the derived tensor product and the extension by zero, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that
\left(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}i_{U!}V^{\bullet}\right)^{n}
is the sheafification of the following presheaf :

W\mapsto\bigoplus_{r+s=n}\Gamma(W,I(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}})^{r})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\Gamma(W,I(\widetilde{V})^{s})\textnormal{ if }W\subset U,\quad 0\textnormal{ if }W\not\subset U.

As this presheaf determines
i_{U!}\left(\mathcal{M}_{|U}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}V^{\bullet}\right)^{n}=i_{U!}\left(I(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}})_{|U}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\widetilde{V})^{\bullet}\right)^{n}
, we are done.
∎

Proposition 3.3.9
.

For each admissible open subspace
U\subset X
there is a natural isomorphism of derived functors:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)_{|U}=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}_{|U}}((-)_{|U},(-)_{|U}).

Proof.

Let
U\subset X
be an admissible open subspace, and choose complexes
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
, and
V^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(U,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
. Then we have the following identities:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(U,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))}(V^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}_{|U}}(\mathcal{M}_{|U}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}_{|U}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}_{|U})}(\mathcal{M}_{|U}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}V^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}_{|U})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})}(i_{U!}\left(\mathcal{M}_{|U}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}V^{\bullet}\right),\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}i_{U!}V^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))}(i_{U!}V^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(U,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))}(V^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})_{|U})

,

so the isomorphism follows by Yoneda.
∎

As a consequence of this, it follows that if
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
have cohomology supported on a closed subvariety
Y\subset X
, then
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
also has cohomology supported on
Y
.

Definition 3.3.10
.

Let
U\subset X
be an admissible open subspace. We define the functor:

R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}_{|U}}((-)_{|U},(-)_{|U}):=R\Gamma(U,R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)):\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})\\
\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})).

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define:

\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{n}_{\mathscr{A}_{|U}}((-)_{|U},(-)_{|U})=\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}_{|U}}((-)_{|U},(-)_{|U})\right).

We now recall the definition of the constant sheaf functor:

Definition 3.3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an elementary quasi-abelian category. The constant sheaf functor is the functor:

c:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\mathcal{C}),

defined by sending an object
x\in\mathscr{C}
to the sheafification of the constant presheaf of value
x
. We call
c(x)
the constant sheaf of stalk
x
.

Notice that
c
is strongly exact, and fits into a commutative diagram:

(22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})):\Gamma(X,-)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})):\Gamma(X,-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

where the pairs of horizontal maps are adjunctions. As the functor:

c:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})),

is exact, it induces a derived adjunction:

c:\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\leftrightarrows\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))):R\Gamma(X,-).

We may use this adjunction to show the following:

Proposition 3.3.12
.

The functor
\widetilde{\operatorname{Hom}}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(-,-):=\Gamma(X,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(-,-))

admits a right derived functor:

R\widetilde{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-):\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})\times\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}).

Furthermore, there is a derived adjunction:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{K}c(\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))}(\mathcal{V}^{\bullet},R\widetilde{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})),

where
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
, and
\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
.
Furthermore, we have:

R\widetilde{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)=R\Gamma(X,-)\circ R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-).

Proof.

The adjunction
c:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})):\Gamma(X,-)
, together with the adequate version of
(\ref{equation tensor relative hom adjunction})
, induces an adjunction:

\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(\mathcal{M}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}c(\mathcal{V}),\mathcal{N})=\operatorname{Hom}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(\mathcal{V},\widetilde{\operatorname{Hom}}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})),

where
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
, and
\mathcal{V}\in LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
. As stated above, the objects in the essential image of
I:\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
are flat with respect to
-\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}-
. In particular, commutativity of the diagram above shows that for every
\mathcal{V}\in LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
we may find some
W\in\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
such that there is an epimorphism
I(c(W))\rightarrow c(\mathcal{V})
, and
I(c(W))
is flat with respect to
-\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}-
. Therefore, the functor:

-\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}c(-):\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))\times LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A})),

satisfies the conditions of
[
25
, Theorem 14.4.8]
, and it follows that
R\widetilde{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}_{|U}}(-,-)
exists, and it is right adjoint to
-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}c(-)
in the derived category.

For the second part of the statement, choose
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
, and
\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
. Then by the identity in
(\ref{equation derived adjunction constant sheaf global functions})
we have:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))}(\mathcal{V}^{\bullet},R\Gamma(X,R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})))\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(c(\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}),R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}))\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{K}c(\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}).

Hence, Yoneda’s Lemma implies that
R\widetilde{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)=R\Gamma(X,-)\circ R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(-,-)
.
∎

We can give a simple characterization of the
\underline{\mathcal{E}xt}_{\mathscr{A}}
sheaves in some situations:

Lemma 3.3.13
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}^{-}(\mathscr{A})
, and
\mathcal{I}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
be homotopically injective. Then the complex:

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Tot}_{\pi}\left(\underline{\mathcal{H}om}^{\bullet,\bullet}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{-\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})\right),

is a homotopically injective complex in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{K}(I(\mathscr{A}))
be an acyclic complex. We need to show that:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}(I(\mathscr{A}))}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}(I(\mathscr{A}))}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})=0.

As
\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}
is homotopically injective, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})
is an acyclic complex. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have the truncations:
\sigma_{\geq n}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})
, given by:

\sigma_{\geq n}I(\mathcal{M}^{m})=I(\mathcal{M}^{m})\textnormal{ for }m\geq n,\textnormal{ and }\sigma_{\geq n}I(\mathcal{M}^{m})=0\textnormal{ for }m<n.

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the truncation
\sigma_{\geq n}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})
is in the essential image of the functor:

I:\operatorname{K}^{b}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{K}^{b}(I(\mathscr{A})),

and we have a filtered colimit
I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\varinjlim\sigma_{\geq n}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})
.

As tensor products commute with colimits, and
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is a Grothendieck abelian category, the following identity holds for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
:

\operatorname{H}^{m}\left(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})\right)=\varinjlim_{n}\operatorname{H}^{m}\left(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\sigma_{\geq n}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})\right).

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the complex
\sigma_{\geq n}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})
is a bounded complex of flat objects. Hence, as
\mathcal{N}
is acyclic, a standard spectral sequence argument shows that
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\sigma_{\geq n}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})
is acyclic for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular,
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})
is also acyclic.
∎

Proposition 3.3.14
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}^{-}(\mathscr{A})
,
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
. For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
the inner
n
-th Ext sheaf

\underline{\mathcal{E}xt}^{n}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
is the sheafification of the presheaf:

U\mapsto\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{n}_{\mathscr{A}_{|U}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}_{|U},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}_{|U}).

Proof.

As
\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\mathscr{A}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category, there is a homotopically injective object
\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}
with a quasi-isomorphism
I(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}
. By
(\ref{equation explicit computation of RHom})

it follows that
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})

may be represented in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
by the following complex:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})=\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Tot}_{\pi}\left(\operatorname{Hom}^{\bullet,\bullet}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{-\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})\right).

By Lemma
3.3.13
,
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})
is a homotopically injective complex.
Thus, for each admissible open subspace
U\subset X
we have:

R\Gamma(U,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}))=\Gamma(U,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})).

Fix some integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\underline{\mathcal{E}xt}^{n}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
is the sheafification of the
n
-th cohomology presheaf of
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
. In particular, it is the sheafification of the presheaf defined for each admissible open
U\subset X
by:

\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(\Gamma(U,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})\right)=\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(R\Gamma(U,\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})\right)\\
=\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(R\Gamma(U,R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\right)=\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}_{\mathscr{A}_{|U}}(\mathcal{M}_{|U},\mathcal{N}_{|U}),

and this is the identity we wanted to show.
∎

3.4.
Base-change along a bimodule

In the next two sections, we will develop some technical tools needed for the proof of the version of Kashiwara’s equivalence presented in Proposition
8.1.1
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be another sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
, and let
\mathcal{S}
be a complete
(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{A})
-bimodule. That is, a sheaf
\mathcal{S}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
equipped with actions:

\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S},\quad\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{S},

satisfying that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}}

Our interest in bimodules stems from the fact that every
(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{A})
-bimodule induces an adjunction:

\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B}).

To wit, we have an extension of scalars functor:

\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}-:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B}),

given by sending a left
\mathscr{A}
-module
\mathcal{M}
to the sheaf of Ind-Banach spaces
\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}
with the structure of a
\mathscr{B}
-module given by the formula:

\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\left(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}\right)=\left(\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S}\right)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}.

This functor is often times called extension of scalars with respect to
\mathcal{S}
. In the other direction, we have the restriction of scalars functor:

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},-):\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}),

is defined by sending a left
\mathscr{B}
-module
\mathcal{N}
to the sheaf of Ind-Banach spaces
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})
with the
\mathscr{A}
-module structure given by:

\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A},\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N}),

as in
(\ref{equation map in tensor hom adjunction})
. This functor is denoted restriction of scalars with respect to
\mathcal{S}
.

If
\mathscr{A}=K
, we recover
the extension and restriction of scalars adjunction
(\ref{equation tensor relative hom adjunction})
