. Furthermore, this construction only uses the properties of symmetric monoidal categories. In particular,
I(\mathcal{S})
is a
(I(\mathscr{B}),I(\mathscr{A}))
-bimodule, and we may repeat this constructions in the left hearts to obtain a pair of functors
I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}-
,
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S}),-)
. Let us now investigate the properties of these functors:

Lemma 3.4.1
.

Extension and restriction of scalars along
\mathcal{S}
fits into an adjunction:

\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}-:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B}):\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},-).

In particular, for
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
, and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B})
, we have:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})).

The equivalent adjunction holds for
I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}-
and
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S}),-)
.

Proof.

Choose
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})
. This is equivalent to a map
f:\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
such that the following diagram of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces commutes:

(23)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\left(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Id}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

By the sheaf version of
(\ref{equation relative tensor hom adjunction})
, the map
f:\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
is equivalent to an
\mathscr{A}
-linear map
g:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})
. On the other hand, by construction, we have:

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})=\operatorname{Eq}\left(\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})\rightrightarrows\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})\right).

Notice that the fact that
\mathcal{S}
is a
(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{A})
-bimodule implies that the maps in the equalizer are
\mathscr{A}
-linear. Hence,
f:\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
induces an
\mathscr{A}
-linear map
\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})
if and only if
g
factors through the equalizer. Thus, we need to describe the maps in the equalizer.

The first map sends a morphism
\varphi\in\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})
to the composition:

\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathcal{N},

where the first map is the action of
\mathscr{B}
on
\mathcal{S}
. By functoriality of
(\ref{equation relative tensor hom adjunction})
, it follows that the composition:

\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N}),

is obtained via the adjunction
(\ref{equation relative tensor hom adjunction})
from the following morphism:

\left(\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S}\right)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N},

where the first map is given by the action of
\mathscr{B}
on
\mathcal{S}
. Using the canonical isomorphism of sheaves:

\left(\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S}\right)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}\cong\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\left(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}\right),

it follows that this map is the lower part of the diagram
(\ref{equation first diagram adjunction relative extension of scalars})
.

The second map of the equalizer sends a morphism
\varphi\in\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})
to the composition:

\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Id}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\varphi}\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}.

Thus, the composition
\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})
is readily seen to be induced by the upper part of
(\ref{equation first diagram adjunction relative extension of scalars})
.
Hence, we get a map
\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})
as wanted. The construction of the map in the other direction is analogous.
∎

Lemma 3.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}))
, and
\mathcal{N}\in LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B}))
. We have a functorial canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
:

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(\mathcal{M},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{N})).

Proof.

We prove this by Yoneda. For simplicity, let
\mathcal{C}:=\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
, and let
\mathcal{T}\in\mathcal{C}
. We have the following chain of isomorphisms of
K
-vector spaces:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{T},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(\left(I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}\mathcal{M}\right)\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{T},\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}\left(\mathcal{M}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{T}\right),\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(\mathcal{M}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{T},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{N}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{T},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(\mathcal{M},\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N})))

.

Thus the isomorphism follows by Yoneda’ Lemma.
∎

We will now deal with the derived functors associated to this adjunction. Instead of dealing with the general case, we will assume that
\mathcal{S}
satisfies that for any
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
we have:

\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}=I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}I(\mathcal{M})=I(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}).

In this case, the construction of the functor
\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathbb{L}}-
shown in
(\ref{equation derived functor relative tensor product in boinded above derived categories})
implies that we have a derived functor
\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathbb{L}}-:\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{B})
of the extension of scalars. Similarly, as
LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B}))
is a Grothendieck abelian category, we get a derived functor

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},-):\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{B})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
.

The idea is that our assumptions on
\mathcal{S}
imply that the adjunction from Lemma
3.4.1
lifts to a derived adjunction. In particular, we have the following proposition:

Proposition 3.4.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B}
be sheaves of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras, and let
\mathcal{S}
be a
(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{A})
-bimodule. Assume
\mathcal{S}
satisfies that for any
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
we have:

\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}=I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}I(\mathcal{M})=I(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}).

Then for
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}^{-}(\mathscr{A})
and
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{B})
we have a functorial identification in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}
be a homotopically injective object equipped with a quasi-isomorphism
I(\mathcal{N})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}
. Our assumptions on
\mathcal{S}
imply that we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{B})
:

\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}=I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}).

Thus,
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
is quasi-isomorphic to the complex:

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A}})I(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Tot}_{\pi}\left(\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}^{\bullet,\bullet}(I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}I(\mathcal{M}^{-\bullet}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})\right).

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
. By Lemma
3.4.2
we have an identification in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
:

\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S})\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}I(\mathcal{M}^{-n}),\mathcal{I}^{m})=\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{-n}),\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{I}^{m})).

In particular, we have an identification:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\cong\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{-\bullet}),\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})).

By construction, we have
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S}),I(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}))\cong\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})
in
\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})
.

Furthermore, as
I(\mathcal{S})
is bounded above, it follows by Lemma
3.3.13
that the complex
\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})
is homotopically injective. Therefore, we have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}))\cong\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{A})}(I(\mathcal{M}^{-\bullet}),\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{I(\mathscr{B})}(I(\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}))

Thus, we arrive at a the following canonical isomorphisms in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})),

which is precisely what we wanted to show.
∎

We point out that a more general statement can be shown by using an argument similar to
[
9
, Proposition 3.36]
. However, we will not develop this here.

3.5.
Closed immersions

Let now
Y
be another rigid space, and assume we have a closed immersion
i:Y\rightarrow X
. Let
\mathscr{B}=i^{-1}\mathscr{A}
. As
i^{-1}
commutes with tensor products, this is a sheaf of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras on
Y
. Furthermore, we have:

Definition 3.5.1
.

Let
X
be a rigid space and
Y\subset X
be an analytically closed subset. We say a sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
(resp. in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
) is supported on
Y
if
\mathcal{F}(U)=0
for every admissible open
U\subset X
such that
U\cap Y=\varnothing
.

Proposition 3.5.2
.

Let
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})_{Y}
denote the full subcategory of

\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})
given by the
\mathscr{A}
-modules supported on
Y
. There is an equivalence of quasi-abelian categories:

i_{*}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})_{Y}:i^{-1}.

Proof.

By
[
4
, Appendix A]
,
i_{*}
and
i^{-1}
yield equivalences of categories between
\operatorname{Shv}(Y,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
and
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))_{Y}
. Thus, the result follows by the facts that
i^{-1}\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{B}
, and that
i^{-1}
commutes with tensor products.
∎

Hence, we have a commutative diagram:

(24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{*,LH}:LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}))_{Y}:i^{-1}_{LH}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})_{Y}:i^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

where the horizontal maps are mutually inverse equivalences of quasi-abelian (resp. abelian) categories, and the vertical maps are the inclusions into the left hearts. The next goal is studying derived versions of these adjunctions. Let us start with the following lemma:

Lemma 3.5.3
.

Let
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))_{Y})
be the derived category of sheaves supported on
Y
. The following hold:

(i)

The mutually inverse equivalences
i_{*},i^{-1}
extend to a derived equivalence:

i_{*}:\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))\leftrightarrows\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))_{Y}):i^{-1}.

(ii)

The canonical map:

\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))_{Y})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))),

is fully faithful, its essential image
are the complexes whose cohomology is supported on
Y
.

The analogous properties hold for the functors:

i_{*}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{B})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})_{Y}:i^{-1}.

Proof.

Statement
(i)
is clear by the fact that:

i_{*}:\operatorname{Shv}(Y,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))_{Y}:i^{-1},

are mutually inverse equivalences of quasi-abelian categories.

For statement
(ii)
, notice that the pushforward
i_{*,LH}:\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
has an exact left adjoint. Hence, its extension to the derived category preserves homotopically injective objects. Therefore, for objects
V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))_{Y})
there is a pair of homotopically injective complexes
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\bullet},\mathcal{I}_{2}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
with quasi-isomorphisms:

V^{\bullet}\rightarrow i_{*,LH}\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\bullet},\quad W^{\bullet}\rightarrow i_{*,LH}\mathcal{I}_{2}^{\bullet}.

Hence, we have the following chain of identities::

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(i_{*,LH}\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\bullet},i_{*,LH}\mathcal{I}_{2}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(i_{*,LH}\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\bullet},i_{*,LH}\mathcal{I}_{2}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\bullet},\mathcal{I}_{2}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\bullet},\mathcal{I}_{2}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))_{Y})}(i_{*,LH}\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\bullet},i_{*,LH}\mathcal{I}_{2}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))_{Y})}(V^{\bullet},W^{\bullet})

.

Thus, the triangulated functor:

\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))_{Y})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))),

is fully faithful. Hence, it is an equivalence onto its essential image.

Let
C^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
be a complex with cohomology supported on
Y
. We have a canonical morphism of complexes
C^{\bullet}\rightarrow i_{*}i^{-1}C^{\bullet}
. It suffices to show that this is a quasi-isomorphism. As both
i_{*}
, and
i^{-1}
are exact, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have:

\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(i_{*}i^{-1}C^{\bullet}\right)=i_{*,LH}i^{-1}_{LH}\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(C^{\bullet}\right)=\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(C^{\bullet}\right),

where the last identity follows by the fact that
\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(C^{\bullet}\right)\in\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
is supported on
Y
. Thus, the map
C^{\bullet}\rightarrow i_{*}i^{-1}C^{\bullet}
is a quasi-isomorphism, as required.
∎

We may use this lemma to study the derived inner hom of complexes with support contained in
Y
. This will be instrumental for the version of Kashiwara equivalence given in Proposition
8.1.1
. In order to keep the notation manageable, we will write:

\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})=\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})),

and follow a similar convention for other sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras.

Lemma 3.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})_{Y}
. Then
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})_{Y}
.

Proof.

We need to show that
R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
has cohomology supported on
Y
.
This follows by Proposition
3.3.9
, as for every
U\subset X
such that
U\cap Y
is empty, we have the following identities in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(U,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})_{|U}=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}_{|U}}(\mathcal{M}_{|U}^{\bullet},\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}_{|U})=0,

where the last identity follows by the fact that
\mathcal{M}_{|U}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}_{|U}=0
in
\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}_{|U})_{Y\cap U}
, because their cohomology is supported on
Y
.
∎

Proposition 3.5.5
.

There is a natural isomorphism of derived functors:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(-,-)=i^{-1}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(i_{*}(-),i_{*}(-)).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
, and
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{B})
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},i^{-1}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(i_{*}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},i_{*}\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(i_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{A}}(i_{*}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},i_{*}\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})}(i_{*}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}i_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},i_{*}\mathcal{T}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{B})}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},\mathcal{T}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},\mathcal{T}^{\bullet})).

where the first identity follows by Lemmas
3.5.3
, and
3.5.4
, and the third one by the fact that tensor products commute with pullbacks. Thus, the isomorphism holds by Yoneda.
∎

Many of these properties are also shared by the derived tensor product:

Lemma 3.5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})_{Y}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})_{Y}
. Then
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}
is supported on
Y
.

Proof.

By
[
9
, Theorem 3.24]
, there are full additive subcategories:

\widetilde{P}_{r}\subset LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}})),\quad\widetilde{P}_{l}\subset LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A})),

such that the pair
(\widetilde{P}_{r},\widetilde{P}_{l})
is projective with respect to
-\widetilde{\otimes}_{I(\mathscr{A})}-
(cf.
[
25
, Definition 10.3.9]
).
Let

\mathcal{P}_{r}
be the full subcategory of

LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}))_{Y}
given by the objects contained in
\widetilde{P}_{r}
, and define

\mathcal{P}_{l}
analogously. We claim that
\mathcal{P}_{r}
and
\mathcal{P}_{l}
are full additive subcategories which generate
LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}))_{Y}
and
LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}))_{Y}
respectively. Indeed, start by choosing an object
\mathcal{X}\in LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}))_{Y}
. Then
\mathcal{X}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathscr{A})
is in
\mathcal{P}_{r}
and we have a surjection:

\mathcal{X}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}.

Hence, our claim holds for
\mathcal{P}_{r}
, and an analogous argument works for
\mathcal{P}_{l}
.

As
LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}))_{Y}
and
LH(\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathscr{A}))_{Y}
are Grothendieck abelian categories, it follows by
[
25
, Lemma 14.4.1]
that there are quasi-isomorphisms

P^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
, and
Q^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
such that
P^{\bullet}
and
Q^{\bullet}
are complexes whose objects are direct sums of elements in
\mathcal{P}_{r}
and
\mathcal{P}_{l}
respectively. In particular, they have support contained on
Y
. By the proof of
[
25
, Theorem 14.4.8]
, we have:

\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}=\operatorname{Tot}_{\oplus}\left(P^{\bullet}\widetilde{\otimes}^{\bullet,\bullet}_{I(\mathscr{A})}Q^{\bullet}\right).

Thus,
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}
has cohomology supported on
Y
, as we wanted to show.
∎

Proposition 3.5.7
.

There is a natural isomorphism of functors:

i_{*}\left(-\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{B}}-\right)=i_{*}(-)\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}i_{*}(-).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{B}^{\operatorname{op}})
,
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{B})
, and
C^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{B}}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},C^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{B})}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},C^{\bullet}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{B})}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},i^{-1}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(i_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},i_{*}C^{\bullet}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A})}(i_{*}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(i_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},i_{*}C^{\bullet}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(i_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}i_{*}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},i_{*}C^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))}(i^{-1}\left(i_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}i_{*}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\right),C^{\bullet}),

where we use the derived adjunction
(\ref{equation adjunction relative tensor-hom})
, together with Lemma
3.5.4
, and Proposition
3.5.5
applied to the case
\mathscr{A}=K
. Thus, we have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(Y,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})))
:

\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{B}}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}=i^{-1}\left(i_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}}i_{*}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\right).

By lemmas
3.5.3
, and
3.5.6
, this identity implies the proposition.
∎

4.
Background: Lie algebroids and co-admissible modules

Now that we have established a technical framework in which to develop our formalism of Hochschild (co)-homology, we will introduce the particular objects to which we will apply it. In particular, we will devote this chapter to introducing the sheaves of (Fréchet-Stein) completions of universal enveloping algebras of Lie algebroids, and the category of sheaves of co-admissible modules attached to them. In latter stages, we will define
\mathcal{C}
-complexes, which are a homological generalization of sheaves of co-admissible modules, and discuss some of the operations that can be performed on them. We would like to point out that we do not claim any originality here. In particular, the theory of co-admissible modules over sheaves of Fréchet-Stein completions of universal enveloping algebras of Lie algebroids was first developed by Ardakov-Wadsley in
[
3
]
, and the six functor formalism for
\mathcal{C}
-complexes was developed by Bode in
[
9
]
.

4.1.
Lie algebroids

In this section, we will give a brief review of the theory of Fréchet-Stein completions of universal enveloping algebras of Lie algebroids on a smooth rigid space
X
, and the theory of co-admissible modules attached to them, as developed in
[
3
]
,
[
4
]
,
[
5
]
.

Remark 4.1.1
.

Recall the following result from
[
3
, Section 2.4]
: Let
A
be an affinoid algebra and
M
be a finite
A
-module. Regard
M
as a Banach
K
-vector space with respect to its topology as a finite
A
-module. Then any
K
-linear derivation
f:A\rightarrow M
is bounded. In particular, we have:

\operatorname{Der}_{K}^{b}(A):=\{\textnormal{bounded derivations }f:A\rightarrow A\}=\operatorname{Der}_{K}(A).

For a rigid space
X
, we let
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
be the tangent sheaf, and
\Omega^{1}_{X/K}
be the sheaf of Kähler differentials. By definition, for an affinoid open subspace
U\subset X
we have:

\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(U)=\operatorname{Der}_{K}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)).

Given
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
and
v\in\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(U)
, we denote the evaluation of
v
at
f
by
v(f)
.

Definition 4.1.2
.

We define the following objects:

(i)

(
[
39
, Section 2]
)
Let
R
be a commutative ring and
A
be a
R
-algebra. A smooth
(R,A)
-Lie algebra is a finite projective
A
-module
L
with a structure of
R
-Lie algebra and an
A
-linear map of
R
-Lie algebras:

\rho:L\rightarrow\operatorname{Der}_{R}(A),

called the anchor map. It satisfies that for
a\in A
,
x,y\in L
we have:

[ax,y]=a[x,y]+\rho(x)(a)y.

(ii)

(
[
3
, Definition 6.1]
)
Let
A
be an affinoid
K
-algebra,
\mathcal{A}\subset A
be an affine formal model of
A
, and
L
be a
(K,A)
-Lie algebra. A smooth
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice of
L

is a smooth
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}\subset L
such that:
L=\mathcal{L}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}K
as
(K,A)
-Lie algebras.

(iii)

(
[
3
, Definition 9.1]
)
A Lie algebroid on a rigid
K
-variety
X
is a sheaf of
(K,\mathcal{O}_{X})
-Lie algebras
\mathscr{L}
such that
\mathscr{L}
is a locally finite-free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module.

Notice that
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
is canonically a sheaf of
(K,\mathcal{O}_{X})
-Lie algebras, and that it is a Lie algebroid if and only if
X
is a smooth rigid space.

Let
A
be an affinoid algebra. As shown in
[
39
]
, to every smooth
(K,A)
-Lie algebra
L
we can associate a universal enveloping algebra
U(L)
. This is a noetherian
K
-algebra with a filtration
F_{\bullet}U(L)
which satisfies the PBW theorem. That is, there is a canonical isomorphism of graded
A
-algebras:

\operatorname{gr}_{F}U(L)=\operatorname{Sym}_{A}(L).

We call
F_{\bullet}U(L)
the PBW filtration. Furthermore, if
\mathcal{A}\subset A
is an affine formal model, and
\mathcal{L}\subset L
is a
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice, then we can also form a universal algebra
U(\mathcal{L})
. By

[
3
, Proposition 2.3]
, the algebra
U(\mathcal{L})
is
\mathcal{R}
-flat, it is noetherian if
K
is discretely valued, and satisfies:

U(L)=K\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}U(\mathcal{L}).

Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
be the affinoid space associated to
A
. In virtue of the contents of
[
3
, Section 9]
, we may uniquely extend
L
to a sheaf of
(K,\mathcal{O}_{X})
-Lie algebras on
X
. Namely, let
\mathscr{L}=\operatorname{Loc}(L)
be the unique sheaf on
X
defined on affinoid subdomains
U\subset X
by the rule:

\mathscr{L}(U)=\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)\otimes_{A}L.

Then the
(K,A)
-Lie algebra structure on
L
extends uniquely to a Lie algebroid structure on
\mathscr{L}
. Furthermore, every Lie algebroid on
X
arises in this way.

Let
\mathscr{L}
be a Lie algebroid on
X
. For every affinoid admissible open subspace
V=\operatorname{Sp}(B)\subset X
we have the following identity:

U(\mathscr{L}(V))=U(B\otimes_{A}L)=B\otimes_{A}U(L).

In particular, we have a sheaf of filtered
K
-algebras
U(\mathscr{L})
on
X
such that:

U(\mathscr{L})(V)=U(\mathscr{L}(V)),

We call
U(\mathscr{L})
the sheaf of enveloping algebras of the Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
.

Definition 4.1.3

(
[
42
, Section 3]
)
.

A
K
-algebra
\mathscr{A}
is called a left (right) Fréchet-Stein algebra if there is an inverse system of Banach
K
-algebras
(\mathscr{A}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

The transition maps
\mathscr{A}_{n+1}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}_{n}
have dense image and are left (right) flat for each
n\geq 0
.

(ii)

Each
\mathscr{A}_{n}
is a two-sided noetherian
K
-algebra.

(iii)

There is an isomorphism of
K
-algebras
\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{A}_{n}
.

If the transition maps are left and right flat, we will say that
\mathscr{A}
is a (two-sided) Fréchet-Stein algebra.

Back to our previous setting, let
A
be an affinoid
K
-algebra with an affine formal model
\mathcal{A}\subset A
, and let
L
be a smooth
(K,A)
-Lie algebra with a smooth
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice
\mathcal{L}
. For every
n\geq 0
, the
\mathcal{A}
-module
\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}
is still a smooth
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice. In particular, we can form the enveloping algebra
U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})
. Let
\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})
be the
\pi
-adic completion of
U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})
. Then
\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})
is a
\mathcal{R}
-flat,
\pi
-adically complete, and
\widehat{U}(\pi^{n+1}\mathcal{L})_{K}
is two sided noetherian
K
-algebra. Assume in addition that
\mathcal{L}
satisfies
[\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}]\subset\pi^{2}\mathcal{L}
and
\rho(\mathcal{L})(\mathcal{A})\subset\pi\mathcal{A}
. Then the canonical maps:

\widehat{U}(\pi^{n+1}\mathcal{L})_{K}\rightarrow\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K},

are
two sided flat and have dense image (cf.
[
2
, Theorem 4.1.11]
). Notice that for an arbitrary smooth
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice
\mathcal{L}
, the lattice
\pi\mathcal{L}
satisfies both the assumptions above. In this situation, we can form the following Fréchet-Stein algebra:

\wideparen{U}(L)=\varprojlim\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K},

which we call the Fréchet-Stein enveloping algebra of
L
. The topological
K
-algebra
\wideparen{U}(L)
does not depend on the choice of affine formal model or smooth Lie-lattice, only on the isomorphism class of
L
as a
(K,A)
-Lie algebra. We can use the particular description of Fréchet-Stein algebras to introduce a relevant category of modules:

Definition 4.1.4

(
[
42
, Section 3]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{A}_{n}
be a Fréchet-Stein algebra, and
M
be a left (right)
\mathscr{A}
-module. We say
M
is a co-admissible module if for each
n\geq 0
the
\mathscr{A}_{n}
-modules
M_{n}=\mathscr{A}_{n}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}M
are finite, and the canonical map of
\mathscr{A}
-modules

M\rightarrow\varprojlim_{n}M_{n}
is an isomorphism. We denote the category of co-admissible modules over
\mathscr{A}
with
\mathscr{A}
-linear maps by
\mathcal{C}(\mathscr{A})
.

In particular, the previous discussion shows the existence of
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{U}(L))
, the category of co-admissible modules over the Fréchet-Stein enveloping algebra
\wideparen{U}(L)
. Most of the contents of this paper are centered around this category and its generalizations. As the name suggests, Fréchet-Stein algebras are in particular Fréchet algebras. Furthermore, every co-admissible module admits a canonical Fréchet topology. Thus, by
[
9
, Theorem 6.4]
we may regard
\wideparen{U}(L)
as an Ind-Banach algebra,
and
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{U}(L))
as an abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(L))
.

The canonical map
U(L)\rightarrow\wideparen{U}(L)
is faithfully flat and has dense image. Furthermore, for any affinoid subdomain
V=\operatorname{Sp}(B)\subset X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
, the restriction map
U(L)\rightarrow B\otimes_{A}U(L)
extends uniquely to a
morphism:

\wideparen{U}(L)\rightarrow\wideparen{U}(B\otimes_{A}L)=B\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(L).

Let
\mathscr{L}=\operatorname{Loc}(L)
. These maps allows us to define a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
on
X
, which is defined by the rule:

V\mapsto\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(V)=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(V)).

It can be shown that
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
has trivial higher Čech cohomology groups.

Definition 4.1.5

(
[
3
, Section 8 ]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a sheaf of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
-modules. We say
\mathcal{M}
is a co-admissible module if
\mathcal{M}(X)\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(X)))
, and for every affinoid subdomain
V=\operatorname{Sp}(B)\subset X
we have:

\mathcal{M}(V)=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(V))\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(X))}\mathcal{M}(X).

We let
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}))
be the abelian category of co-admissible
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
-modules with
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
-linear maps.

Given a co-admissible
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(X))
-module
\mathcal{M}
, we obtain a sheaf of co-admissible
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
-modules on
X
by setting:

\operatorname{Loc}(\mathcal{M})(V)=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(V))\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(X))}\mathcal{M},

where
V=\operatorname{Sp}(B)\subset X
is an affinoid subdomain. We may condense the main results on co-admissible modules into the following proposition:

Proposition 4.1.6
.

Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
be a smooth affinoid space equipped with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
. Then the following hold:

(i)

(
[
3
, Theorems 8.2, 8.4]
)
There are mutually inverse equivalences of abelian categories:

\Gamma(X,-):\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}))\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(X))):\operatorname{Loc}(-).

Furthermore, objects in
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}))
have trivial higher Čech cohomology.

(ii)

(
[
3
, Theorem 8.4]
)
A sheaf of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
-modules
\mathcal{M}
is co-admissible if and only if for every admissible affinoid cover
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, we have
\mathcal{M}_{|V_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{|V_{i}}))
.

(iii)

(
[
9
, Proposition 5.5]
)
Let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(X)))
. Then
\mathcal{M}
is an
A
-nuclear Fréchet space (cf.
[
9
, Definition 5.3]
).

If
X
is an arbitrary rigid analytic space with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
, we define its category of co-admissible
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
-modules as the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}))
given by the objects which are co-admissible when restricted to an admissible affinoid cover. The previous proposition shows that this is well defined. Indeed, if
X
is affinoid, we recover our previous definition.

4.2.
Operations on C-complexes

Now that we have defined the category of co-admissible modules, the next step is studying their homological algebra. In particular, we will start by recalling the definition of
\mathcal{C}
-complexes from
[
9
, Section 6]
. These complexes are to be understood as a
p
-adic analog of the bounded complexes with coherent cohomology in the algebraic theory of
\mathcal{D}
-modules. After that, we will introduce
p
-adic versions of some of the classical operations in the theory of
\mathcal{D}
-modules (direct image, inverse image,
etc
).

Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
be a smooth affinoid space,
\mathcal{A}\subset A
an affine formal model, and

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
an
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice. As shown above, the choice of a
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice induces a Fréchet-Stein presentation:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\varprojlim_{n}\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}})_{K}.

We can use this presentation to make the following definition:

Definition 4.2.1

(
[
9
, Section 8]
)
.

Let
C^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. We say
C^{\bullet}
is a
\mathcal{C}
-complex if the following conditions hold:

(i)

\operatorname{H}^{i}(C^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(ii)

For
n\geq 0
we have
\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}})_{K}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\Gamma(X,\operatorname{H}^{i}(C^{\bullet}))=0
for all but finitely many
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Let
\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
be the full subcategory of
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
given by the
\mathcal{C}
-complexes.

The definition of
\mathcal{C}
-complex does not depend on the choice of affine formal model or Lie lattice. Furthermore,
\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is a full triangulated subcategory of
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. For a general smooth rigid space
X
, we say a complex
C^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is a
\mathcal{C}
-complex if its restriction to an (any) admissible affinoid cover is a
\mathcal{C}
-complex. We will now describe the direct image and inverse image functors, as defined in
[
9
]
.
The homological machinery of Ind-Banach spaces allows to give a formulation which is pretty similar to that of the classical theory of algebraic
\mathcal{D}
-modules. Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of smooth rigid analytic spaces. Let us start by introducing the following objects:

Definition 4.2.2

(
[
9
, Lemmas 5.3,5.5]
)
.

The transfer bimodules associated to
f:X\rightarrow Y
are:

\displaystyle\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\rightarrow Y}=\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}f^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y},

\displaystyle\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y\leftarrow X}=\Omega_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\rightarrow Y}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}f^{-1}\Omega_{Y}^{-1}.

The transfer bimodule
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\rightarrow Y}
is a bornological
(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},f^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})
-bimodule, and
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y\leftarrow X}
is a bornological
(f^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
-bimodule. We use the transfer bimodules to define the following operations:

Definition 4.2.3

(
[
9
, Section 7.3,7.4]
)
.

We define the following functors:

(i)

The inverse image functor
f^{!}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is defined via the following formula:

f^{!}\mathcal{M}=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\rightarrow Y}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{f^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}}f^{-1}\mathcal{M}[\operatorname{dim}(X)-\operatorname{dim}(Y)]\\
=\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}f^{-1}\mathcal{M}[\operatorname{dim}(X)-\operatorname{dim}(Y)].

(ii)

The direct image functor
f_{+}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}
is defined by the following formula:

f_{+}\mathcal{N}=Rf_{*}\left(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y\leftarrow X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{N}\right).

We point out that there are versions of these operators for right modules, defined analogously. Some of the good properties of the classic versions of these operations survive to the rigid analytic setting. To us, the most important will be rigid analytic version of Kashiwara’s equivalence: Let
i:X\rightarrow Y
be a closed immersion of smooth rigid analytic

K
-varieties. Let
\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}^{X}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})
denote the category of
\mathcal{C}
-complexes on
Y
with cohomology supported on
X
. We have the following:

Theorem 4.2.4

(
[
9
, Proposition 9.5]
)
.

For
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
, and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}^{X}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})
there is a functorial isomorphism:

R\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})}(i_{+}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=R\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}(\mathcal{M},i^{!}\mathcal{N}).

In particular,
i_{+}:\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}^{X}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}):i^{!}
are mutually inverse equivalences of triangulated categories. Furthermore,
i_{+}
and
i^{!}
send co-admissible modules into co-admissible modules.

5.
Products of Lie algebroids and the bi-enveloping algebra

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic space equipped with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
. The final goal of this paper is developing a formalism of Hochschild (co)-homology for sheaves of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
-bimodules. Define the enveloping algebra of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
as the following sheaf of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras on
X
:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}:=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}.

Ideally, we would wish to study the following derived functors:

\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(-):=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),-),

\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(-):=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{L}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}}-.

The first problem we encounter with this definition is that the sheaf
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}
is pretty badly behaved. In particular, its algebraic properties are not clear, and it is rather hard to determine its global sections. In particular, it is not clear if
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}
is a sheaf of Fréchet-Stein algebras (in fact, this is probably false), and this prevents us from using the material developed in previous sections.

In order to circumvent these issues, we will slightly modify the definition of Hochschild (co)-homology that we wish to study. In particular, we will introduce two auxiliary sheaves of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras on the product
X^{2}
, which we will denote by
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
, and
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
. As we will see, these sheaves are much better behaved than
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}
, and there are exact functors from the category of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}
-modules to the categories of Ind-Banach modules over

\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
and
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
respectively. We will now give a brief description of these sheaves, and explain some of the rationale behind their definitions.

Let us deal with
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
first. The sheaf
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
is called the sheaf of complete bi-enveloping algebras of
\mathscr{L}
, and its defined as the following sheaf of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras on the product
X^{2}:=X\times X
:

\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}):=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\boxtimes}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}=\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}}\left(p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}\right).

Notice that it is a priory not clear that
\wideparen{E}_{X}
is contained in degree zero, nor that it is indeed an algebra. We will see, however, that both properties do hold. Let
\Delta:X\rightarrow X^{2}
be the diagonal embedding. Assuming that
\wideparen{E}_{X}
is an Ind-Banach algebra, it follows from the definition that there is a functor of quasi-abelian categories:

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{E}_{X}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Delta_{*}\mathcal{M}

Using this functor, we will be able to define Hochschild (co)-homology as follows:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(-):=\Delta^{-1}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})}(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\Delta_{*}(-)),\\
\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(-):=\Delta^{-1}\left(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{L}_{\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})}\Delta_{*}(-)\right).

Hence, we may translate our homological problems from
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}
to
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
. At first, it may seem like we are not gaining much with this change. However, it turns out that
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
presents a much nicer behavior than
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}
, and the study of the category sheaves of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
-modules takes most of the following section.

Part of our interest in
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
stems from our second sheaf of algebras,
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
. This sheaf is defined as the following sheaf of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras on
X^{2}
:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2}):=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\boxtimes}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})=\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}}\left(p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\right).

Most of the contents of the section are destined towards studying the properties of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
. As the notation suggests, we will define a Lie algebroid on
X^{2}
, which we denote by
\mathscr{L}^{2}
, satisfying that
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
is the sheaf of complete enveloping algebras of
\mathscr{L}^{2}
. In particular, we can apply the contents of Section
4
, to the category of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
-modules. The relation between
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
and
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
will be further studied in later sections. Let us just mention that the side-switching
operations for
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
-modules can be extended to this setting, and that this indices a natural equivalence of derived categories between
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}))
and
\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2}))
. This equivalence will later be used to give a more convenient description of
\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(-)
and
\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(-)
, and to obtain explicit calculations in certain situations.

5.1.
Products of Lie-Rinehart algebras

Let
X
and
Y
be smooth and separated rigid analytic
K
-varieties, equipped with Lie algebroids
\mathscr{L}_{X}
and
\mathscr{L}_{Y}
. In this section we will construct a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}
on the product
X\times Y
. This Lie algebroid is called the product of
\mathscr{L}_{X}
and
\mathscr{L}_{Y}
, and it can be characterized by the following universal property: Let
\mathscr{L}_{Z}
be a Lie algebroid on
X\times Y
satisfying that we have maps:

\mathscr{L}_{X}\rightarrow p_{X*}\mathscr{L}_{Z},\quad\mathscr{L}_{Y}\rightarrow p_{Y*}\mathscr{L}_{Z},

which are
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear (resp.
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-linear) maps of sheaves of
K
-Lie algebras. Then there is a unique map of Lie algebroids

\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{Z}
extending these maps. Thus, letting
f^{*}
denote the pullback of coherent modules along a morphism of rigid analytic spaces
f:Z\rightarrow T
, it follows that
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}
is in some sense the smallest Lie algebroid on
X\times Y
containing the Lie algebroids
p_{X}^{*}\mathscr{L}_{X}
and
p_{Y}^{*}\mathscr{L}_{Y}
.

Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
,
Y=\operatorname{Sp}(B)
be smooth affinoid
K
-varieties, and choose affine formal models
\mathfrak{X}=\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{A})
, and
\mathfrak{Y}=\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{B})
. We let
C=A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
, so we have
X\times Y=\operatorname{Sp}(C)
. We will write the projections onto the first and second factors by
p_{X}:X\times Y\rightarrow X
, and
p_{Y}:X\times Y\rightarrow Y
respectively.

Proposition 5.1.1
.

\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
is an admissible
\mathcal{R}
-algebra such that the following identity holds:

\left(\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\right)\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}K=A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
. We need to show that
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathcal{R}
-flat and topologically of finite presentation. We start by choosing a pair of topological algebra presentations:

\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{R}\langle x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\rangle/\mathcal{I}_{1},\quad\mathcal{B}\cong\mathcal{R}\langle y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\rangle/\mathcal{I}_{2},

where
\mathcal{I}_{1}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
are finitely generated ideals of
\mathcal{R}\langle x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\rangle

and
\mathcal{R}\langle y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\rangle
respectively. Consider the following
\mathcal{R}\langle x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\rangle\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}\langle y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\rangle
-module:

\mathcal{J}:=\mathcal{I}_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}\langle y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\rangle+\mathcal{R}\langle x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\rangle\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{I}_{2}.

As all
\mathcal{R}
-modules mentioned above are flat, we have the following short exact sequence of
\mathcal{R}
-modules:

(25)

0\rightarrow\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}\langle x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\rangle\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}\langle y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\rangle\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow 0.

As
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
is flat over
\mathcal{R}
, it does not have
\pi
-torsion. Thus, the
\pi
-adic completion of the previous sequence is still exact, and this shows that
\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
is topologically of finite presentation over
\mathcal{R}
. In order to show that
\mathcal{C}
is flat, it suffices to see that it is torsion-free. We start by showing that it does not have
\pi
-torsion. As the algebra
\mathcal{R}\langle x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\rangle\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}\langle y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\rangle=\mathcal{R}\langle x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\rangle
is flat over
\mathcal{R}
, it suffices to show that the map
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}/\pi\rightarrow\mathcal{R}\langle x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\rangle/\pi
is injective. However, this follows by flatness of
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
together with exactness of the sequence in equation (
25
). Hence,
\mathcal{C}
does not have
\pi
-torsion. As the completion of
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
does not depend on the choice of the pseudo-uniformizer, the argument above shows that

\mathcal{C}
is
\rho
-torsion free for every choice of pseudo-uniformizer
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
. In other words,
\mathcal{C}
is flat as a
\mathcal{R}
-module.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}=\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{C})
.
By Proposition
5.1.1
,
\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}
is an affine formal model of the rigid analytic space
X\times Y
.

Proposition 5.1.2

(
[
16
, Proposition 16.5]
)
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and
A,B
two
R
-algebras. There is a canonical isomorphism of
A\otimes_{R}B
-modules:

\Omega^{1}_{A\otimes_{R}B/R}=\Omega^{1}_{A/R}\otimes_{R}B\oplus A\otimes_{R}\Omega^{1}_{B/R}.

We can extend this proposition to the setting of formal schemes, as follows:

Lemma 5.1.3
.

We have the following identity of
\mathcal{C}
-modules:

\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{R}}=\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{R}}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{R}}.

In particular, we have the following identity:

\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{A})\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{B})=\widehat{\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}}(\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}).

Proof.

Let
D=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
. Notice that we have
\mathcal{C}=\widehat{D}
. Let
M
be a
\mathcal{C}
-module. The
\pi
-adic topology on
M
makes it a topological
\mathcal{C}
-module. Hence, we have the following chain of identities:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{R}},M)=\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{C},M)=\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(D,M)\\
=\operatorname{Hom}_{D}(\Omega^{1}_{D/\mathcal{R}},M)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{D}}(\Omega^{1}_{D/\mathcal{R}}\otimes_{D}\mathcal{C},M),

where the second identity follows by the fact that
D
is dense in
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{R}
-linear derivations are continuous with respect to the
\pi
-adic topology. Next, notice that by Lemma
5.1.3
we have:

\Omega^{1}_{D/\mathcal{R}}=\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{R}}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{R}}.

As
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are affine formal models of
A
and
B
respectively, the modules
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{R}}
and
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{R}}
are finitely generated over
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
. Furthermore, they are flat as
\mathcal{R}
-modules. Thus, it follows by
[
10
, Theorem 7.3.4]

that
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{R}}
and
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{R}}
are finitely presented over
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
respectively. Hence,
\Omega^{1}_{D/\mathcal{R}}
is a finitely presented
D
-module, and this implies that
\widehat{\Omega}^{1}_{D/\mathcal{R}}=\Omega^{1}_{D/\mathcal{R}}\otimes_{D}\mathcal{C}
. The last part of the lemma is a direct consequence of the isomorphism
\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{B})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(\Omega_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{R}},\mathcal{B})
.
∎

As
\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}
is an affine formal model of
X\times Y
, we immediately get:

Lemma 5.1.4
.

There is a canonical isomorphism of coherent
X\times Y
-modules:

\Omega^{1}_{X\times Y/K}=p_{X}^{*}\Omega^{1}_{Y/K}\oplus p_{Y}^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/K}.

In particular, we have the following isomorphism of
C
-modules:

(26)

\mathcal{T}_{X\times Y/K}(X\times Y)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}}(\Omega^{1}_{X\times Y/K},\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y})\\
=\operatorname{Der}_{K}(A)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B\oplus A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\operatorname{Der}_{K}(B).

Proof.

follows by Lemma
5.1.2
, together with remark
4.1.1
.
∎

The commutator induces a
(K,A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B)
-Lie algebra structure on the tangent sheaf
\mathcal{T}_{X\times Y/K}(X\times Y)
. We will now give an explicit description of this structure in terms of the decomposition obtained in equation
(\ref{equation decomposition of vector fields on a product})
.

Lemma 5.1.5
.

For
i=1,2
, let
\omega_{i}=(v_{i}\otimes f_{i})\oplus(g_{i}\otimes w_{i})\in\mathcal{T}_{X\times Y/K}(X\times Y)

be vector fields on
X\times Y
. The following identity holds:

(27)

[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]=([v_{1},v_{2}]\otimes f_{1}f_{2})\oplus(g_{2}g_{1}\otimes[w_{1},w_{2}])\\
+(-g_{2}v_{1}\otimes w_{2}(f_{1}))\oplus(v_{1}(g_{2})\otimes f_{1}w_{2})+(g_{1}v_{2}\otimes w_{1}(f_{2}))\oplus(-v_{2}(g_{1})\otimes f_{2}w_{1}).

Proof.

Consider the following derivations:

\delta_{1}=[(v_{1}\otimes f_{1})\oplus 0,(v_{2}\otimes f_{2})\oplus 0]\textnormal{, }\delta_{2}=[0\oplus(g_{1}\otimes w_{1}),0\oplus(g_{2}\otimes w_{2})].

For any
a\otimes b\in A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
we have:

\delta_{1}(a\otimes b)=((v_{1}\otimes f_{1})(v_{2}\otimes f_{2})-(v_{2}\otimes f_{2})(v_{1}\otimes f_{1}))(a\otimes b)\\
=(v_{1}(v_{2}(a))\otimes f_{1}f_{2}b)-(v_{2}(v_{1}(a))\otimes f_{1}f_{2}b)\\
=([v_{1},v_{2}](a)\otimes f_{1}f_{2}b)=([v_{1},v_{2}]\otimes f_{1}f_{2})(a\otimes b).

Thus, we have
\delta_{1}=([v_{1},v_{2}]\otimes f_{1}f_{2})\oplus 0
. Symmetrically, we also get the identity
\delta_{2}=0\oplus(g_{1}g_{2}\otimes[w_{1},w_{2}])
. Next, consider the derivations:

\delta_{3}=[(v_{1}\otimes f_{1})\oplus 0,0\oplus(g_{2}\otimes w_{2})],\textnormal{ }\delta_{4}=[0\oplus(g_{1}\otimes w_{1}),(v_{2}\otimes f_{2})\oplus 0].

For any
a\otimes b\in A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
we have:

\displaystyle\delta_{3}(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=v_{1}(g_{2}a)\otimes f_{1}w_{2}(b)-g_{2}v_{1}(a)\otimes w_{2}(f_{1}b)

\displaystyle=(g_{2}v_{1}(a)+av_{1}(g_{2}))\otimes f_{1}w_{2}(b)-g_{2}v_{1}(a)\otimes(f_{1}w_{2}(b)+bw_{2}(f_{1}))

\displaystyle=av_{1}(g_{2})\otimes f_{1}w_{2}(b)-g_{2}v_{1}(a)\otimes bw_{2}(f_{1}).

Thus, we have:

\delta_{3}=(-g_{2}v_{1}\otimes w_{2}(f_{1}))\oplus(v_{1}(g_{2})\otimes f_{1}w_{2}),\textnormal{ and }\delta_{4}=(g_{1}v_{2}\otimes w_{1}(f_{2}))\oplus(-v_{2}(g_{1})\otimes f_{2}w_{1}).

As Lie brackets are bilinear, we have
[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\delta_{i}
, as wanted.
∎

Let now
(L_{1},[-,-]_{1},\tau_{1})
be a finite free
(K,A)
-Lie algebra,
and
(L_{2},[-,-]_{2},\tau_{2})
be a finite free
(K,B)
-Lie algebra. For simplicity in the notation, we will often just denote them by
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, and omit the sub-indices from the brackets and anchor maps when no confusion is possible. We make the following definition:

Definition 5.1.6
.

Let
L_{1}\times L_{2}
be the
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
-module given by:

L_{1}\times L_{2}:=p_{X}^{*}L_{1}\oplus p_{Y}^{*}L_{2}=L_{1}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B\oplus A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}L_{2},

where the second identity holds because
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are finitely generated over
A
and
B
respectively.

As
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
is an affinoid algebra, it induces a unique Banach topology on
L_{1}\times L_{2}
. Consider the
A\otimes_{K}B
-module:

\mathfrak{L}=L_{1}\otimes_{K}B\oplus A\otimes_{K}L_{2}.

It is clear that
\mathfrak{L}
is a dense subspace of
L_{1}\times L_{2}
. In particular, considering
\mathfrak{L}
as a locally convex
K
-vector space with respect to the subspace topology, it follows that it is a topological
A\otimes_{K}B
-module, such that its completion agrees with

L_{1}\times L_{2}
. Furthermore, by Lemma
5.1.4
we have:

\operatorname{Der}_{K}(A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B)=\widehat{\operatorname{Der}}_{K}(A\otimes_{K}B).

Hence, in order to construct a
(K,A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B)
-Lie algebra structure on
L_{1}\times L_{2}
, it suffices to construct a

(K,A\otimes_{K}B)
-Lie algebra structure on
\mathfrak{L}
such that the Lie bracket and the anchor map are continuous.

Let us start by recalling some constructions on filtered algebras:

Definition 5.1.7
.

(i)

Let
R
be a ring and let
A
and
B
be two filtered
R
-algebras. Assume the filtrations
F_{\bullet}A
and
F_{\bullet}B
satisfy
R\subset F_{0}A,F_{0}B
. For each
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\overline{F_{i}A\otimes_{R}F_{j}B}
be the image of the map:

F_{i}A\otimes_{R}F_{j}B\rightarrow A\otimes_{R}B.

We obtain a filtration on the tensor product
C=A\otimes_{R}B
given by the following family of
\mathcal{R}
-submodules:

F_{n}C=\sum_{i+j=n}\overline{F_{i}A\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}F_{j}B}.

We denote this the tensor filtration. Unless mentioned, we will regard
C
as a filtered algebra with respect to the tensor filtration.

(ii)

For graded
R
-algebras
A
and
B
, satisfying
R\subset A_{0}
and
R\subset B_{0}
, we define the tensor grading on
C
using the analogous construction.

Remark 5.1.8
.

(i)

If
F_{\bullet}A
and
F_{\bullet}B
are exhaustive, then
F_{\bullet}C
is also exhaustive.

(ii)

The tensor filtration (grading) on
R[x,y]
induced by the isomorphism
R[x,y]\cong R[x]\otimes_{R}R[y]
is the usual filtration (grading) by degree of monomials.

We may now apply these definitions to out case of interest:

Lemma 5.1.9
.

The following hold:

(i)

F_{0}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)=A\otimes_{K}B

(ii)

[A\otimes_{K}B,F_{n}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)]\subset F_{n-1}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)
.

(iii)

\operatorname{gr}_{F}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)
is an
A\otimes_{K}B
-algebra.

Proof.

By definition we have the following identities:

F_{0}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)=F_{0}U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}F_{0}U(L_{2})=A\otimes_{K}B,

so claim
(i)
holds. For statement
(ii)
, choose
a\otimes b\in A\otimes_{K}B
,
v\in F_{i}U(L_{1})
,
w\in F_{j}U(L_{2})
satisfying
i+j=n
. Then we have the following chain of identities:

[a\otimes b,v\otimes w]=av\otimes bw-va\otimes wb=av\otimes([b,w]+wb)-va\otimes wb=[a,v]\otimes wb+av\otimes[b,w].

By the PBW Theorem
[
39
, Theorem 3.1]
, we have
[a,v]\in F_{i-1}U(L_{1})
, and
[b,w]\in F_{j-1}U(L_{2})
. Hence, we have

[a\otimes b,v\otimes w]\in F_{n-1}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)
, so statement
(ii)
holds. Passing to the associated graded we have:

A\otimes_{K}B\subset Z(\operatorname{gr}_{F}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)),

which shows statement
(iii)
.
∎

Proposition 5.1.10
.

We have a canonical isomorphism of graded
A\otimes_{K}B
-algebras:

\operatorname{Sym}_{A}(L_{1})\otimes_{K}\operatorname{Sym}_{B}(L_{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{gr}_{F}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right).

Thus,
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
is a filtered
K
-algebra such that its associated graded is commutative. In other words, it is an an almost commutative filtered
K
-algebra (cf.
[
1
, Definition 3.4]
).

Proof.

By construction of the tensor filtration, the inclusions:

U(L_{1})\rightarrow U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2}),\textnormal{ and }U(L_{2})\rightarrow U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2}),

are maps of filtered
K
-algebras. Hence, we get a map of graded
K
-algebras:

\psi:\operatorname{Sym}_{A}(L_{1})\otimes_{K}\operatorname{Sym}_{B}(L_{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{gr}_{F}(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})),

where the grading on the left-hand side is the tensor grading.
By Lemma
5.1.9
, the zero-graded
K
-algebra on both sides is
A\otimes_{K}B
. Hence, this is in fact a morphism of
A\otimes_{K}B
-algebras.

Let
x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}
be an ordered basis of
L_{1}
as an
A
-module, and
y_{1},\cdots,y_{m}
be an ordered basis of
L_{2}
as a
B
-module. By the PBW Theorem
[
39
, Theorem 3.1]
, for each
i\geq 0
the
A
-module
F_{i}U(L_{1})
is free, on a basis given by
A
-linear combinations of elements of the form:

x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}},\textnormal{ with }\sum_{s=1}^{n}\alpha_{s}\leq i.

By symmetry, we get an analogous description of the
B
-module structure of
F_{i}U(L_{2})
.

Therefore, we have a basis of
F_{l}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)=\sum_{i+j=l}F_{i}U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}F_{j}U(L_{2})

as an
A\otimes_{K}B
-module given by the following simple tensors:

x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}\otimes y_{1}^{\beta_{1}}\cdots y_{m}^{\beta_{m}},\textnormal{ such that }\sum_{s=1}^{n}\alpha_{s}+\sum_{s=1}^{m}\beta_{s}\leq l.

In particular,
F_{l}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)/F_{l-1}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)
is a free
A\otimes_{K}B
-module, with a basis given by the simple tensors:

x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}\otimes y_{1}^{\beta_{1}}\cdots y_{m}^{\beta_{m}},\textnormal{ such that }\sum_{s=1}^{n}\alpha_{s}+\sum_{s=1}^{m}\beta_{s}=l.

The family of all such tensors, for varying
l
, yields a basis of
\operatorname{gr}_{F}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)
as a module over
A\otimes_{K}B
. Notice that
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}(L_{1})
and
\operatorname{Sym}_{B}(L_{2})
are polynomial algebras over
A
and
B
respectively. In particular, we have an isomorphism of graded
A
-algebras (resp.
B
-algebras):

\operatorname{Sym}_{A}(L_{1})\rightarrow A[x_{1},\cdots x_{n}],\textnormal{ }\operatorname{Sym}_{B}(L_{2})\rightarrow B[y_{1},\cdots y_{m}].

Hence, we have an isomorphism of graded
A\otimes_{K}B
-algebras:

\operatorname{Sym}_{A}(L_{1})\otimes_{K}\operatorname{Sym}_{B}(L_{2})\rightarrow A[x_{1},\cdots x_{n}]\otimes_{K}B[y_{1},\cdots y_{m}]\\
\cong A\otimes_{K}B[x_{1},\cdots x_{n},y_{1},\cdots y_{m}].

Notice that by remark
5.1.8
the grading on
A\otimes_{K}B[x_{1},\cdots x_{n},y_{1},\cdots y_{m}]
induced by the tensor grading is the usual grading on polynomial rings. Thus,
\psi
maps a basis of
A\otimes_{K}B[x_{1},\cdots x_{n},y_{1},\cdots y_{m}]
as an
A\otimes_{K}B
-module to a basis of the graded algebra
\operatorname{gr}_{F}(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2}))
. Therefore,
\psi
is an isomorphism of graded
A\otimes_{K}B
-algebras, as we wanted to show.
∎

Corollary 5.1.11
.

For any
i\geq 0
, we have a decomposition of
A\otimes_{K}B
-modules:

F_{i}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{i}F_{j}U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}F_{i-j}U(L_{2}).

In particular,
F_{i}U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
is a finite-free
A\otimes_{K}B
module.

In particular, we have an injective map
of
K
-algebras:

j:A\otimes_{K}B\rightarrow U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2}).

This gives
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
a structure of a left
A\otimes_{K}B
-module. On the other hand, the PBW Theorem also yields injective maps of
A
-modules (resp.
B
-modules):

i_{1}:L_{1}\rightarrow U(L_{1}),\quad i_{2}:L_{2}\rightarrow U(L_{2}).

Thus, we get the following morphism of
A\otimes_{K}B
-modules:

i:\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2}).

We will define the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{L}
using the commutator on
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
:

Corollary 5.1.12
.

The following hold:

(i)

i:\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
is injective. Thus, we may identify
\mathfrak{L}
with its image.

(ii)

\mathfrak{L}=F_{1}(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2}))\setminus A\otimes_{K}B
.

(iii)

The commutator on
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
induces a
K
-Lie algebra structure:

[-,-]:\mathfrak{L}\times\mathfrak{L}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{L}.

(iv)

For
i=1,2
, let
\omega_{i}=(v_{i}\otimes f_{i})\oplus(g_{i}\otimes w_{i})\in\mathfrak{L}
. The bracket on
\mathfrak{L}
is:

(28)

[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]=([v_{1},v_{2}]\otimes f_{1}f_{2})\oplus(g_{2}g_{1}\otimes[w_{1},w_{2}])\\
+(-g_{2}v_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}(w_{2})(f_{1}))\oplus(\tau_{1}(v_{1})(g_{2})\otimes f_{1}w_{2})\\
+(g_{1}v_{2}\otimes\tau_{2}(w_{1})(f_{2}))\oplus(-\tau_{1}(v_{2})(g_{1})\otimes f_{2}w_{1}).

Proof.

Let
U=U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
. Assume that
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
have basis given by
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
and
y_{1},\cdots,y_{m}
as free modules over
A
and
B
respectively. Then, we have a basis of
F_{1}(U)\setminus A\otimes_{K}B
given by the simple tensors
x_{i}\otimes 1
,
1\otimes y_{j}
, where
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m
. As this is a basis of
\mathfrak{L}
over
A\otimes_{K}B
, it follows that
(i)
and
(ii)
hold.

As the associated graded
\operatorname{gr}(U)
is commutative, we have
[F_{i}U,F_{j}U]\subset F_{i+j-1}U
for all
i,j\geq 0
. In particular, we have:
[\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{L}]\subset F_{1}U=\mathfrak{L}\oplus(A\otimes_{K}B)
. Thus, statement
(iii)
holds if and only if
[\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{L}]\cap A\otimes_{K}B=0
. Hence, it is enough to show
(iv)
. However, as the commutator bracket is
K
-bilinear, and we have
[x,a]=\tau_{1}(x)(a)
,
[y,b]=\tau_{1}(y)(b)
for all
x\in L_{1}
,
y\in L_{2}
,
a\in A
, and
b\in B
, we can repeat the calculations leading up to equation
(\ref{description bracket product of vector fields})
to show that statement
(iv)
holds.
∎

As a consequence, we get the following:

Lemma 5.1.13
.

Consider the map:

\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}=\tau_{1}\otimes_{K}\operatorname{Id}_{B}\oplus\operatorname{Id}_{A}\otimes_{K}\tau_{2}:\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow\operatorname{Der}_{K}(A\otimes_{K}B).

The following hold:

(i)

\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}
is an
A\otimes_{K}B
-linear map of
K
-Lie algebras.

(ii)

(\mathfrak{L},[-,-],\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2})
is a
(K,A\otimes_{K}B)
-Lie algebra.

(iii)

We have a canonical isomorphism of filtered
K
-algebras:

U(\mathfrak{L})\rightarrow U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2}).

Proof.

By definition, the anchor map
\tau_{1}
( resp.
\tau_{2}
) is a morphism of
A
-modules (resp.
B
-modules). Thus, it follows that
\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}
is
A\otimes_{K}B
-linear. Furthermore, both are maps of
K
-Lie algebras. One can check that applying
\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}
to equation
(\ref{equation Lie bracket on a product})
yields equation
(\ref{description bracket product of vector fields})
, thus showing that
\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}
is a morphism of
K
-Lie algebras. Hence, statement
(i)
holds.

Statement
(ii)
follows by inspection of
(\ref{equation Lie bracket on a product})
. In order to show
(iii)
, it is enough to show that
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
satisfies the universal property of enveloping algebras with respect to the maps:

i:\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2}),\quad j:A\otimes_{K}B\rightarrow U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2}).

Let
D
be an associative
K
-algebra, together with a map of
K
-Lie algebras
i_{D}:\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow D
, and a map of
K
-algebras
j_{D}:A\otimes_{K}B\rightarrow D
. We also assume that for each
f\in A\otimes_{K}B
, and each
v\in\mathfrak{L}
we have
j_{D}(f)i_{D}(v)=i_{D}(fv)
. Consider the restrictions of
i_{D}
and
j_{D}
to
L_{1}\otimes_{K}1
and
A\otimes_{K}1
respectively. This induces a unique map of
K
-algebras
U(L_{1})\rightarrow D
. Similarly, we get a unique map of
K
-algebras
U(L_{2})\rightarrow D
. Hence, we get a unique map of
K
-algebras
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\rightarrow D
extending
i_{D}
and
j_{D}
, as wanted.
∎

The next step is showing that the
(K,A\otimes_{K}B)
-Lie algebra structure on
\mathfrak{L}
extends to a
(K,A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B)
-Lie algebra structure
L_{1}\times L_{2}
. In particular, we need to show that the bracket and anchor map are continuous. As
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are free, we may choose an
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice
\mathcal{L}_{1}
of
L_{1}
, and a
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B})
-Lie lattice
\mathcal{L}_{2}
of
L_{2}
. The topology of
L_{1}\times L_{2}
as a finite
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
-module is induced by the
\pi
-adic topology on the finite
\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
-module:

\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\mathcal{L}_{2}=\mathcal{L}_{1}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{L}_{2}.

Notice that this lattice is the
\pi
-adic completion of the

\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
-module:

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{L}_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{L}_{2}.

As
\mathcal{R}
-linear maps between spaces with the
\pi
-adic topology are always continuous, it suffices to show that the restriction of the bracket and anchor map of
\mathfrak{L}
to
\mathcal{M}
makes
\mathcal{M}
a
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B})
-Lie algebra.

Lemma 5.1.14
.

The following hold:

(i)

\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}(\mathcal{M})\subset\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B})
.

(ii)

[\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}]\subset\mathcal{M}
.

In particular,
(\mathcal{M},[-.-],\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2})
is a
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B})
-Lie algebra.

Proof.

By definition, both
\mathcal{L}_{i}
are Lie lattices. Thus, we have:

\tau_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{1})\subset\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{A}),\quad\tau_{2}(\mathcal{L}_{2})\subset\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{B}).

Therefore, we have:

\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}(\mathcal{M})\subset\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{A})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{B})=\operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}).

For the second part, notice that equation
(\ref{equation Lie bracket on a product})
gives an explicit description of the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{L}
. As
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
are Lie lattices, they are closed under their respective brackets, as well as closed by multiplication by elements in
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
respectively. Hence, we may use equation
(\ref{equation Lie bracket on a product})
to show that
[\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}]\subset\mathcal{M}
.
∎

We may summarize our findings so far into the following proposition:

Proposition 5.1.15
.

The following hold:

(i)

(L_{1}\times L_{2},[-,-],\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2})
, is a finite-free
(K,A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B)
-Lie algebra.

(ii)

The inclusion
j_{1}:L_{1}\rightarrow L_{1}\times L_{2}
given by
x\mapsto x\otimes 1
is an
A
-linear map of
K
-Lie algebras satisfying
\tau_{1}=(\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2})\circ j_{1}
.

(iii)

The inclusion
j_{2}:L_{2}\rightarrow L_{1}\times L_{2}
given by
y\mapsto 1\otimes y
is a
B
-linear map of
K
-Lie algebras satisfying
\tau_{2}=(\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2})\circ j_{2}
.

Now that we have a local description of
L_{1}\times L_{2}
, we will commit our efforts to giving an explicit description of the universal property claimed at the beginning of the section.

Lemma 5.1.16
.

Let
L
be a
(K,A)
-Lie algebra and
M
be a
(K,A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B)
-Lie algebra. Denote their anchor maps by
\tau_{L}
and
\tau_{M}
respectively. Consider a
K
-linear map
\theta:L\rightarrow M
satisfying the following:

(i)

\theta
is an
A
-linear map of
K
-Lie algebras.

(ii)

\tau_{L}=\tau_{M}\circ\theta
, where we identify
\operatorname{Der}_{K}(A)
with its image inside
\operatorname{Der}_{K}(A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B)
.

Then there is a unique extension
U(\theta):U(L_{1})\rightarrow U(M)
, which is a morphism of filtered
K
-algebras.

Proof.

Clear by the universal property of universal enveloping algebras.
∎

Proposition 5.1.17
.

Let
M
be a
(K,A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B)
-Lie algebra, such that
M
is coherent as a
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
-module. Assume we have
K
-linear maps:

\theta_{1}:L_{1}\rightarrow M,\quad\theta_{2}:L_{2}\rightarrow M,

satisfying the conditions in Lemma
5.1.16
. Then there is a unique map of
(K,A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B)
-Lie algebras:
\theta:L_{1}\times L_{2}\rightarrow M
which induces the following factorization for
j=1,2
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}\times L_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

Proof.

By Lemma
5.1.16
, we get a unique map of associative
K
-algebras:

\psi:U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\rightarrow U(M).

Endowing the left hand side with the tensor filtration, it follows that this is a map of filtered
K
-algebras. In particular, we get a map:

\theta:\mathfrak{L}:=L_{1}\otimes_{K}B\oplus A\otimes_{K}L_{2}\rightarrow M.

By Lemma
5.1.13
, we have a canonical isomorphism
U(\mathfrak{L})\rightarrow U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
. Thus,
\theta
is a
A\otimes_{K}B
-linear map of
K
-Lie algebras which satisfies that
\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}=\tau_{M}\circ\theta
.
As
M
is coherent, it has a unique topology as a finite
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
-module. As this topology is complete, it follows that
\theta
extends uniquely to a map

\theta:L_{1}\times L_{2}\rightarrow M
, which is a morphism of
(K,A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B)
-Lie algebras.
∎

Let
X,Y
be smooth separated rigid analytic varieties, and consider Lie algebroids
\mathscr{L}_{X}
,
\mathscr{L}_{Y}
on
X
and
Y
respectively. Consider the following coherent module:

\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}=p_{X}^{*}\mathscr{L}_{X}\oplus p_{Y}^{*}\mathscr{L}_{Y}.

Using the above, we can show that
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}
is also a Lie algebroid on
X\times Y
.

Indeed, as
\mathscr{L}_{X}
is a Lie algebroid, we can choose an admissible affinoid cover
\mathfrak{U}=\left(U_{i}\right)_{i\in I}
such that for all
i\in I
there is some affine formal model
\mathcal{A}_{i}
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U_{i})
satisfying that
\mathscr{L}_{X}(U_{i})
admits a finite free
\mathcal{A}_{i}
-Lie lattice.
As we assumed that
X
is separated, we may also freely assume that
\mathfrak{U}
is closed under finite intersections. We let
\mathfrak{V}=\left(V_{j}\right)_{j\in J}
be an admissible affinoid cover of
Y
satisfying the analogous conditions for
\mathscr{L}_{Y}
. Thus, we get an admissible affinoid cover
\mathfrak{U\times V}=\left(U_{i}\times V_{j}\right)_{i\in I,j\in J}
of
X\times Y
which is closed under finite intersections.

Let
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y|U_{i}\times V_{j}}
