be the restriction of
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}
to
U_{i}\times V_{j}
.
By construction, we have the following identity:

\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(U_{i}\times V_{j})=\mathscr{L}_{X}(U_{i})\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(V_{j}),

and by Theorem
5.1.15
this has a canonical
(K,\mathcal{O}_{X}(U_{i}\times V_{j}))
-Lie algebra structure.

As
U_{i}\times V_{j}
is an affinoid space,
[
3
, Lemma 9.2]
shows that the
(K,\mathcal{O}_{X}(U_{i}\times V_{j}))
-Lie algebra structure on
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(U_{i}\times V_{j})
extends uniquely to a Lie algebroid structure on
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y|U_{i}\times V_{j}}
. In order to show that all these Lie algebroids are compatible, we just need to show that they agree on the intersections of elements in
\mathfrak{U\times V}
. For any,
i_{1},i_{2}\in I
and
j_{1},j_{2}\in J
,
let
U_{i_{1}i_{2}}=U_{i_{1}}\cap U_{i_{2}}
, and
V_{j_{1}j_{2}}=V_{j_{1}}\cap V_{j_{2}}
.
We then have:

U_{i_{1}}\times V_{j_{1}}\cap U_{i_{2}}\times V_{j_{2}}=U_{i_{1}i_{2}}\times V_{j_{1}j_{2}}.

For simplicity, let
W=U_{i_{1}i_{2}}\times V_{j_{1}j_{2}}
.
We have the following identities of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(W)
-modules:

\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y|U_{i_{1}}\times V_{j_{1}}}(W)=\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y|W}(W)=\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y|U_{i_{2}}\times V_{j_{2}}}(W).

Again by
[
3
, Lemma 9.2]
, it suffices to show that these are, in fact, identifications of
(K,\mathcal{O}_{X}(W))
-Lie algebras. However, by equation
(\ref{equation Lie bracket on a product})
, the brackets and anchor maps of these algebras are uniquely determined by the Lie-Rinehart algebra structures of
\mathscr{L}_{X}(U_{i_{1}i_{2}})
and
\mathscr{L}_{Y}(V_{j_{1}j_{2}})
, so they must agree.

Theorem 5.1.18
.

Let
X
,
Y
be smooth and separated rigid analytic varieties, with Lie algebroids
\mathscr{L}_{X}
,
\mathscr{L}_{Y}
. There is a unique lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}
on
X\times Y
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

For all affinoid subspaces
U\subset X
,
and
V\subset Y
such that
\mathscr{L}_{X}(U)
,
\mathscr{L}_{Y}(V)
are free modules over
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
and
\mathcal{O}_{X}(V)
respectively, we have an identification of
(K,\mathcal{O}_{X}(U\times V))
-Lie algebras:

\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(U\times V)=\mathscr{L}_{X}(U)\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(V).

(ii)

The canonical morphisms:

i_{X}:\mathscr{L}_{X}\rightarrow p_{X*}\left(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}\right),\quad i_{Y}:\mathscr{L}_{Y}\rightarrow p_{Y*}\left(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}\right),

are injective
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear (resp.
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-linear) maps of sheaves of
K
-Lie algebras which satisfy:

\tau_{X}=p_{X*}\left(\tau_{X}\times\tau_{Y}\right)\circ i_{X},\quad\tau_{Y}=p_{Y*}\left(\tau_{X}\times\tau_{Y}\right)\circ i_{Y}.

(iii)

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a Lie algebroid on
X\times Y
, and assume that there are maps:

\theta_{X}:\mathscr{L}_{X}\rightarrow p_{X*}\mathscr{M},\quad\theta_{Y}:\mathscr{L}_{Y}\rightarrow p_{Y*}\mathscr{M},

which satisfy conditions analogous to those in statement
(ii)
. Then there is a unique morphism of Lie algebroids
\theta:\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathscr{M}
,
which satisfies:

\theta_{X}=p_{X*}\theta\circ i_{X},\quad\theta_{Y}=p_{Y*}\theta\circ i_{Y}.

We call
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}
the product of
\mathscr{L}_{X}
and
\mathscr{L}_{Y}
, and we will write
\mathscr{L}_{X}^{2}:=\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{X}
.

Remark 5.1.19
.

Notice that if
\mathscr{L}=\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
, then
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}^{2}=\mathcal{T}_{X^{2}/K}
.

Perhaps the most interesting case is
Y=X
and
\mathscr{L}_{X}=\mathscr{L}_{Y}=\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
. As
X
is separated, the diagonal map
\Delta:X\rightarrow X^{2}
being a closed immersion, and we may apply the version of Kashiwara’s equivalence developed in
[
4
]
to study the relation between the categories of co-admissible modules over
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
and the category of co-admissible modules of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
supported on the diagonal.

5.2.
Universal enveloping algebras of products of Lie-Rinehart algebras

In this section, we will study the relation between the tensor product of the universal enveloping algebras of two finite-free Lie-Rinehart algebras
L_{1},L_{2}
defined over smooth affinoid algebras
A
and
B
, and the universal enveloping algebra of the product
L_{1}\times L_{2}
. in particular, we will show that:

U(L_{1}\times L_{2})=\varinjlim\widehat{F}_{s}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right),

where
\widehat{F}_{s}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)
denotes the completion of
F_{s}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)
with respect to its topology as a finite
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
-module. As in the previous section, this reinforces the intuition that
U(L_{1}\times L_{2})
is the smallest filtered
K
-algebra which contains
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
and
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
.

As in the beginning of the previous section, we let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
,
Y=\operatorname{Sp}(B)
be smooth affinoid
K
-varieties, and choose affine formal models
\mathfrak{X}=\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{A})
, and
\mathfrak{Y}=\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{B})
of
X
and
Y
respectively. We let
C=A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
, and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
, and we choose
\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}=\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{C})
as affine formal model of
X\times Y=\operatorname{Sp}(C)
. As before, we regard
A,B
, and
C
as Banach
K
-algebras with respect to the norm induced by the associated formal models. Fix a pair of finite-free Lie-Rinehart algebras
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
over
A
and
B
respectively, and let
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
be a finite free
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{A})
-Lie lattice and a finite-free
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B})
-Lie lattice respectively.

Lemma 5.2.1
.

We have a canonical map of filtered
K
-algebras:

j:U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\rightarrow U(L_{1}\times L_{2}).

Proof.

This was part of the proof of Proposition
5.1.17
.
∎

Recall that for any
n\geq 0
, the
\mathcal{A}
-module
\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}
is a smooth
\mathcal{A}
-Lie lattice, and the analogous holds for
\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{2}
. This leads us to the following definition:

Definition 5.2.2
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
be non-negative integers. We define the
\mathcal{R}
-algebra:

V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}:=U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}U(\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}),

and regard it as a filtered
\mathcal{R}
-algebra with respect to the tensor filtration.

Notice that for each
n,m\geq 0
we have a canonical isomorphism of filtered
K
-algebras:

V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}K\rightarrow U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2}).

Corollary 5.2.3
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
be non-negative integers. The following holds:

(i)

There is an isomorphism of graded
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
-algebras:

\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathcal{A}}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathcal{B}}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{gr}_{F}(V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}).

(ii)

For any
s\geq 0
, we have a decomposition of
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
-modules:

F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{s}F_{i}U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}F_{s-i}U(\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}).

In particular,
F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is a finite-free
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
module.

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
5.1.10
carries over verbatim.
∎

For every
i\geq 0
we have that
F_{i}U(L_{1})
is a finitely generated
A
-module. Hence, we get a unique topology on
F_{i}U(L_{1})
making it a topological
A
-module. Similarly,
F_{j}U(L_{2})
has a unique complete topology making it a topological
B
-module for every
j\geq 0
. From now on, we will regard the filtration terms
F_{i}U(L_{1})
and
F_{j}U(L_{2})
as topological modules with respect to this topology.

Fix some non-negative integers
n,m\geq 0
. The
\mathcal{A}
-module
F_{i}U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1})
is an open lattice of
F_{i}U(L_{1})
. Let
q_{i,n}
be its associated gauge norm (cf.
[
41
, Chapter I.2]
). We may then regard
F_{i}U(L_{1})
, together with the gauge
q_{i,n}
, as a complete, normed
A
-module. Analogously, we regard

F_{j}U(L_{2})
as a normed
B
-module with respect to the gauge
q_{j,m}
induced by
F_{j}U(\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2})
. By Corollary
5.1.11
, for each
s\geq 0
we have the following identity of
A\otimes_{K}B
-modules:

F_{s}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{s}F_{i}U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}F_{s-i}U(L_{2}).

Consider the following
\mathbb{R}
-valued function:

q_{s,n,m}(\oplus_{i=0}^{s}a_{i})=\operatorname{max}\{q_{i,n}\otimes q_{s-i,m}(a_{i})\textnormal{ : }0\leq i\leq s\}.

A quick calculation shows that
q_{s,n,m}
is a
K
-norm on
F_{s}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)
, making it a normed
A\otimes_{K}B
-module. Furthermore, the norm on
q_{s,n,m}
is the gauge of the following lattice:

F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{s}F_{i}U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}F_{s-i}U(\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}).

Let
\widehat{F}_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
be the
\pi
-adic completion of
F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
, and let :

\widehat{F}_{s}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right),

be the completion of
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
with respect to
q_{s,n,m}
. We have the following identity of Banach
K
-vector spaces:

\widehat{F}_{s}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)=\widehat{F}_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}K.

For each
s\geq 0
, we have inclusions
F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\subset F_{s+1}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
. Hence, we get the following inductive limit of complete
\mathcal{R}
-modules:

\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\widehat{F}_{1}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\widehat{F}_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow\cdots,

We can now make the following definition:

Definition 5.2.4
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
. We define:

\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}=\varinjlim\widehat{F}_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}.

We regard
\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
as a topological
\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
-module with respect to the colimit topology.

We will now show some basic properties of
\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
:

Proposition 5.2.5
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
. The following hold:

(i)

The map
\widehat{F}_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow\widehat{F}_{s+1}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is a homeomorphism onto its image.

(ii)

\widehat{F}_{1}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}=\left(\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\right)\oplus\left(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}\right)
.

(iii)

The map
V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is injective and has dense image.

Proof.

Claim
(i)
follows by the fact that
F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
and
F_{s+1}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
are finite-free
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
-modules, and
\pi
-adic completion commutes with finite direct sums. For
(ii)
, notice that by the PBW Theorem we have:

F_{1}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}.

Again, using the fact that
\pi
-adic completion commutes with finite direct sums yields the result.
For statement
(iii)
, it is enough to show that the map
F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow\widehat{F}_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is injective and has dense image for each
s\geq 0
. As this map is simply the map into the
\pi
-adic completion, it is enough to show that the
\pi
-adic topology on
F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is separated. The
\pi
-adic topology on
F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
agrees with the subspace topology with respect to
F_{s}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)
. As this is the direct sum of finitely many tensor products of two Banach spaces, it is separated. Hence, the
\pi
-adic topology on
F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is separated.
∎

We may use this lemma to add some extra structure to
\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
:

Proposition 5.2.6
.

The following hold:

(i)

There is a unique continuous
\mathcal{R}
-algebra structure on
\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
such that:

V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m},

is a
\mathcal{R}
-algebra homomorphism.

(ii)

\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is generated as an algebra by
\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
,
\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\otimes 1
, and
1\otimes\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}
.

(iii)

The filtration
F_{\bullet}\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
given by:

F_{s}\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}:=\widehat{F}_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m},\textnormal{ for }s\geq 0,

is a positive and exhaustive
\mathcal{R}
-algebra filtration on
\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
.

Proof.

Choose non-negative integers
r,s\geq 0
. As
V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is a filtered algebra, multiplication fits into the following diagram:

F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\times F_{r}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow F_{s+r}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}.

As multiplication is
\mathcal{R}
-linear, we may apply
\pi
-adic completion to this map. Taking colimits, we get a map
\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\times\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
. This is a
\mathcal{R}
-linear multiplication map extending the product on
V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
. As the product is continuous and
V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is dense in
\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
, it follows that
\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is a
\mathcal{R}
-algebra. Furthermore, by construction of the product,
F_{\bullet}\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
is a positive and exhaustive
\mathcal{R}
-algebra filtration. This shows
(i)
and
(iii)
.

We will now show claim
(ii)
. Choose any
x\in\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
. There is a minimal
s\geq 0
such that
x\in F_{s}\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
. By definition of
\pi
-adic completion, there is a sequence
(z_{r})_{r\geq 0}\subset F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
such that we have
x=\sum_{r\geq 0}\pi^{r}z_{r}
. Let
x_{1},\cdots x_{t}
be a basis of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
as an
\mathcal{A}
-module, and let
y_{1},\cdots,y_{l}
be a basis of
\mathcal{L}_{2}
as a
\mathcal{B}
-module. For any multi-index
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{t+l})\in\mathbb{N}^{t+l}
, we define
|\lambda|=\sum_{i=1}^{l+t}\lambda_{i}
. We will write:

(\pi^{n}x\otimes\pi^{m}y)^{\lambda}=(\pi^{n}x_{1})^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots(\pi^{n}x_{t})^{\lambda_{t}}\otimes(\pi^{m}y_{1})^{\lambda_{t+1}}\cdots(\pi^{m}y_{l})^{\lambda_{t+l}}.

For simplicity, we will assume
n=m=1
, the general case is analogous. By the PBW Theorem, we may express each of the
z_{r}
in the following way:

z_{r}=\sum_{|\lambda|\leq s}a^{r}_{\lambda}(x\otimes y)^{\lambda},

where all
a^{r}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
. Furthermore, this expression is unique. We have the following identities:

x=\sum_{r\geq 0}\pi^{r}z_{r}=\sum_{r\geq 0}\pi^{r}\left(\sum_{|\lambda|\leq s}a^{r}_{\lambda}(x\otimes y)^{\lambda}\right)\\
=\sum_{|\lambda|\leq s}\left(\sum_{r\geq 0}\pi^{r}a^{r}_{\lambda}\right)(x\otimes y)^{\lambda}=\sum_{|\lambda|\leq s}c_{\lambda}(x\otimes y)^{\lambda},

where the second identity follows by the fact that there are only finitely many different multi-indices
\lambda
such that
|\lambda|\leq s
. As
\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
is
\pi
-adically complete, it follows that it contains each of the
c_{\lambda}
. The expression on the right hand side is an element in the subalgebra of
\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
generated by
\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}
,
\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}
, and
\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}
, as we wanted.
∎

Corollary 5.2.7
.

Consider the topological
K
-algebra:

\tilde{V}(L_{1},L_{2}):=\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}K.

The following hold:

(i)

The canonical map
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\rightarrow\tilde{V}(L_{1},L_{2})
is a map of
K
-algebras with dense image.

(ii)

The map
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\rightarrow U(L_{1}\times L_{2})
extends to an isomorphism of filtered
K
-algebras:

\tilde{V}(L_{1},L_{2})\rightarrow U(L_{1}\times L_{2}).

In particular, we have
U(L_{1}\times L_{2})=\varinjlim\widehat{F}_{s}\left(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\right)
.

Proof.

Statement
(i)
is clear. For statement
(ii)
, recall that for each
n,m\geq 0
, the product
L_{1}\times L_{2}
admits the following smooth
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C})
-Lie lattice:

\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}=\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}.

The map of filtered
K
-algebras
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})\rightarrow U(L_{1}\times L_{2})

maps
\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}
to
\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}
. Hence, it induces a map of filtered
\mathcal{R}
-algebras:

\psi:V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}).

For each
s\geq 0
, the
\mathcal{C}
-module
F_{s}U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2})
is finite. In particular, it is
\pi
-adically complete. Thus, taking
\pi
-adic completions, we get an extension
\widehat{F}_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow F_{s}U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2})
. Notice that this is in fact an isomorphism. Indeed, the PBW theorems
5.2.3

and
[
39
, Theorem 3.1]
, give explicit basis as
\mathcal{C}
-modules of both modules, and these get identified by the map. Taking colimits, we get an isomorphism of filtered
\mathcal{C}
-modules:

\widehat{\psi}:\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2}),

which extends the map
V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2})
. Thus, we only need to show that

\widehat{\psi}
is a morphism of algebras. However, as this is a morphism of filtered
\mathcal{C}
-modules, it suffices to show that for each
s\geq 0
we have a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{s}\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\times F_{r}\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{r+s}\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s}U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2})\times F_{r}U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{r+s}U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}\times\widehat{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

This follows by the fact that the maps
F_{s}V(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}\rightarrow F_{s}\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}
have dense image.
∎

Corollary 5.2.8
.

For each
n,m\geq 0
there is a canonical isomorphism of filtered
\mathcal{R}
-algebras:

U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1}\times\pi^{m}\mathcal{L}_{2})=\tilde{V}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})_{n,m}=\varinjlim_{s\geq 0}\widehat{F}_{s}\left(U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}U(\mathcal{L}_{2})\right).

Proof.

This follows by the proof of Corollary
5.2.7
.
∎

Notice that the previous Corollary endows
U(L_{1}\times L_{2})
with a locally convex topology. This topology is in fact the finest locally convex topology of
U(L_{1}\times L_{2})
as a
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
-module. In particular, as
U(L_{1}\times L_{2})
is an inductive limit of finite
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
-modules. Hence,
given a topological
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
-module
M
, it follows that every
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
-linear map
U(L_{1}\times L_{2})\rightarrow M
is continuous. Thus, the topology on
U(L_{1}\times L_{2})
is independent of the choice of Lie lattices.

5.3.
Fréchet Completions

Let
X
and
Y
be smooth and separated rigid varieties with Lie algebroids
\mathscr{L}_{X}
and
\mathscr{L}_{Y}
. In this section we will show that there is a canonical isomorphism of sheaves of complete bornological spaces:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y})\simeq\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})\overrightarrow{\boxtimes}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{Y})\\
=\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{p_{X}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\left(p_{X}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{Y}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{Y})\right).

We start by recalling the following well-known result:

Theorem 5.3.1

(
[
10
, Corollary 9.4.2]
)
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a flat morphism of quasi-compact quasi-separated rigid spaces. Then
f
sends admissible open subspaces to admissible open subspaces, and admissible covers to admissible covers.

We will most of the times apply this theorem to the projections
p_{X}:X\times Y\rightarrow X
, and

p_{Y}:X\times Y\rightarrow Y
, which are flat morphisms of quasi-separated rigid spaces. A first application of this theorem is the following lemma:

Lemma 5.3.2
.

The sheaves
p_{X}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})
and
p_{Y}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{Y})
are sheaves of complete bornological
K
-algebras on
X\times Y
.

Proof.

As the pullback of sheaves is strict symmetric monoidal with respect to
\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}
, it is clear that
p_{X}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})
and
p_{Y}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{Y})
are sheaves of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras on
X\times Y
. Thus, we need to see that they take values on complete bornological spaces. We only do this for
p_{X}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})
, the other case being analogous. By assumption,
X
and
Y
are separated. In particular, the intersection of two affinoid spaces in
X\times Y
is still affinoid. This, fact together with the fact that the dissection functor:

\operatorname{diss}:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}),

commutes with inverse limits, implies that it suffices to show that the sections of
p_{X}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})
on small enough affinoid spaces are complete bornological spaces.

Thus, we may assume that
X
and
Y
are affinoid spaces. As
X\times Y
is quasi-separated, we can define the following site: we let
(X\times Y)_{w}
be the site with objects the quasi-compact subspaces of
X\times Y
and covers being the finite covers. As every admissible cover of
X\times Y
can be refined by such a cover, there is an equivalence of quasi-abelian categories:

(29)

\operatorname{Shv}((X\times Y)_{w},\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X\times Y,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})).

In particular, it is enough to study the restriction of
p_{X}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})
to
(X\times Y)_{w}
.

Notice that the projection
p_{X}:X\times Y\rightarrow Y
is a flat morphism of quasi-separated rigid spaces, and that every object in
(X\times Y)_{w}
is quasi-compact. In particular, it follows by Theorem
5.3.1
that the pullback of presheaves:

p^{-1}_{X,\operatorname{pre}}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})(V)\mapsto\varinjlim_{p_{X}(V)\subset U}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})(U)=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})(p_{X}(V)),\textnormal{ where }V\in(X\times Y)_{w}.

is already a sheaf of Ind-Banach spaces on
(X\times Y)_{w}
. By construction,
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})(p_{X}(V))
is a complete bornological space (in fact, it is a Fréchet space). Hence,
p_{x}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})(V)
is a complete bornological space, as we wanted to show.
∎

Recall from Theorem
5.1.18
that there is a canonical morphism of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules:

j_{X}:\mathscr{L}_{X}\rightarrow p_{X,*}\left(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}\right).

As
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}
is a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}
-module, we may regard it as a sheaf of complete bornological
\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}
-modules. This implies that
p_{X,*}\left(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}\right)
is a sheaf of complete bornological
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. Furthermore, it is clear that the map
j_{X}
is bounded. In particular, it is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear morphism in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. Hence, applying the pushforward-pullback adjunction for sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces, we have an
p_{X}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear map of sheaves of Ind-Banach
K
-Lie algebras:

i_{X}:p_{X}^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}.

This map satisfies the following property:

Lemma 5.3.3
.

The map
i_{X}:p_{X}^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}
extends uniquely to a morphism of sheaves of complete bornological
K
-algebras:

p_{X}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})\rightarrow\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}).

Proof.

We can freely assume that
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
, and
Y=\operatorname{Sp}(B)
are affinoid spaces, and that
\mathscr{L}_{X}
and
\mathscr{L}_{Y}
are free. Choose affine formal models
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, and free Lie lattices
\mathcal{L}_{X}
,
\mathcal{L}_{Y}
of
\mathscr{L}_{X}(X)
and
\mathscr{L}_{Y}(Y)
.

By the pushforward-pullback adjunction, it is enough to show that:

i_{X}:\mathscr{L}_{X}\rightarrow p_{X,*}\left(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}\right),

extends uniquely to a morphism of sheaves of complete Ind-Banach
K
-algebras:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})\rightarrow p_{X*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}).

Let
U\subset X
be an affinoid space. Then
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}(U))
and
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(U\times Y))
are complete bornological
K
-algebras. Furthermore, by
[
9
, Corollary 5.19]
, it follows that
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}(U))
is the completion of
U(\mathscr{L}_{X}(U))
, and
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(U\times Y))
is the completion of
U(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(U\times Y))
(with respect to the subspace bornologies). In particular, if the extension exists, it is unique. By the same token, it suffices to show that the canonical map:

U(i_{X}):U(\mathscr{L}_{X}(U))\rightarrow U(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(U\times Y)),

is bounded. We show this for
U=X
, the general case is analogous.

A subset
B\subset U(\mathscr{L}_{X}(X))
is bounded if and only if for every
n\geq 0
there is some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that:

B\subset\pi^{m}U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{X}(X)).

The bounded subsets of
U(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(X\times Y))
have an analogous description in terms of the lattices
\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{X}\times\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{Y}
. By construction
U(i_{X})(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{X})\subset\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{X}\times\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}_{Y}
. Thus,
U(i_{X})
maps bounded subsets to bounded subsets, as wanted. For general
X,Y
, we use the fact that we have an admissible cover of
X\times Y
by products of affinoid spaces satisfying the above.
∎

Let us introduce the following useful space:

Definition 5.3.4

(
[
9
, Section 5.5]
)
.

Let
s\geq 0
be an integer and
K\langle x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}\rangle
be the (bornologification of) the Tate algebra on
s
variables. We define the following complete bornological space:

K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}\}:=\varprojlim_{n}K\langle\pi^{n}x_{1},\cdots,\pi^{n}x_{s}\rangle.

Notice that
K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}\}
is the bornologification of a nuclear Fréchet space. Furthermore, the space
K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}\}
has a natural structure as complete bornological
K
-algebra. Namely, it is the space of holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{A}^{s}_{K}
. However, this algebra structure will rarely be of use, so we just regard
K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}\}
as a complete bornological
K
-vector space. Let us start with the following lemmas:

Lemma 5.3.5
.

Let
X
be a smooth rigid space with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
. Assume there are sections
s_{1},\dots,s_{r}\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{L})
inducing an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules:

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}.

Then there is an isomorphism of sheaves of complete bornological
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules:

\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}\}\rightarrow\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),

which is the identity on
\mathcal{O}_{X}
and sends
x_{i}
to
s_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
.

Proof.

This is shown in
[
9
,
Lemma 6.5]
.
∎

Lemma 5.3.6
.

Let
X
be a smooth rigid space with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
. Then
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
is a strongly flat Ind-Banach
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module.

Proof.

As flatness is a local property and
\mathscr{L}
is a Lie algebroid, we can assume that the conditions of Lemma
5.3.5
hold. In particular, we have:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}\},

as sheaves of complete bornological
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. Thus, it suffices to show that
K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}\}
is strongly flat in
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. This is shown in
[
9
, Section 5.5]
.
∎

Proposition 5.3.7
.

The morphism of sheaves of complete bornological
K
-algebras:

p_{X}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{Y}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{Y})\rightarrow\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}),

extends to an isomorphism of sheaves of complete bornological
\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}
-modules:

(30)

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})\overrightarrow{\boxtimes}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{Y})\rightarrow\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}),

which is the identity on
\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}
. In particular, for each pair of affinoid open subspaces
U\subset X
,
V\subset Y
such that
\mathscr{L}_{X|U}
and
\mathscr{L}_{Y|V}
are free, we have:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y})(U\times V)=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}(U))\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{Y}(V)).

Proof.

First, notice that by Lemma
5.3.5
the sheaf
p_{X}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{Y}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{Y})
is strongly flat over
p_{X}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. Thus, the map in equation (
30
) is a well-defined morphism of Ind-Banach
\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}
-modules, the existence of which is a consequence of Lemma
5.3.3
. We need to see it is an isomorphism. As this can be done locally, we may assume that
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
,
Y=\operatorname{Sp}(B)
, and that
\mathscr{L}_{X}
, and
\mathscr{L}_{Y}
are free, of ranks
r
and
s
respectively. As the conditions of Lemma
5.3.5
hold, we have isomorphisms of complete bornological
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules (resp.
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules):

(31)

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}\},\textnormal{ and }\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{Y})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{Y}\overrightarrow{\otimes}K\{x_{r+1},\cdots,x_{r+s}\},

induced by choosing basis
x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}
, and
x_{r+1},\cdots,x_{r+s}
of
\mathscr{L}_{X}(X)
and
\mathscr{L}_{Y}(Y)
respectively. Using the fact that the pullback of functors is strong symmetric monoidal, we have the following identities:

p_{X}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{Y}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{Y})\simeq\left(p_{X}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\right)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r+s}\}.

Similarly, the construction of
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}
shows that
x_{1},\cdots,x_{r+s}
are a basis of
\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y}(X\times Y)
, so that we also have:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{L}_{Y})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r+s}\},

and it follows that (
30
) is an isomorphism.

For the second statement, notice that the tensor product of two sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
is the sheafification of the presheaf:

W\mapsto\mathcal{F}(W)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{G}(W).

As shown in
[
9
, Section 5.5]
, the functor
-\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r+s}\}
is strongly exact. Thus, the restriction of the presheaf:

W\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}(W)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r+s}\},

to the site
(X\times Y)_{w}
(cf. the proof of Lemma
5.3.2
) is already a sheaf. As we have
\mathcal{O}_{X\times Y}(X\times Y)=A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B
, the result follows by the isomorphisms above.
∎

Remark 5.3.8
.

Notice that the isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}_{X})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}\},

is an isomorphism of sheaves of
K
-algebras if and only if
\mathscr{L}_{X}
has trivial bracket.

Corollary 5.3.9
.

Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A),Y=\operatorname{Sp}(B)
be smooth affinoid spaces with finite-free Lie algebroids
L_{X}
,
L_{Y}
. Then the Ind-Banach
K
-algebra:

\wideparen{U}(L_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(L_{Y}),

is a two-sided Fréchet-Stein algebra.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.3.7
, we have an identification of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras:

\wideparen{U}(L_{X})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(L_{Y})=\wideparen{U}(L_{X}\times L_{Y}).

Hence, it suffices to show the result for
\wideparen{U}(L_{X}\times L_{Y})
. This is
[
3
, Theorem 6.7]
.
∎

5.4.
Sheaves of complete bi-enveloping algebras

For the rest of this section, we fix a smooth and separated rigid analytic space
X
with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
.
In order to study the Hochschild (co)-homology of
\mathscr{L}
, we need to find a convenient way to study the category of modules over the enveloping algebra of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}):

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}:=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}.

To this effect, we introduce the sheaf of complete bornological
K
-algebras
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
:

Definition 5.4.1
.

Consider the following sheaf of complete bornological spaces:

\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}):=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\boxtimes}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}=\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}}\left(p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}\right).

We call
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
the sheaf of complete bi-enveloping algebras of
\mathscr{L}
. If
\mathscr{L}=\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
, we will write
\wideparen{E}_{X}:=\wideparen{E}(\mathcal{T}_{X/K})
, and call
\wideparen{E}_{X}
the sheaf of (complete) bi-enveloping algebras of
X
.

Notice that it is not a priory clear that

\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
is a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras. Indeed, as
p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is not central in

p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}
, it is not immediate that the algebra structure extends uniquely to the completed tensor product defining
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
. In order to overcome this difficulty, we will use some special features of the enveloping algebras of Lie-Rinehart algebras to define the product on
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
locally, and then we will show that these uniquely defined products glue to a global Ind-Banach algebra structure.

Let us paint the local picture first: Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
be a smooth affinoid space with a free Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
. Let
\mathfrak{X}=\operatorname{Spf}(\mathcal{A})
be an affine formal model, and let
\mathcal{L}
be a free
\mathcal{A}
-Lie lattice of
L=\mathscr{L}(X)
. Choose a basis of
\mathcal{L}
as an
\mathcal{A}
-module
x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}
. We then have the following proposition:

Proposition 5.4.2
.

By the PBW theorem, every element in
U(L_{2})
admits a unique expression
\beta=\sum f_{(r_{1},\cdots,r_{d})}x_{1}^{r_{1}}\cdots x_{d}^{r_{d}}
. Consider the following expression:

(32)

\beta^{t}=\sum(-1)^{\sum r_{i}}x_{1}^{r_{1}}\cdots x_{d}^{r_{d}}f_{r}.

Then the following statements hold:

(i)

(\beta^{t})^{t}=1
.

(ii)

The map
\beta\mapsto\beta^{t}
induces isomorphisms of filtered
K
-algebras:

T:U(L_{2})\rightarrow U(L_{2})^{\operatorname{op}},\quad T^{-1}:U(L_{2})^{\operatorname{op}}\rightarrow U(L_{2}).

Proof.

This is done in
[
22
, Section 1.2]
.
∎

By construction of
T
, it follows that for each
n\geq 0
,
T
induces an isomorphism of filtered
\mathcal{R}
-algebras:

T_{n}:U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})\rightarrow U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})^{\operatorname{op}}.

Therefore, the following proposition is straightforward:

Proposition 5.4.3
.

The map
T:U(L)\rightarrow U(L)^{\operatorname{op}}
satisfies the following:

(i)

T
extends uniquely to an isomorphism of Banach
K
-algebras:

\widehat{T}_{n}:\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K}\rightarrow\widehat{U}(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K}^{\operatorname{op}},\textnormal{ for each }n\geq 0.

(ii)

T
extends uniquely to an isomorphism of Fréchet-Stein algebras:

\wideparen{T}:\wideparen{U}(L)\rightarrow\wideparen{U}(L)^{\operatorname{op}}.

The map
T^{-1}:U(L)^{\operatorname{op}}\rightarrow U(L)
satisfies analogous properties.

The fact that
\mathscr{L}
is globally free has the following consequence:

Corollary 5.4.4
.

There are mutually inverse isomorphisms of sheaves of complete bornological
K
-algebras on
X
:

\wideparen{T}:\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\leftrightarrows\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}:\wideparen{T}^{-1}.

Which are defined on affinoid spaces
U\subset X
by
\wideparen{T}:\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(U))\rightarrow\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(U))^{\operatorname{op}}
.

Proof.

The choice of a basis of
\mathcal{L}
induces an isomorphism of sheaves of complete bornological
K
-vector spaces
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}\}
. Using this identification, it follows that for any pair of affinoid subdomains
W\subset V\subset X
, the restriction maps
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(V)\rightarrow\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(W)
are identified with the image under the dissection functor of the maps:

\mathcal{O}_{X}(V)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}\}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(W)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}\}.

,
In particular, the restriction maps are the identity on the
K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}\}
factor, and then it is clear that
\wideparen{T}
commutes with the restriction maps. Hence, it defines a map of sheaves, which is an isomorphism by Proposition
5.4.3
.
∎

Definition 5.4.5
.

We endow
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
with the unique product satisfying that the morphism of sheaves on Ind-Banach spaces:

\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}:=\operatorname{Id}\otimes\left(\operatorname{Id}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{T}\right):\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})\rightarrow\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}),

is an isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras.

Notice that, under the current definition, it may seem that the Ind-Banach algebra structure on
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
depends on the choice of a basis of global sections of
\mathscr{L}
. Indeed, by construction,
\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}
is obtained via (the pullback of) the following composition:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}\}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}\}\simeq\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}},

where the first and third maps are induced by the choice of a global basis for
\mathscr{L}
, and the middle map is obtained from Propositions
5.4.2
and
5.4.3
.

Lemma 5.4.6
.

Let
x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}
and
y_{1},\cdots,y_{d}
be two basis of global sections of
\mathscr{L}
. The base-change induces a unique commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}\})^{\operatorname{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{y_{1},\cdots,y_{d}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{y_{1},\cdots,y_{d}\})^{\operatorname{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wideparen{T}}

where all maps are isomorphisms of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras. In particular, the isomorphism class of
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
is independent of the choice of a basis for
\mathscr{L}
.

Proof.

The leftmost vertical maps are defined via the compositions:

\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}\}\rightarrow\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{y_{1},\cdots,y_{d}\},

where the first map is the one obtained in Lemma
5.3.5
, and the second one is the inverse. The vertical map on the right hand side is obtained by applying
(-)^{\operatorname{op}}
to this map. In order to see that the diagram commutes, it is enough to test if on the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}
and the elements of the basis
x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}
. This is a straightforward calculation. For the second part, we apply the functor
\operatorname{Id}\otimes\left(\operatorname{Id}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}(-)\right)
to obtain a commutative diagram of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces:

(33)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})}}

where every morphism is an isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces. Notice that the leftmost vertical map is an isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras. Thus, the rightmost vertical map is an isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras if the two horizontal maps are.
∎

Back to the general setting, we let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic space with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
. The previous construction shows that, at least locally,
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
can be given the structure of a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras extending the products on
p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}
. The idea now is showing that these structures can be combined into a global structure:

Theorem 5.4.7
.

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic space with a lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
, and let
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
be the sheaf of complete bi-enveloping algebras of
\mathscr{L}
. Then
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
admits a unique structure as a sheaf of complete bornological algebras satisfying the following properties:

(i)

The canonical maps:

p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}\rightarrow\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}),\quad\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\rightarrow\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}),

are morphisms of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras.

(ii)

Let
U\subset X
be an affinoid subdomain satisfying that
\mathscr{L}_{|U}
is globally free. A choice of a basis of global sections
s_{1},\cdots,s_{d}\in\Gamma(U,\mathscr{L})
induces a unique isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces on
X^{2}
:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})_{|U\times U}=\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})_{|U\times U}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{U\times U}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{2d}\}.

Then the canonical morphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces:

\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}:\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})_{|U\times U}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{U\times U}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{2d}\}\\
\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{U\times U}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{2d}\}\simeq\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})_{|U\times U},

where the middle map is defined as in Definition
5.4.6
is an isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras.

Proof.

Uniqueness is clear. Thus, we only need to show that such a structure exists. As
\mathscr{L}
is a Lie algebroid, we can choose an admissible affinoid cover
\mathfrak{U}
of
X
satisfying that for each
U\in\mathfrak{U}
the restriction
\mathscr{L}_{|U}
admits a basis of global sections. Choose affinoid subdomains
U,V,W\in\mathfrak{U}
. A seen before, any choice of a basis of global sections of
\mathscr{L}_{|U}
induces an isomorphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces:

\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}_{U}:\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})_{|U\times U}\rightarrow\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})_{|U\times U},

endowing
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})_{|U\times U}
with a unique product. Choose a basis
(s_{1}^{U},\cdots,s_{d}^{U})
of
\mathscr{L}_{|U}
, and let
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})_{|U\times U}^{U}
be the corresponding sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras. Additionally, we let
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})_{|V\times V}^{V}
, and
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})_{|W\times W}^{W}
be the analogous algebras obtained from choices of basis of
\mathscr{L}
over
V
and
W
, which we denote by
(s_{1}^{V},\cdots,s_{d}^{V})
and
(s_{1}^{W},\cdots,s_{d}^{W})
respectively. It suffices to see that these products glue to a global Ind-Banach algebra structure on
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
. That is, we need to find isomorphisms of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras:

\rho_{U,V}:\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})^{U}_{|(U\cap V)\times(U\cap V)}\rightarrow\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})^{V}_{|(U\cap V)\times(U\cap V)},\\
\rho_{V,W}:\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})^{V}_{|(V\cap W)\times(V\cap W)}\rightarrow\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})^{W}_{|(V\cap W)\times(V\cap W)},

and show that they satisfy the cocycle condition. In order to construct this map, we look at the diagram (
33
) and notice that, by construction of the product of Lie algebroids, the choice of the basis
(s_{1}^{U},\cdots,s_{d}^{U})
induces a unique basis of
\mathscr{L}^{2}_{|U\times U}
. Thus, we can define the following map:

\rho_{U,V}:\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})^{U}_{|(U\cap V)\times(U\cap V)}\xrightarrow{\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}_{U}^{-1}}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})_{|(U\cap V)\times(U\cap V)}\\
\xrightarrow{\tau_{U,V}}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})_{|(U\cap V)\times(U\cap V)}\xrightarrow{\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}_{V}}\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})^{V}_{|(U\cap V)\times(U\cap V)},

where
\tau_{U,V}
is the unique automorphism of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})_{|(U\cap V)\times(U\cap V)}
induced by the base change in
\mathscr{L}^{2}_{|(U\cap V)\times(U\cap V)}
from the basis induced by
(s_{1}^{U},\cdots,s_{d}^{U})
to the basis induced by
(s_{1}^{V},\cdots,s_{d}^{V})
. It follows that the
\rho_{U,V}
satisfy the cocycle condition if and only if the
\tau_{U,V}
do, and this is clear since
\mathscr{L}^{2}
is a coherent module in
X^{2}
.
∎

From now on, we will always regard
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
as a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras with respect to this product. As can be seen from the definition, the algebras
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
and
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
are closely related. This relation will be further explored in future sections, where we will study the category of Ind-Banach

\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
-modules via comparing it with the category of Ind-Banach
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
-modules. However, before doing that, let us first motivate the above definition by showcasing
an important feature of
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
:

Lemma 5.4.8
.

Let
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}})_{\Delta}
denote the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}})
given by the modules supported on the diagonal. There is an equivalence of quasi-abelian categories:

\Delta_{*}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}})_{\Delta}:\Delta^{-1}.

Proof.

There is an isomorphism of sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}=\Delta^{-1}\left(p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}\right).

Thus, this is a special case of
3.5.2
.
∎

Proposition 5.4.9
.

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic space with a lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
. There is a strongly right exact functor:

\displaystyle\Delta^{E}_{*}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\mapsto\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}}\Delta_{*}(\mathcal{M})

Analogously, we also have a right exact functor between the left hearts:

\Delta^{I(E)}_{*}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(I(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}))^{e})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(I(\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}))),

and this functor admits a left derived functor:

\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{E}:=\mathbb{L}(\Delta^{I(E)}_{*})\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}))^{e})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}_{X}).

Proof.

The existence of the functors, together with their strong right exactness is a consequence of Lemma
5.4.8
, together with the fact that tensor products have a right adjoint, and therefore preserve arbitrary cokernels. In order to see that
\Delta^{I(E)}_{*}
admits a left derived functor, we again invoke Lemma
5.4.8
to reduce the claim to showing that the extension of scalars:

\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(p_{1}^{-1}I(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}))\tilde{\otimes}_{I(K)}p_{2}^{-1}I(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}))^{\operatorname{op}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}(I(\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}))),

admits a left derived functor. However, this follows by the existence of flat resolutions for modules, as seen in Proposition
3.3.2
.
∎

As in previous instances, tensor products do not (generally) behave nicely with respect to the passage the left heart. Namely, given
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})
we generally do
not
have:

I(\Delta_{*}^{E}(M))=\Delta_{*}^{I(E)}(I(\mathcal{M})).

However, we will later identify a full subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})
in which this identity always holds.

5.5.
Definition of Hochschild (co)-homology

Again, we fix a smooth and separated rigid analytic space
X
with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
. Building on the material developed in previous sections, we are finally ready to give our first formal definition of Hochschild (co)-homology for Ind-Banach
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
-bimodules:

Definition 5.5.1
.

For any
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})
we define the following objects:

(i)

The inner Hochschild cohomology complex of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
is the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}):=\Delta^{-1}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{E}_{X}}(\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),

and we call
\mathcal{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}):=\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})\right)
the
n
-th Hochschild cohomology sheaf of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
.

(ii)

The Hochschild cohomology complex of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
is the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})).

As before, we call
\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{H}^{n}\left(\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})\right)
the
n
-th Hochschild cohomology space (group) of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
.

Additionally, if
\mathscr{A}
is an Ind-Banach algebra and
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
, we define:

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=R\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),

and call this the Hochschild cohomology complex of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
.

Notice that for any
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})
, the
n
-th Hochschild cohomology sheaf
\mathcal{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M})
is (generally) not an object in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
, but rather an object in the left heart
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))
. The analogous phenomena also holds for the Hochschild cohomology groups.

Ideally, we would like to show that inner Hochschild cohomology
\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),-)
can be expressed as a derived inner homomorphism functor in the derived category of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}
-modules. Namely, we would like to show that for any
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}))
there is an identification:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}).

Unfortunately, this does not seem to be correct. Furthermore, there is no clear way of comparing these two functors. A more sensible approach would be replacing
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
by
\Delta^{-1}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
in the right hand side. In this case, it can be shown that there is a natural transformation:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),-)\rightarrow R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\Delta^{-1}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}(-)),

but we are unable to determine if this is always a natural transformation.

Let us now paint the homological side of the picture. We start with the following observation: Let
\mathscr{A}
be a sheaf of Ind-Banach algebras on
X
. We remark that
\mathscr{A}
also has a canonical structure as a right
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module. Indeed, a right
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module is a left module over
(\mathscr{A}^{e})^{\operatorname{op}}=\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathscr{A}
. This is precisely the enveloping algebra of
\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}
. Hence,
\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}
has a canonical structure as a right
\mathscr{A}^{e}
-module. We define the structure on
\mathscr{A}
via the identity
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}
as objects in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
. We may generalize this as follows:

Lemma 5.5.2
.

\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
is canonically a complex of right
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
-modules.

Proof.

Let
A=p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}
for simplicity. By the discussion above, we know that
\Delta_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
is a left module over
A^{\operatorname{op}}
, so we have a complex of right
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
-modules:

\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{A^{\operatorname{op}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\Delta_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})=(\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}}A)^{\operatorname{op}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{A^{\operatorname{op}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\Delta_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\\
=\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Delta_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})=\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),

and this is precisely what we wanted to show.
∎

Thus, we are free to regard
\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
as a right Ind-Banach
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
-module, and we will do so without further notice. We now have the following definition:

Definition 5.5.3
.

For any
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})
we define the following objects:

(i)

The inner Hochschild homology complex of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
is the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}):=\Delta^{-1}\left(\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\right),

and we call
\mathcal{HH}_{n}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}):=\operatorname{H}^{-n}\left(\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})\right)
the
n
-th Hochschild homology sheaf of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
.

(ii)

The Hochschild homology complex of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
is the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))
:

\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})).

As before, we call
\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{H}^{-n}\left(\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})\right)
the
n
-th Hochschild homology space (group) of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
.

Additionally, if
\mathscr{A}
is an Ind-Banach algebra and
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}(\mathscr{A}^{e})
, we define:

\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\mathscr{A}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}^{e}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}

and call this the Hochschild homology complex of
\mathscr{A}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
.

Unlike in the cohomological case, we can show the following proposition:

Proposition 5.5.4
.

There are canonical natural equivalences of functors:

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),-)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}}\left(\Delta^{-1}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}(-)\right)\\
\xrightarrow{\simeq}\left(\Delta^{-1}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\right)\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}}-.

Moreover, we also have an equivalence
\Delta^{-1}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}(-)=\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\mathbb{L}}(-)
.

Proof.

In order to simplify notation, let
A=p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{\operatorname{op}}
. Choose any
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})
. We have the following chain of identities:

\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}):=

\displaystyle\Delta^{-1}\left(\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{-1}\left(\left(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})\right)\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})}\left(\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}\Delta_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{-1}\left(\Delta_{*}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}\Delta_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}\Delta^{-1}\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}.

By construction, we have
\Delta^{-1}\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})=\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\mathbb{L}}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}
. Hence, we may rewrite the last complex of sheaves of Ind-Banach spaces as:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}=\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e}}\Delta^{-1}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet},

which is the first identity we wanted to show. The remaining ones are analogous.
∎

6.
Co-admissible modules over the bi-enveloping algebra

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic space with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
. In the previous chapter, we introduced two sheaves of complete bornological algebras,
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
and
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
, and showcased the relevance of the sheaf of bi-enveloping algebras
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
in the study of Hochschild cohomology and homology. This chapter is devoted to bringing
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
into the limelight. By construction,
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
is the completion of the sheaf of enveloping algebras of the Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}^{2}
on
X^{2}
. In particular, its (derived) category of Ind-Banach modules presents great homological behavior, and has been extensively studied in
[
9
]
. The goal of this section is showing that many interesting constructions in
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2}))
, such as sheaves of co-admissible modules, can be translated to the setting of
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
-modules.

In order to do this, we will develop a formalism of side-switching operations for bimodules. In particular, we will obtain a pair of mutually inverse equivalences of quasi-abelian categories:

\operatorname{S}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2}))\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})):\operatorname{S}^{-1},

which we call the side-switching equivalence. As shown above, if
\mathscr{L}
is globally free on
X
, then there is a (non-canonical) isomorphism of sheaves of complete bornological algebras:

\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}:\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})\rightarrow\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}).

In this situation, the previous equivalence can be explicitly obtained via extension of scalars along
\wideparen{\mathbb{T}}
. In the general case, the description of
\operatorname{S}
is more involved, and will be discussed below.

The fact that
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
and
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
are locally isomorphic has very strong repercussions in the structure of
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
. In particular, it shows that
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
is locally a Fréchet-Stein algebra with
c
-flat restriction maps. Hence, we can define the (abelian) category of co-admissible
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
-modules, which we denote by
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}))
. As one may suspect, the side-switching equivalence restricts to an equivalence of abelian categories:

\operatorname{S}:\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2}))\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})):\operatorname{S}^{-1}.

Even better, we can work in the derived setting to obtain a category of
\mathcal{C}
-complexes of
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
-modules, and the derived version of the side-switching equivalence induces an equivalence of triangulated categories between the corresponding categories of
\mathcal{C}
-complexes over
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
and
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
.

Another upshot of the side-switching equivalence is that we obtain new formulas to calculate the inner Hochschild (co)-homology complex. Namely, we define the immersion functor as the following composition:

\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}:\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})\xrightarrow{\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{E}}\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L}))\xrightarrow{\operatorname{S}^{-1}}\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})).

As a consequence of the derived side-switching equivalence, we obtain the following formula for the inner Hochschild cohomology complex:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\Delta^{-1}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})}(\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}),\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),

where
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})^{e})
. We point out that there are right module versions for all of the constructions above, which lead to analogous results for Hochschild homology.

6.1.
Side-switching operations for bimodules

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic variety. In this section, we adapt the formalism of side-switching operations for Ind-Banach
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules developed in
[
4
, Section 3]
and
[
9
, Section 6.3]
to the context of bimodules. As in previous sections, we will work in greater generality, and study the case of a general Lie algebroid on
X
.

Let us start by recalling the algebraic situation: Let
A
,
B
be two
K
-algebras, with smooth Lie algebroids
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
. We start with the following:

Lemma 6.1.1
.

Assume
L_{2}
is locally free of rank
n
as a
B
-module, and let:

\Omega_{L_{2}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(\bigwedge_{B}^{n}L_{2},B).

Then
\Omega_{L_{2}}
is a right
U(L_{2})
-module, with action given by:

x\alpha=-\lambda_{\alpha}(x),\textnormal{ for }x\in\Omega_{L_{2}},\textnormal{ and }\alpha\in L_{2},

where
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)
denotes the Lie derivative.

Proof.

This is shown in
[
24
, Proposition 2.8]
. For the construction of the Lie derivative, check
[
24
, pp. 10]
and
[
39
, Equation 4.3]
.
∎

The module
\Omega_{L_{2}}
can be used to construct a Morita equivalence between
U(L_{2})
and
U(L_{2})^{\operatorname{op}}
. For convenience of the reader, we now recall the construction from
[
4
, 3.1]
: Let
M
be a left
U(L_{2})
-module, and consider the
B
-module
\Omega_{L_{2}}\otimes_{B}M
. This module has a canonical
U(L_{2})^{\operatorname{op}}
-module structure given by the following expression:

(34)

(d\otimes m)\alpha=(d\alpha)\otimes m-d\otimes(\alpha m),\textnormal{ for }d\in\Omega_{L_{2}},m\in M,\textnormal{ and }\alpha\in L_{2}.

Assume in addition that
M
is a
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
-module. Then
M
has structures as a left
U(L_{1})
-module, and left
U(L_{2})
-module, which satisfy the following compatibility condition:
For
a\in U(L_{1})
,
b\in U(L_{2})
, let
a\cdot:M\rightarrow M
be the
K
-linear endomorphism induced by the action of
a
, and
b\cdot:M\rightarrow M
be the
K
-linear endomorphism induced by the action of
b
. Then

a\cdot:M\rightarrow M
is
U(L_{2})
-linear, and

b\cdot:M\rightarrow M
is
U(L_{1})
-linear.

In this case, for any
x\in U(L_{1})
, the map
x:M\rightarrow M
is
B
-linear. Hence, we get an induced map:

\operatorname{Id}\otimes x:\Omega_{L_{2}}\otimes_{B}M\rightarrow\Omega_{L_{2}}\otimes_{B}M.

In particular,
\Omega_{L_{2}}\otimes_{B}M
is a
U(L_{1})
-module, and for any
\beta\in L_{1},\alpha\in L_{2}
we have:

(35)

(\beta(d\otimes m))\alpha=(d\alpha)\otimes\beta m-d\otimes(\alpha\beta m)=(d\alpha)\otimes\beta m-d\otimes(\beta\alpha m)=\beta((d\otimes m)\alpha).

Thus,
\Omega_{L_{2}}\otimes_{B}M
is a
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})^{\operatorname{op}}
-module. Furthermore, a straightforward calculation shows that this construction is functorial.

Let now
M
be a right
U(L_{2})
-module, and consider the
B
-module:

\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(\Omega_{L_{2}},M)=\Omega_{L_{2}}^{-1}\otimes_{B}M.

Again, this has a canonical structure as a left
U(L_{2})
-module given by:

\alpha\phi(d)=\phi(d\alpha)-\phi(d)\alpha,\textnormal{ for }d\in\Omega_{L_{2}},\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(\Omega_{L_{2}},M),\textnormal{ and }\alpha\in L_{2}.

Assume that
M
is a
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})^{\operatorname{op}}
-module. Arguing as above, it follows that the action of every
x\in U(L_{1})
is
B
-linear. Hence, we have a structure of a left
U(L_{1})
-module on
\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(\Omega_{L_{2}},M)
given by composition. Furthermore, it is clear that this makes
\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(\Omega_{L_{2}},M)
a
U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})
-module, and that this operation is functorial. We may condense this discussion into the following proposition:

Proposition 6.1.2
.

There is an equivalence of abelian categories:

\Omega_{L_{2}}^{-1}\otimes_{B}-:\operatorname{Mod}(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})^{\operatorname{op}})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{Mod}(U(L_{1})\otimes_{K}U(L_{2})):\Omega_{L_{2}}\otimes_{B}-\textnormal{.}

Proof.

This follows by
[
4
, Proposition 3.1]
. One just needs to check that the canonical isomorphisms:

M\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(\Omega_{L_{2}},\Omega_{L_{2}}\otimes_{B}M),\quad\Omega_{L_{2}}\otimes_{B}\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(\Omega_{L_{2}},N)\rightarrow N,

are
U(L_{1})
-linear, and this is clear by the construction of the action.
∎

Following
[
22
]
, we can get a more explicit version of this equivalences whenever
L_{2}
is free as a
B
-module. Indeed, assume that
L_{2}
is a free
B
-module, with basis
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}
, and let
d\alpha_{1},\cdots,d\alpha_{n}
denote the dual basis of the dual vector bundle
\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(L_{2},B)
. In particular, we have:

\Omega_{L_{2}}=Bd\alpha_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\alpha_{n}.

As in Proposition
5.4.2
, there is an isomorphism of algebras
T:U(L_{2})\rightarrow U(L_{2})^{\operatorname{op}}
uniquely determined by the expression:

T(\sum f_{r}\alpha_{1}^{r_{1}}\cdots\alpha_{n}^{r_{n}})=\left(\sum(-1)^{\sum r_{i}}\alpha_{1}^{r_{1}}\cdots\alpha_{d}^{r_{d}}f_{r}\right)^{t}:=\sum(-1)^{\sum r_{i}}\alpha_{1}^{r_{1}}\cdots\alpha_{d}^{r_{d}}f_{r}.

Thus, we get an identification of the categories of left and right
U(L_{2})
-modules given by extension of scalars along the map
T:U(L_{2})\rightarrow U(L_{2})^{\operatorname{op}}
. The idea is comparing this identification with the one obtained above.

It can be shown that the action of
\beta\in U(L_{2})
on an element
fd\alpha_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\alpha_{n}\in\Omega_{L_{2}}
is given by the following formula:

\beta(fd\alpha_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\alpha_{n})=(\beta^{t}f)d\alpha_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\alpha_{n}.

Hence, we arrive at the following proposition:

Proposition 6.1.3
.

Let
M
be an
U(L_{2})
-module. Then we have a functorial isomorphism of
U(L_{2})
-modules:

\Omega_{L_{2}}\otimes_{B}M\rightarrow T^{*}M=U(L_{2})^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes_{U(L_{2})}M.

In particular, there is a natural equivalence of functors
\Omega_{L_{2}}\otimes_{B}-\simeq T^{*}
. Similarly, there is also a natural equivalence
\Omega_{L_{2}}^{-1}\otimes_{B}-\simeq T^{-1,*}
.

Proof.

This is also done in
[
22
, Section 1.2]
.
∎

Let
X
be a separated smooth rigid analytic variety with a Lie algebroid
\mathscr{L}
. The goal of this section is extending the previous discussion to the categories of Ind-Banach modules over
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}^{2})
, and
\wideparen{E}(\mathscr{L})
. Let us start with the following lemmas:

Lemma 6.1.4
.

Let
X,Y
be two quasi-compact, quasi-separated rigid spaces. Then every quasi-compact admissible open
U\subset X\times Y
admits an admissible cover
\left(U_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{n}
satisfying the following:

(i)

Each of the
U_{i}
is quasi-compact and quasi-separated.

(ii)

p_{Y}(U_{i})\subset Y
is an affinoid admissible open subspace.

Furthermore, if
X
and
Y
are affinoid, then the
U_{i}
can be taken to be affinoid.

Proof.

As
X,Y
are quasi-compact and quasi-separated, it follows that
X\times Y
is also quasi-compact and quasi-separated. Hence, by Theorem
5.3.1
, it follows that
p_{Y}(U)\subset Y
is a quasi-compact admissible open. Hence, we get a factorization
U\rightarrow X\times p_{Y}(U)\rightarrow X\times Y
. Notice that all these maps are open immersions of quasi-compact admissible open subspaces. Therefore, as
X\times Y
is quasi-separated, the map
U\rightarrow X\times p_{Y}(U)
is quasi-compact. Let
\left(V_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{n}
be a finite affinoid cover of
p_{Y}(U)
. Then the family
U_{i}=U\cap\left(X\times V_{i}\right)
satisfies our requirements.
∎

Lemma 6.1.5
.

Assume
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
is affinoid and
\mathscr{L}
is free. For every quasi-compact admissible open subspace
U\subset X
there is a pair of mutually inverse equivalences of quasi-abelian categories:

\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{A}-:\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}\left(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)\right)\leftrightarrows\\
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}\left(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}}\right):\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}^{-1}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{A}-.

Proof.

As both
U
and
X
are quasi-compact, it follows that
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)
and
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)
are Fréchet spaces. In particular, they are metrizable. Hence, it follows by
[
9
, Proposition 4.25]
that the Ind-Banach
K
-algebras:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X),\quad\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}},

are the images under the dissection functor
\operatorname{diss}:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
of the following complete bornological
K
-algebras:

\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X),\quad\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}}.

The first step is showing that there are mutually inverse equivalences of quasi-abelian categories:

\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\widehat{\otimes}_{A}-:\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}\left(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)\right)\leftrightarrows\\
\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}\left(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}}\right):\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}^{-1}\widehat{\otimes}_{A}-.

By
[
9
, Corollary 5.19]
, we have that
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L}(X))
is the completion of
U(\mathscr{L}(X))
with respect to the induced bornology. As bornological completion commutes with tensor products,
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)
must be the completion of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\otimes_{K}U(\mathscr{L})(X)
. Thus, we have a canonical equivalence of quasi-abelian categories:

\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\otimes_{K}U(\mathscr{L})(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)).

Similarly,
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}}
is the completion of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\otimes_{K}U(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}}
. Thus, we have an equivalence:

\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\otimes_{K}U(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}}).

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}}(\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\otimes_{K}U(\mathscr{L})(X))
, and regard
\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\otimes_{A}\mathcal{M}
as a bornological space. By Proposition
6.1.2
, this is an abstract
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)\otimes_{K}U(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}}
-module. In order to see that it is a bornological module, it suffices to see that the actions of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)
and
U(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}}
are bounded. The action of
U(\mathscr{L})(X)^{\operatorname{op}}
is bounded by
[
9
, Proposition 6.10]
.

Let now
S\subset\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)
,
V\subset\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}
and
W\subset\mathcal{M}
be bounded
\mathcal{R}
-submodules. By definition, the bornology on
\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\otimes_{A}\mathcal{M}
is the quotient bornology with respect to the map:

q:\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\otimes_{K}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\otimes_{A}\mathcal{M}.

Hence, it is generated by bounded subsets of the form
q(V\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}W)
. Every element
x\in\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)
acts on
\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\otimes_{A}\mathcal{M}
by
\operatorname{Id}\otimes x
. Thus, we have:

\bigcup_{x\in S}x(q(V\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}W))\subset q(V\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\bigcup_{x\in S}x(W)).

As the action of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)
on
\mathcal{M}
is bounded,

\cup_{x\in S}x(W)
is bounded. Thus, the action of
\wideparen{U}(\mathscr{L})(U)
on
\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\otimes_{A}\mathcal{M}
is bounded. By taking completions, we get our desired functor. The second functor is constructed analogously. In order to see that they are mutually inverse, we can use Proposition
6.1.2
,
to obtain natural isomorphisms:

\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}^{-1}\otimes_{A}\left(\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\otimes_{A}\mathcal{M}\right),\textnormal{ and }\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\otimes_{A}\left(\Omega_{\mathscr{L}(X)}^{-1}\otimes_{A}\mathcal{N}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{N},

which are bounded by
[
9
, Theorem 6.11]
