we have:

\operatorname{H}^{i}\left(\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}(C^{\bullet})\right)=\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\left(\operatorname{H}^{i}(C^{\bullet})\right).

In particular, the cohomology groups of
\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}(C^{\bullet})
are co-admissible modules supported in
\Delta(X)
. Choose a Fréchet-Stein presentation
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\varprojlim_{n}\widehat{D}_{n}
such that we have a Fréchet-Stein presentation
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}=\varprojlim_{n}\widehat{D}_{n}^{e}
. It suffices to show that for all
n\geq 0
we have:

\widehat{D}_{n}^{e}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}\Delta_{*}^{E}\operatorname{H}^{i}(C^{\bullet})(X^{2})=\widehat{D}_{n}^{e}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}\operatorname{H}^{i}(C^{\bullet})(X)=0,

for all but finitely many
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Notice that we have:

\widehat{D}_{n}^{e}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{e}}\operatorname{H}^{i}(C^{\bullet})(X)=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\operatorname{H}^{i}(C^{\bullet})(X)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{n}.

As
C^{\bullet}
is a diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complex, it is in particular a
\mathcal{C}
-complex over
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. Hence, the right hand side of the above equation is zero for all but finitely many
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

For the last part, we first notice that there is a canonical morphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach algebras
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}\rightarrow\Delta^{-1}\wideparen{E}_{X}
, so that the functor is well-defined. Additionally, for any
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{Bi}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
there is a canonical
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
-linear map:

\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\Delta^{-1}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}=\Delta^{-1}\wideparen{E}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}.

We need to show that this is a quasi-isomorphism. However, as
\Delta^{-1}
is an exact functor, if suffices to show that for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
the following map is an isomorphism:

\operatorname{H}^{i}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\Delta^{-1}\operatorname{H}^{i}(\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}))=\Delta^{-1}\operatorname{H}^{i}(\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}))=\Delta^{-1}\mathbb{L}\Delta^{E}_{*}(\operatorname{H}^{i}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})),

where the last identity follows because
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
is a diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complex, so its cohomology sheaves are co-admissible diagonal
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-bimodules. Thus, we are reduced to showing
\mathcal{M}=\Delta^{-1}\Delta_{*}^{E}\mathcal{M}
for
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
. This follows from Theorem
7.3.6
.
∎

Corollary 7.4.6
.

The derived immersion functor induces a triangulated functor:

\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}:\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{Bi}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}\rightarrow\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{2}).

Corollary 7.4.7
.

There is a natural transformation of functors:

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},-)\xrightarrow{\cong}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}}^{\mathbb{L}}-.

Proof.

Follows from Proposition
5.5.4
and the second part of Proposition
7.4.5
.
∎

7.5.
D-cap as a co-admissible diagonal bimodule

In this section we will show that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is an object of
\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
, and determine its image under the immersion functor. This will be instrumental for our calculations of Hochschild (co)-homology of diagonal
C
-complexes in the final chapter.

Proposition 7.5.1
.

Let
X
be smooth and separated. Then
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\in\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
.

Proof.

As this is a local property, we may assume that
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
is affinoid and there is an étale map
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{K}
. In this case, we have that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
is the completion of
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
with respect to the induced bornology. Furthermore,
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
has a canonical filtration
\Phi_{\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
known as the PBW filtration, or filtration by order of differential operators. The ideal
\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}
is given by elements of the form
c_{f}=f\otimes 1-1\otimes f
, for
f\in A
. In particular, for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
, we have
c_{f}\alpha=[f,\alpha]
. By definition of the order of a differential operator, it follows that
\alpha\in\Phi_{n}\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
if and only if
c_{f}^{n}\alpha=0
for all
f\in A
. As the PBW filtration is exhaustive, it follows that
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)\subset\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{\Delta})
. However, as
\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)
is dense in
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, it follows that

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{\Delta})
. The fact that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is co-admissible as a left and right module over itself is clear.
∎

Thus,
\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
-module supported on the diagonal.

Proposition 7.5.2
.

There is an isomorphism of co-admissible
\wideparen{D}_{X^{2}}
-modules:

\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{X}.

Proof.

By Kashiwara’s equivalence, it is enough to show that:

\Delta^{!}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\mathcal{O}_{X}.

We can assume that
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
is affinoid, and that there is an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{K}
, induced by local coordinates
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in A
. These local coordinates induce a basis
\partial_{x_{1}},\cdots,\partial_{x_{n}}
of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}
. This basis satisfies the following properties:

\mathcal{T}_{X/K}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}\partial_{x_{i}},\textnormal{ }[\partial_{x_{i}},\partial_{x_{j}}]=0\textnormal{, }\partial_{x_{i}}(x_{j})=\delta_{i,j}\textnormal{, for all }1\leq i,j\leq n.

Notice that, under these conditions, we have the following identity in
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
for each
j\geq 0
, and each
1\leq i\leq n
:

x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}^{j+1}-\partial_{x_{i}}^{j+1}x_{i}=(j+1)\partial_{x_{i}}^{j}.

Next, notice that, as co-admissible modules are determined by their global sections, we need to show that the following identity of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-modules holds:

\left(\Omega_{X}^{-1}(X)\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\right)[\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}(X^{2})]=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X).

Our choice of an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{K}
induces a trivialization of
\Omega_{X}
as a line bundle. In particular, we can freely take
\Omega_{X}^{-1}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
, and every
a\otimes b\in A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}A
acts on each
\alpha\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
by the formula
(a\otimes b)\alpha=a\alpha b
.

Recall from Lemma
7.2.2
the ideals
\mathcal{J}_{r}
in
A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}A
generated by
(1\otimes x_{j}-x_{j}\otimes 1)_{j=1}^{r}
, and let
X_{r}=\operatorname{Sp}(A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}A/\mathcal{J}_{r})
. We have an ascending chain of ideals:

\mathcal{J}_{1}\subset\mathcal{J}_{2}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{J}_{n}\subset\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}.

Hence, we get an associated family of closed immersions:

X\xrightarrow{i_{n+1}}X_{n}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow X_{1}\xrightarrow{i_{1}}X^{2},

such that the composition of all these maps is
\Delta:X\rightarrow X^{2}
.

By
[
9
, Lemma 9.3]
,
i_{1}^{!}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is the co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X_{1}}
-module associated to the kernel of the following short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)[\mathcal{J}_{1}]\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\xrightarrow{1\otimes x_{1}-x_{1}\otimes 1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\rightarrow 0.

Let
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\prime}
be the subalgebra of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
which is topologically generated by
A
, and
\partial_{x_{2}},\cdots,\partial_{x_{n}}
.
Notice that
x_{1}
commutes with every element in
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\prime}
. Hence, we have
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\prime}\subset\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)[\mathcal{J}_{1}]
. By construction of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
, we may express every element
f\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
as the power series:

f=\sum_{i\geq 0}P_{i}\partial_{x_{1}}^{i},\textnormal{ where }P_{i}\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\prime}.

Assume that
f\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)[\mathcal{J}_{1}]
. Then we have:

0=x_{1}f-fx_{1}=\sum_{i\geq 0}P_{i}(x_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}^{i}-\partial_{x_{1}}^{i}x_{1})=\sum_{i\geq 1}iP_{i}\partial_{x_{1}}^{i-1}.

Hence,
P_{i}=0
for
i\geq 1
, so
f\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\prime}
. It follows that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)[\mathcal{J}_{1}]=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\prime}
.

We may inductively repeat the argument above, and it follows that we have:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)[\mathcal{J}_{n}]=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X),

and the result follows from the fact that the action of
\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}(X)
on
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
is trivial.
∎

Corollary 7.5.3
.

There is an isomorphism of co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
-modules:

\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}_{r}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Delta^{r}_{+}\Omega_{X}.

Proof.

We have the following identities:

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}_{r}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{S}^{-1}_{r}(\operatorname{T}(\Delta_{*}^{E}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))=

\displaystyle\Omega_{X^{2}/K}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}}\operatorname{S}^{-1}(\Delta_{*}^{E}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Omega_{X^{2}/K}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}}\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}^{r}\Omega_{X},

where the first identity is Corollary
6.1.11
, the second is Proposition
6.1.10
, the third is Proposition
7.5.2
, and the last one follows by
[
9
, pp. 100]
.
∎

We can use a similar approach to produce multiple examples of objects in
\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
. However, as this is not directly used in the body of the text, we will postpone it to Appendix
A.1
.

8.
Hochschild (Co)-homology

Let
X
be a separated smooth rigid space. In this final chapter, we will apply the machinery developed thus far to obtain explicit calculations of the Hochschild cohomology and homology of diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complexes on
X
. In particular, we obtain a canonical quasi-isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet},

which allows us to relate the Hochschild cohomology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
with the de Rham cohomology of
X
. Similarly, the Hochschild homology of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
can be calculated via the formula:

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Omega_{X/K}^{\bullet}[2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

Hence, we obtain an upper bound on the degrees on which de Rham cohomology is non-zero, and obtain a duality between Hochschild cohomology and homology.

8.1.
Hochschild Cohomology

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid analytic variety. In this section, we will study the inner Hochschild cohomology sheaves of diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complexes on
X
. In order to do this, we will first show a version of Kashiwara’s equivalence for sheaves of inner homomorphisms:

Proposition 8.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}}^{-}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
, and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})
. There is a canonical isomorphism of complexes in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{2}}(\Delta_{+}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=\Delta_{*}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\Delta^{!}\mathcal{N}).

Proof.

This proof follows
[
22
, Proposition 1.5.25]
. We will start by showing that the transfer bimodule
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow X}
is a
(\Delta^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
-bimodule satisfying the conditions of Proposition

3.4.3
. As this may be done locally, we may assume that
X
is affinoid and admits an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{K}
.

Let
Y\rightarrow X^{2}
be the smooth rigid space from Lemma
7.2.2
. By the discussion after Definition
A.1.2
, we know that we have an isomorphism of right

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}
-modules:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}\cong K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}.

As the canonical closed immersion
X\rightarrow Y
identifies
X
with a connected component of
Y
, it follows that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow X}\cong K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
. Hence, for any
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
we have:

\displaystyle\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{M}=

\displaystyle I\left(K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\right)\widetilde{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})}I(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\})\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}I(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\widetilde{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{M}),

as we wanted to show. We then have the following chain of identities:

\displaystyle R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}(\Delta_{+}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=

\displaystyle R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}(\Delta_{*}\left(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{M}\right),\Delta_{*}\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\Delta^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{M},\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\Delta^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow X},\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{N}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\Delta^{!}\mathcal{N})),

where the first identity follows by Lemma
3.5.3
, the second by Proposition
3.5.5
, the third is Proposition
3.4.3
, and the last one is
[
9
, Lemma 7.4]
.
∎

Corollary 8.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
be a diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complex on
X
. Then we have:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\Delta^{!}\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}).

Proof.

This is a consequence of Corollary
6.1.18
, Corollary
7.4.6
, Proposition
7.5.2
, and Proposition
8.1.1
.
∎

Recall from
[
9
, Section 6.3]
that for any smooth rigid analytic variety
X
we have a canonical resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
by locally free
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules. Namely, we have the Spencer resolution:

(50)

0\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wedge^{n}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}.

For
1\leq i\leq n
, the chain maps are the completions of the following
\mathcal{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-linear maps:

\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\mathcal{D}_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wedge^{i}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wedge^{i-1}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}\\
P\otimes(\wedge\partial_{j})\mapsto\sum_{j}(-1)^{j+1}P\partial_{j}\otimes\partial_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{\partial_{j}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\partial_{i}\\
+\sum_{j<j^{\prime}}(-1)^{j+j^{\prime}}P[\partial_{j},\partial_{j^{\prime}}]\otimes\partial_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{\partial_{j}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{\partial_{j}^{\prime}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\partial_{i}

We will denote this resolution by:

S^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wedge^{n}\mathcal{T}_{X/K}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow 0\right).

Applying side-changing, we get a resolution of
\Omega_{X}
by finite locally-free
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}}
-modules:

(51)

\overline{S}^{\bullet}:=\left(0\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\Omega_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\rightarrow 0\right).

We may use this resolution together with Corollary
8.1.2
to give a precise description of the inner Hochschild complex:

Definition 8.1.3
.

For any
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
, the de Rham complex of
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
is defined as the following complex in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\operatorname{dR}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}):=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}).

The idea is relating the Hochschild cohomology complex with the de Rham complex:

Lemma 8.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
. Then we have an identification:

R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\Omega_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}[-n].

Proof.

The proof of
[
22
, Proposition 2.6.14]
works in this setting.
∎

Remark 8.1.5
.

Notice that we have:

\operatorname{dR}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=R\underline{\mathcal{H}om}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})=\Omega_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X}[-n]=\overline{S}^{\bullet}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X}[-n]=\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}.

Hence, we recover
\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}
as the de Rham complex of the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

We may condense our results into the following theorem:

Theorem 8.1.6
.

Let
X
be a separated and smooth rigid space. For any diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complex

\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{Bi}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
we have the following identifications:

\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{dR}\left(\Delta^{!}\mathbb{L}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\right)=

\displaystyle\operatorname{dR}\left(\Omega_{X}^{-1}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}[\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Omega_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\left(\Omega_{X}^{-1}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}[\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}]\right)[-\operatorname{dim}(X)].

In particular, we have:

(i)

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}
as objects in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
.

(ii)

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X}^{\bullet})
as objects in
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
.

And there is a Hodge-de Rham spectral sequence in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
:

E^{p,q}_{1}:=\operatorname{H}^{q}(X,\Omega_{X}^{p})\Rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{p+q}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}).

Proof.

The first part was shown in Corollary
8.1.2
. For the second part, we have:

\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{dR}\left(\Delta^{!}\Delta_{*}^{\operatorname{S}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\right)=\operatorname{dR}\left(\mathcal{O}_{X}\right)=\Omega_{X}^{\bullet},

where the second identity is Proposition
7.5.2
, and the third one is Remark
8.1.5
.
∎

Notice that the Hochschild cohomology spaces:

\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}):=\operatorname{H}^{n}(R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X}^{\bullet})),

do not agree with the usual de Rham cohomology groups. Indeed, by forgetting the bornological structure, one can regard
\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}
as an object of
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Vect}_{K}))
. Then we have:

\operatorname{H}^{n}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X):=\mathbb{H}^{n}(X,\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}),

In particular,
\operatorname{H}^{n}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)
is a
K
-vector space. On the other hand, our definition of Hochschild cohomology spaces takes into account the canonical bornological structure of

\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}
. In particular, as the complex
\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}
is not necessarily strict, the spaces
\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
are elements in
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
, which need not be complete bornological spaces. However, we can use the material of
[
37
]
to recover the de Rham cohomology groups from the Hochschild cohomology groups.

Indeed, recall that we have an adjunction:

J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{B}c_{K}:\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}},

where
J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}c_{K}
denotes the inclusion, and
\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}}:\mathcal{B}c_{K}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
denotes the completion of bornological spaces. This adjunction satisfies the following properties:

Proposition 8.1.7

(
[
37
, Proposition 5.14]
)
.

The functor
J:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}c_{K}
is exact, and gives rise to a functor:

J:\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{B}c_{K}).

The functor
\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}}:\mathcal{B}c_{K}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
is cokernel preserving, and has a left derived functor:

L\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}}:\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{B}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}).

These derived functors fit into a derived adjunction:

J:\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\leftrightarrows\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{B}c_{K}):L\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}}.

Furthermore, we have an identification of derived functors:

L\widehat{\operatorname{Cpl}}\circ J=\operatorname{Id}.

In particular,
\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is equivalent to a full triangulated subcategory of
\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{B}c_{K})
, and
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
is equivalent to a reflexive abelian subcategory of
LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})
.

We will denote the restriction of
J:\operatorname{D}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{B}c_{K})
to
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
by:

J:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K}),\quad\mathcal{V}\mapsto J(\mathcal{V})=\operatorname{H}^{0}(J(\mathcal{V})).

By the previous proposition, this is an exact functor. Furthermore, its extension to the derived categories is given by
J
(
cf
.
[
43
, Proposition 1.3.10]
).

On the other hand, we have the forgetful functor:

\operatorname{Forget}(-):\mathcal{B}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

By construction of the quasi-abelian structure on bornological spaces, this functor is strongly exact. In particular, it preserves arbitrary kernels and cokernels. As
\operatorname{Vect}_{K}
is an abelian category, it is canonically isomorphic to its left heart. Thus, we get an exact functor:

\widetilde{\operatorname{Forget}}(-):LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

Both of these functors extend to functors between the corresponding derived categories, and these extensions get identified via the isomorphism:

\operatorname{D}(\mathcal{B}c_{K})=\operatorname{D}(LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})).

Thus, in order to simplify notation, we will denote
\widetilde{\operatorname{Forget}}(-)
simply by
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
.

In order to apply these functors to our setting, we need to extend them to the corresponding categories of sheaves. However, as
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
are not elementary quasi-abelian categories, their associated categories of sheaves are not well-behaved (
i.e.
no sheafification functor). However, as
\mathcal{B}c_{K}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}
are quasi-elementary, their left hearts are Grothendieck abelian categories. Hence, their categories of sheaves are also Grothendieck, and thus are suited for homological algebra.

Definition 8.1.8
.

We define the following functor:

J:\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})),\quad\mathcal{F}\mapsto J(\mathcal{F}),

where
J(\mathcal{F})
satisfies
J(\mathcal{F})(U)=J(\mathcal{F}(U))
for every open
U\subset X
. Analogously:

\operatorname{Forget}(-):\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Vect}_{K}),\quad\mathcal{G}\mapsto\operatorname{Forget}(\mathcal{G}),

where
\operatorname{Forget}(\mathcal{G})
satisfies
\operatorname{Forget}(\mathcal{G})(U)=\operatorname{Forget}(\mathcal{G}(U))
for every open
U\subset X
.

Notice that as both of the following functors are exact:

J:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K}),\textnormal{ }\operatorname{Forget}(-):LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}.

Hence, the definitions above make sense. Furthermore, we have the following:

Lemma 8.1.9
.

The functor
J:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})
commutes with filtered colimits.

Proof.

We start by giving a slightly different interpretation of the functor
J:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})
. Indeed, let
\operatorname{Sns}_{K}
be the category of semi-normed spaces over
K
, and let
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})
be its associated Ind-category. Then by
[
37
, Proposition 3.10]
,
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})
is an elementary quasi-abelian category. Furthermore, there is a canonical identification:

LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})=LH(\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})).

In virtue of
[
37
, Section 5]
, we can use the fact that Banach space is a semi normed space, to obtain a canonical inclusion:

F:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K}).

Using the identification
LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}))=LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})
, we arrive at the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

where all functors except for maybe
J
commute with filtered colimits. Let
\mathcal{I}
be a small filtered category. We let
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}

be the category of functors
\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
. As
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
is an elementary quasi-abelian category, it follows by
[
43
, Proposition 1.4.15]

that
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}
is a quasi-abelian category, and that there is a canonical isomorphism:

(52)

LH(\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})^{\mathcal{I}})=LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}.

Similarly,
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}
, is also a quasi-abelian category, and there is a canonical equivalence:

(53)

LH(\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})^{\mathcal{I}})=LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}.

As both
\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
, and
\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})
are co-complete, we have the following additive functors:

\varinjlim_{\mathcal{I}}:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K}),\quad\varinjlim_{\mathcal{I}}:\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K}),

where the first one is given by sending a functor
\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})
to its colimit, and the second one is defined analogously. Notice that
F:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})
commutes with filtered colimits if and only if the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{\mathcal{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{\mathcal{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

Passing onto the left hearts, and using the identities (
52
), (
53
), and the fact that:

I:\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})\rightarrow LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}),\quad I:\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{Sns}_{K})\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K}),

commute with filtered colimits, we arrive at the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})^{\mathcal{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{\mathcal{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{\mathcal{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}

We are done if we show that this diagram commutes. However,
LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})

is a Grothendieck abelian category. In particular, filtered colimits are exact. Hence, the vertical maps in the diagram are exact. As mentioned above,
J:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})
is also exact. Hence,
J_{*}
is exact as well. Thus, it follows by
[
43
, Proposition 1.2.34]
that the diagram is commutative, as we wanted to show.
∎

Lemma 8.1.10
.

The following hold:

(i)

The functor
J:\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K}))
is exact.

(ii)

The functor
\operatorname{Forget}(-):\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K}))\rightarrow\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
is exact.

Proof.

As kernels of morphisms of sheaves can be calculated in the category of presheaves, it follows from left exactness of
J:LH(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}c_{K})\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})
, and
\operatorname{Forget}(-):LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}

that both functors are left exact. In order to show that they are also right exact, we need to show that they commute with sheafification. As the functors are left exact, it suffices to show that they commute with filtered colimits. The first functor was dealt with in Lemma
8.1.9
.

For the second functor, notice that
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
can be expressed as the following composition:

LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})\xrightarrow{C}\mathcal{B}c_{K}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Forget}(-)}\operatorname{Vect}_{K},

where
C:LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}c_{K}
is right adjoint to
I:\mathcal{B}c_{K}\rightarrow LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})
. As
C
is a right adjoint, it commutes with filtered colimits. Furthermore, the definition of colimit
of bornological spaces shows that
\operatorname{Forget}(-):\mathcal{B}c_{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}
also commutes with filtered colimits. Thus, the functor
\operatorname{Forget}(-):LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{K}
commutes with filtered colimits, as we wanted to show.
∎

We can use these lemmas to show the following proposition:

Proposition 8.1.11
.

We have the following identities of derived functors:

(i)

\operatorname{Forget}(R\Gamma(X,-))=R\Gamma(X,\operatorname{Forget}(-)).

(ii)

R\Gamma(X,J(-))=JR\Gamma(X,-).

Proof.

For the first identity, notice that as
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
is exact, it suffices to show that it sends injective objects to
\Gamma(X,-)
-acyclic objects. In particular, it suffices to show that the image under

\operatorname{Forget}(-)
of any injective object in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K}))
is Čech-acyclic. However, this follows by the fact that
\operatorname{Forget}(-)
is exact, and every injective object in
\operatorname{Shv}(X,LH(\mathcal{B}c_{K}))
is Čech-acyclic. The second identity is completely analogous.
∎

As a consequence, we obtain an interpretation of de Rham cohomology of
X
in terms of the Hochschild cohomology groups of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
:

Corollary 8.1.12
.

We have the following identity in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Vect}_{K})
:

\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X)=\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})).

Proof.

This follows by Theorem
8.1.6
, and Proposition
8.1.11
, as we have:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Forget}(J\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}))=\operatorname{Forget}(JR\Gamma(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/K}))=

\displaystyle R\Gamma(X,\operatorname{Forget}(J\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/K}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\bullet}(X).

∎

Remark 8.1.13
.

Notice that we could use the previous proposition to endow the de Rham cohomology groups
\operatorname{H}_{dR}^{n}(X)
with a bornology. However, this bornology is usually not interesting, and presents pathological behavior. However, in
[
34
]
we will study a class of spaces for which this induced bornology makes the de Rham cohomology groups into nuclear Fréchet spaces.

8.2.
Hochschild Homology

Let
X
be a separated smooth rigid analytic variety. In this section, we will use the machinery developed thus far to obtain an explicit description of the inner Hochschild homology complex of diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complexes on
X
. We start with the following proposition:

Proposition 8.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
, and
\mathcal{N}\in\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}})
. There is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X,\operatorname{Ind(Ban}_{K})))
:

\Delta^{r}_{+}\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}\mathcal{N}=\Delta_{*}\left(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\Delta^{!}\mathcal{N}\right)[\operatorname{dim}(X)].

Proof.

We have the following identities:

\Delta^{-1}\left(\Delta^{r}_{+}\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}\mathcal{N}\right)=\Delta^{-1}\left(\Delta_{*}\left(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\rightarrow X^{2}}\right)\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}\mathcal{N}\right)\\
=\left(\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\rightarrow X^{2}}\right)\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\Delta^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{N},

where the second identity follows by Proposition
3.5.7
. Hence, we have:

\Delta^{-1}\left(\Delta^{r}_{+}\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}\mathcal{N}\right)=\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\left(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X\rightarrow X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\Delta^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{N}\right)\\
=\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\Delta^{!}\mathcal{N}[\operatorname{dim}(X)].

As
\Delta^{r}_{+}\mathcal{M}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}}\mathcal{N}
has support contained in the diagonal, we are done.
∎

This allows us to calculate the Hochschild homology of diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complexes:

Theorem 8.2.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth and separated rigid space. For any diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complex

\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{Bi}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
we have:

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}=\Omega_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\left(\Omega_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}[\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}]\right)[\operatorname{dim}(X)].

In particular the following identities hold:

(i)

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\mathcal{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})[2\operatorname{dim}(X)]
.

(ii)

\operatorname{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})[2\operatorname{dim}(X)]
.

(iii)

\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\operatorname{HH}^{2\operatorname{dim}(X)-n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Furthermore, for
\mathcal{M}=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
, we have:

\mathcal{HH}_{\bullet}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X},\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\Omega_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X}[\operatorname{dim}(X)]=\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}[2\operatorname{dim}(X)].

Proof.

The first identity is Corollary
7.4.7
, the second one is a consequence of Corollaries
6.1.18
,
7.5.3
, and Proposition
8.2.1
. The rest follows by the description of the inner Hochschild cohomology complex from Theorem
8.1.6
.
∎

Thus, we obtain a duality between Hochschild cohomology and homology for
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules on separated smooth rigid analytic spaces. This can be regarded as a
p
-adic version of the classical duality between Hochschild cohomology and homology of
\mathcal{D}
-modules on smooth algebraic varieties and smooth complex analytic spaces.

Furthermore, for each
n\geq 0
we have the following identity:

\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}^{2\operatorname{dim}(X)-n}\left(R\Gamma(X,\Omega_{X}^{\bullet})\right).

This was obtained for affine algebraic varieties over a characteristic zero field, Stein complex manifolds, and compact
C^{\infty}
-manifolds by Wodzicki in
[
49
, Theorem 2]
. The result for affine algebraic varieties was also shown by Kassel-Mitschi (see
[
11
, pp. 19]
), and for smooth real manifolds by Brylinski-Getzler in
[
11
, pp. 18]
. As a consequence of Theorem
8.2.2
, we recover the following classical result:

Corollary 8.2.3
.

Let
X
be a separated and smooth rigid analytic space. We have:

\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=0\textnormal{ for all }n>2\operatorname{dim}(X).

In particular,
\operatorname{H}^{n}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)=0
for all
n>2\operatorname{dim}(X)
.

Proof.

Choose some
n>2\operatorname{dim}(X)
. By Theorem
8.2.2
we have:

\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{HH}_{n-2\operatorname{dim}(X)}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=\operatorname{H}^{n-2\operatorname{dim}(X)}\left(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\right).

As
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is trivial on positive degrees, this shows
\operatorname{HH}^{n}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})=0
.
∎

Appendix A

A.1.
Examples of co-admissible diagonal bimodules

The goal of this appendix is producing some examples of objects in
\mathcal{C}_{B}^{\operatorname{Bi}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta}
. We start by recalling the following well-known theorem:

Theorem A.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module which is also a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module,
then
\mathcal{V}
is a vector bundle.

Proof.

It suffices to show the case where
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
is affinoid, with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
. As
\mathcal{V}
is a coherent module, for
x\in X
we have
\mathcal{V}_{x}=\mathcal{V}(X)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
. Then, the proof of
[
22
, 1.4.10]
shows that
\mathcal{V}_{x}
is a finite-free module over
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
. Thus,
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{x}=\mathcal{V}(X)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
is a finite-free module over the maximal adic completion
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}_{x}\subset A
be the maximal ideal associated to the point
x\in X
. Then, by
[
10
, 4.1.2]
, we have that
\widehat{A}_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}=\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
. As
A
is noetherian, the map
A_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\rightarrow\widehat{A}_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
is faithfully flat. In particular,
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{x}
is finite-free if and only if
\mathcal{V}(X)\otimes_{A}A_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
is finite-free. Applying the discussion above, it follows that
\mathcal{V}(X)\otimes_{A}A_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
is finite free for every maximal ideal of
A
. Thus,
\mathcal{V}(X)
is finite and locally free. Equivalently,
\mathcal{V}
is a vector bundle.
∎

Definition A.1.2
.

We define
\operatorname{Conn}(X)
as the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})
given by the
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-coherent modules. We call elements
\mathcal{V}\in\operatorname{Conn}(X)
integrable connections.

By
[
4
, Theorem 7.3]
there is an equivalence of categories between the categories of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-coherent co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules, and the category of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules. In particular, and integrable connection is equivalent to a vector bundle
\mathcal{V}
, equipped with a bounded
K
-linear map:

\nabla:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{1}_{X/K},

called an integrable (flat) connection on
\mathcal{V}
. This map needs to satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

\nabla(fv)=v\otimes d(f)+f\nabla(v)
, for an admissible open
U\subset X
, and sections
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(U),v\in\mathcal{V}(U)
.

(ii)

The bounded morphism:
\nabla^{2}:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{2}_{X/K}
, defined by:

\nabla^{2}:=(\nabla\otimes 1)\circ\nabla+\nabla\otimes d,

satisfies
\nabla^{2}=0
. This is called the integrability condition.

Notice that Theorem
A.1.1
implies that
\operatorname{Conn}(X)
is an abelian category. We will now show that every integrable connection on
X
induces a co-admissible diagonal bimodule in a natural way. In order to do so, we will need an explicit description of the pushforward along
\Delta:X\rightarrow X^{2}
in the local case. We borrow this description from
[
22
, Example 1.3.5]
.

Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
be an affinoid variety with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{K}
induced by sections
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}
. These sections yield a basis of
\Omega_{X}^{1}(X)
, and we let

\partial_{1},\cdots,\partial_{n}\subset\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
be its dual basis. Define the following vector fields in
\mathcal{T}_{X^{2}/K}(X^{2})
:

\alpha_{i}=\partial_{i}\otimes 1-1\otimes\partial_{i},\textnormal{ }\beta_{i}=\partial_{i}\otimes 1+1\otimes\partial_{i},\textnormal{ for }1\leq i\leq n.

Then
\mathcal{B}:=\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n}\}
is a
\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})
-basis of
\mathcal{T}_{X^{2}/K}(X^{2})
.
Furthermore, this is the dual basis of the differentials of
the following family of rigid functions:

\{f_{1}\otimes 1-1\otimes f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\otimes 1-1\otimes f_{n},f_{1}\otimes 1+1\otimes f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\otimes 1+1\otimes f_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}(X^{2}).

Recall the smooth rigid variety
Y
from Lemma
7.2.2
, and consider the canonical closed immersion
i:Y\rightarrow X^{2}
. By definition,
Y
is the zero locus of the sections
f_{1}\otimes 1-1\otimes f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\otimes 1-1\otimes f_{n}
. Hence, as shown in
[
9
, Lemma 7.9]
we have an isomorphism of right
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}
-modules:

(54)

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}\cong K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}.

We can use this isomorphism to give an explicit description of the left action of
i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
on
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}
. Indeed, we have a decomposition of sheaves of Ind-Banach
K
-vector spaces:

(55)

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}\cong K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}.

Regard
K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}
as a sheaf of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras with respect to the unique structure making the previous identification an algebra isomorphism.

Identify
K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}
with the sheaf
defined on any affinoid subdomain
U\subset X^{2}
by:

K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}(U)=K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}1\subset\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(U).

By construction of
\mathcal{B}
, we have
[\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}]=0
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
. Thus,
K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}
, is a sheaf of subalgebras of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
. Furthermore,

K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}
is a sheaf of commutative Ind-Banach
K
-algebras. Analogously, we may regard
K\{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n}\}
as a sheaf of commutative subalgebras of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
.

Define
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}:=K\{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}
. Notice that this is a subsheaf of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
which contains the unit of the sheaf of algebras
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
. Furthermore,
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
is closed under the product in
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
. Hence,
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
is canonically a sheaf of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras, and the inclusion
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
is a morphism of sheaves of Ind-Banach
K
-algebras.

We may rewrite
(\ref{equation decomposition of DX2})
as

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}\cong K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
, and this is an identity of right
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
-modules. Therefore, we have an isomorphism of right
i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
-modules:

(56)

i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}=i^{-1}\left(K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}\right)\\
=K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}.

Hence, in order to define the action of
i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
on
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}
, it suffices to give actions of

K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}
and
i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
. The action of
K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}
on
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}
is given by left multiplication using
(\ref{equation decomposition transfer bimodule})
. For the action of
i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
, notice that we have:

i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{i^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}}\mathcal{O}_{Y}=K\{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\cong\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}.

Hence, we have a canonical action of
i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
on
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}
given by left multiplication. The action of
i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
on
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}
is defined via the following composition:

i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}\cong i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}\rightarrow i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}\\
\cong K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}\rightarrow K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y},

where the first map is
(\ref{equation decomposition transfer bimodule})
, the second map is induced by the multiplication in
i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
, the third is
(\ref{equation decomposition pullback od differentials on product})
, and the last is induced by the action of
i^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
on
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}
.

By Proposition
5.3.7
, we have a decomposition
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}=p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
,
as sheaves of Ind-Banach
K
-vector spaces. Under the isomorphism
(\ref{equation decomposition of DX2})
, we get isomorphisms:

(57)

\displaystyle p_{1}^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\cong K\{\partial_{1}\otimes 1,\cdots,\partial_{n}\otimes 1\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X},

(58)

p_{2}^{-1}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}\cong K\{1\otimes\partial_{1},\cdots,1\otimes\partial_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}p_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}.

As
X
is affinoid we have
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})\cong K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{O}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})
. Similarly, we get two different identifications of subalgebras of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})
:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\cong\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{1}:=K\{\partial_{1}\otimes 1,\cdots,\partial_{n}\otimes 1\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}(A\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}1),\\
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\cong\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{2}:=K\{1\otimes\partial_{1},\cdots,1\otimes\partial_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}(1\otimes A),

We will now describe the actions of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
on
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}(X)
. Notice that for any
1\leq i\leq n
we have the following identities in
\mathcal{T}_{X^{2}/K}(X^{2})
:

\partial_{i}\otimes 1=\frac{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}{2},\textnormal{ }1\otimes\partial_{i}=\frac{\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i}}{2}.

Hence, for
f\in K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}
, and
d\in\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}(Y)

we have:

(59)

\partial_{i}\otimes 1\left(f\otimes d\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{i}f\otimes d+f\otimes\beta_{i}d\right),\\
1\otimes\partial_{i}\left(f\otimes d\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(f\otimes\beta_{i}d-\alpha_{i}f\otimes d\right),

where we have used the fact that
K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n}\}
is a commutative subalgebra of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})
.

Lemma A.1.3
.

Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
be a smooth affinoid space with an étale map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{K}
, and let
\mathcal{V}
be an integrable connection on
X
. We have the following identification:

\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V}(X^{2})=K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{V}(X).

Proof.

Consider the closed immersion
j:X\rightarrow Y
. Then we have
\Delta=i\circ j
and, by Lemma
7.3.5
, we have

\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V}=i_{+}j_{+}\mathcal{V}=i_{+}j_{*}\mathcal{V}
.
Thus, the discussion above shows that
\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V}
is the co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}
-module with global sections:

\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V}(X^{2})=i_{*}\left(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}}j_{*}\mathcal{V}\right)(X^{2})=K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{V}(X),

as we wanted to show.
∎

Lemma A.1.4
.

Let
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
be a smooth affinoid space as in Lemma
A.1.3
. Assume there is an affine formal model
\mathcal{A}\subset A
such that
\partial_{i}(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathcal{A}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Then
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}\partial_{i}
is a finite-free
\mathcal{A}
-Lie lattice of
\mathcal{T}_{X/K}(X)
, and we have:

(i)

\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{A}\subset A\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}A
is an affine formal model.

(ii)

\mathcal{T}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{A}\alpha_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{A}\beta_{i}
is a finite free
\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{A}
-Lie lattice of
\mathcal{T}_{X^{2}/K}(X^{2})
.

(iii)

\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T})_{K}\otimes_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})}\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V}(X^{2})=K\langle\pi^{m}\alpha_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\alpha_{n}\rangle\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{V}(X)
.

Proof.

We only show statement
(iii)
. As the statement does not need sheaf theory, we will work with the underlying bornological spaces. Let
B=A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}A
. Notice that, by statement
(ii)
, we have a Fréchet-Stein presentation:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})=\wideparen{U}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{2}/K}(X^{2}))=\varprojlim_{m}\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T})_{K}.

Similarly, we have
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)=\varprojlim_{m}\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}})_{K}
. As
\mathcal{V}
is an integrable connection, we may apply
[
4
, Section 7.2]
to show that for every
m\geq 0
we have the following identities of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-modules:

\mathcal{V}(X)=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{V}(X)=\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}})_{K}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}(X)}\mathcal{V}(X).

In particular, we have a presentation of
\mathcal{V}(X)
as a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-module given by the trivial inverse limit
\mathcal{V}(X)=\varprojlim_{m}\mathcal{V}(X)
. The version for left modules of
[
4
, 5.4]
, shows that we have the following identities of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})
-modules:

\displaystyle\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}(Y)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}(Y)}\mathcal{V}(X)=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow Y}(Y)\wideparen{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}(Y)}\mathcal{V}(X)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{m}\left(\left(K\langle\pi^{m}\alpha_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\alpha_{n},\pi^{m}\beta_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\beta_{n}\rangle\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B\right)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{Y}(Y)}\mathcal{V}(X)\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{m}\left(\left(K\langle\pi^{m}\alpha_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\alpha_{n},\pi^{m}\beta_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\beta_{n}\rangle\widehat{\otimes}_{K}B\right)\widehat{\otimes}_{\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}})_{K}}\mathcal{V}(X)\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{m}\left(B\langle\pi^{m}\alpha_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\alpha_{n},\pi^{m}\beta_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\beta_{n}\rangle\widehat{\otimes}_{B\langle\pi^{m}\beta_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\beta_{n}\rangle}\mathcal{V}(X)\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{m}\left(K\langle\pi^{m}\alpha_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\alpha_{n}\rangle\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{V}(X)\right).

Hence, we have the following Fréchet-Stein presentation of
\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V}(X^{2})
:

K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{V}(X)=\varprojlim_{m}\left(K\langle\pi^{m}\alpha_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\alpha_{n}\rangle\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{V}(X)\right),

with respect to
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})=\varprojlim_{m}\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T})_{K}
. In particular, we have:

\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T})_{K}\otimes_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})}\varprojlim_{m}\left(K\langle\pi^{m}\alpha_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\alpha_{n}\rangle\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{V}(X)\right)\\
=K\langle\pi^{m}\alpha_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\alpha_{n}\rangle\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{V}(X),

as we wanted to show.
∎

Proposition A.1.5
.

There is an exact embedding of abelian categories:

\operatorname{Conn}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{Bi}}_{B}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})_{\Delta},

given by the following expression:

(60)

\mathcal{V}\mapsto\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}=\Delta^{-1}\left(\operatorname{S}\left(\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V}\right)\right)=\Omega_{X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\left(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}\leftarrow X}\overrightarrow{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}}\mathcal{V}\right).

In particular, we have
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
, and
\Delta^{!}\Delta^{\operatorname{S}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{V}})=\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an integrable connection on
X
. By construction,
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is an Ind-Banach
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{e}
-module. Furthermore, as
\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V}
has support contained in the diagonal, by Theorem
6.3.6
and Proposition
7.2.5
,
we have
\operatorname{S}\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V}(X)_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{\Delta})=\operatorname{S}\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V}(X)
. Thus, we have:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}(X)_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{\Delta})=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}(X).

Hence, we just need to show that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is co-admissible as a
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
and
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}^{\operatorname{op}}_{X}
module. As this is a purely local property, we may assume that
X=\operatorname{Sp}(A)
is affinoid and satisfies the conditions of Lemma
A.1.4
. We are also free to assume that
\mathcal{V}\cong\bigoplus^{r}_{i=1}\mathcal{O}_{X}
as a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. For simplicity, we will assume
r=1
. The general case is analogous. Also, as we do not need to work with sheaves, we will work with the underlying complete bornological spaces.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
denote
\mathcal{O}_{X}
with the
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module structure induced by the isomorphism
\mathcal{V}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}
, and let
A_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}(X)
.
By Lemma
A.1.3
, we have:

\Delta_{+}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}(X^{2})=K\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}A_{\mathcal{V}}=:\widehat{B}_{\mathcal{V}}.

The element
1\otimes 1\in\widehat{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
induces a canonical
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})
-linear bounded map:

\psi:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})\rightarrow\wideparen{B}_{\mathcal{V}}.

Recall the discussion from the beginning of the appendix, and in particular the definition of the algebras
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
. By restriction, we get two bounded maps:

\varphi_{1}:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\rightarrow\wideparen{B}_{\mathcal{V}},\quad\varphi_{2}:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{2}\rightarrow\wideparen{B}_{\mathcal{V}},

which endow
\wideparen{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
with two different bornological
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)
-module structures. We need to show that
\widehat{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
is co-admissible with respect to both structures. We do this only for the first one, as the second is analogous. By Lemma
A.1.4
, for each
m\geq 0
, the map
\psi:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})\rightarrow\wideparen{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
induces a map:

\psi^{m}:\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T})_{K}\rightarrow K\langle\pi^{m}\alpha_{1},\cdots,\pi^{m}\alpha_{n}\rangle\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\mathcal{V}(X)=:\widehat{B}^{m}_{\mathcal{V}},

where
\wideparen{B}_{\mathcal{V}}=\varprojlim_{m}\widehat{B}^{m}_{\mathcal{V}}
. Similarly, the composition
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\rightarrow\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X^{2}}(X^{2})\rightarrow\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T})_{K}
factors as follows:

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{m}_{1}:=K\langle\pi^{m}\partial_{1}\otimes 1,\cdots,\pi^{m}\partial_{n}\otimes 1\rangle\widehat{\otimes}_{K}(A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}1)\rightarrow\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T})_{K},

such that
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{1}=\varprojlim_{m}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{m}_{1}
is a Fréchet-Stein presentation. In particular, we have a family of maps:

\widehat{\varphi}_{1}^{m}:\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{m}_{1}\rightarrow\widehat{U}(\pi^{m}\mathcal{T})_{K}\rightarrow\widehat{B}^{m}_{\mathcal{V}},

such that
\varphi_{1}=\varprojlim\widehat{\varphi}_{1}^{m}
. It is enough to show that
\widehat{\varphi}_{1}^{m}
is an isomorphism for
m\geq 0
.

It suffices to show this for
m=0
, as the other cases follow from this case multiplying the sections inducing the map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{K}
by an appropriate power of
\pi
. Thus, we will drop the
m
from the notation for the rest of the proof. Consider the following
K
-vector spaces:

\mathcal{D}_{1}\cong(A\widehat{\otimes}_{K}1)\otimes_{K}K[\partial_{1}\otimes 1,\cdots,\partial_{n}\otimes 1],\textnormal{ }B_{\mathcal{V}}:=K[\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}]\otimes_{K}A_{\mathcal{V}}.

Notice that
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a dense subalgebra of
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
, and

B_{\mathcal{V}}
, is a dense subspace of
\widehat{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
. Hence, we regard them as bornological
K
-vector spaces with respect to the subspace bornology.

We will first show that
\widehat{\varphi}_{1}
restricts to an isomorphism:

\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow B_{\mathcal{V}}
. Any polynomial ring
K[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]
has a canonical filtration given by the total degree of the polynomials. Hence, it induces filtrations
F_{\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{1}
and

F_{\bullet}B_{\mathcal{V}}
. We first show that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}
respects those filtrations. Choose some non-negative integer
r\geq 0
. Elements in
F_{r}\mathcal{D}_{1}
are
K
-linear combinations of operators of the form:

\gamma=(1\otimes\partial_{1})^{m_{1}}\cdots(1\otimes\partial_{n})^{m_{n}}f,\textnormal{ where }f\in A,\textnormal{ and }\sum_{i}m_{i}\leq r.

We may assume for simplicity that

\sum_{i}m_{i}=r
. Then we have:

(61)

\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(\gamma)=\frac{1}{2^{r}}(\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1})^{m_{1}}\cdots(\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n})^{m_{n}}\left(1\otimes f\right)=\frac{1}{2^{r}}\alpha_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots\alpha_{n}^{m_{n}}\otimes f+R(\gamma).

Notice that
R(\gamma)
has total degree in the
\alpha_{i}
at most
r-1
. Thus, it is contained in
F_{r-1}B_{\mathcal{V}}
. Hence,
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}
is a map of filtered
K
-vector spaces, and we get a map between the associated graded spaces:

\operatorname{gr}\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}:\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow\operatorname{gr}B_{\mathcal{V}}.

Given an element
x\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
, we denote its image in
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}_{1}
by
[x]
, and we adopt the same convention for
B_{\mathcal{V}}
. As shown in equation
(\ref{equation image of map in proof of integrable connections to diagonal bimodules})
, we have:

\operatorname{gr}\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}([\gamma])=[\alpha_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots\alpha_{n}^{m_{n}}\otimes f].

In particular,
\operatorname{gr}\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}
is an isomorphism of graded
K
-vector spaces, so
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow B_{\mathcal{V}}
is a bounded and bijective map of filtered bornological
K
-vector spaces.

Notice that for each
r\geq 0
,
F_{r}\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
F_{r}B_{\mathcal{V}}

are finite direct sums of Banach spaces. In particular,
F_{r}\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a finite sum of copies of
A
, and
F_{r}B_{\mathcal{V}}
is a finite sum of copies of
A_{\mathcal{V}}
. Hence, the restriction

\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}:F_{r}\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow F_{r}B_{\mathcal{V}}
is a bijective
K
-linear bounded map between Banach spaces. Hence, it is an isomorphism of Banach spaces. We have identities
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\varinjlim_{r}F_{r}\mathcal{D}_{1}
, and
B_{\mathcal{V}}=\varinjlim_{r}F_{r}B_{\mathcal{V}}
of bornological
K
-vector spaces. Therefore,
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow B_{\mathcal{V}}
is an isomorphism of bornological
K
-vector spaces. Thus, it induces an isomorphism between the completions, and we get an isomorphism
\widehat{\varphi}_{1}:\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\rightarrow\widehat{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
. By taking inverse limits, it follows that
\varphi_{1}:\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\rightarrow\wideparen{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
is an isomorphism of complete bornological
K
-vector spaces, thus showing that
\wideparen{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\cong\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
-module. Equivalently,
\wideparen{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)\cong\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
-module. Using the side-changing equivalence
[
4
, Theorem 3.4]
, it follows that
S(\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V})(X^{2})=\Omega_{X}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{A}B_{\mathcal{V}}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)^{\operatorname{op}}
-module.

Furthermore, for every affinoid subdomain
V\subset X
we have:

\displaystyle\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}(V)=\operatorname{S}(\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V})(V\times X)=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(V)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}(X)=

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}(\Delta_{+}\mathcal{V})(X\times V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(X)}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}(V).

Hence,
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-module and a co-admissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\operatorname{op}}
-module.
∎

Corollary A.1.6
.

Let
C^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X})^{-}
be a bounded above
\mathcal{C}
-complex such that:

\operatorname{H}^{i}(C^{\bullet})\in\operatorname{Conn}(X),\textnormal{ for each }i\in\mathbb{Z}.

Then
\Delta^{-1}\left(\operatorname{S}\left(\Delta_{+}C^{\bullet}\right)\right)
is a bounded above diagonal
\mathcal{C}
-complex on
X
.
